# Ethics Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Ethics owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: ETH-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## ETHICS — Part 1: Human Interface, Values, Attitude, Aptitude, Emotional Intelligence and Indian Moral Thinkers

> Section note: (Owners 01–06, basic and advanced). Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner; `(✅ <source>)` = confirmed from a fetched page; `⚠️` = inference, memory-only, or owner-vs-source conflict flagged inside the card. The owners quote the 2nd ARC 4th Report *Ethics in Governance* (2007) for the Human Interface and Values owners; dated 2007 statistics are flagged where they appear.

---

### GROUP — 01 Ethics and Human Interface

### Flashcard ETH-1001 — Ethics and Human Interface: core theme
**Front:** What is the core theme of the Human Interface owner, and what is its answer thesis?
**Definition (learn):** The human interface is the point where a public official's discretion and control over public money meet the citizen's need. Ethical conduct there is produced by values that are durably contained in institutions, not by values alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Causal chain: authority + discretion + control of funds → temptation → corruption risk.
- Structural aggravators: colonial legacy of unchallenged power; asymmetry of power; early-decades over-regulation.
- Correctives: competition and transparency; citizen empowerment; over-centralisation reversed; enforcement of rule of law.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 4th Report *Ethics in Governance* (January 2007), Ch.1 paras 1.1–1.22 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1002 (ethics definition), ETH-1011 (ARC synthesis), ETH-1012 (coercive vs collusive), ETH-1018 (political market), ETH-1024 (CNP).
**Trap:** The owner's thesis is *not* "values fix corruption" or "institutions fix corruption". Answers that pick one side lose the synthesis mark.
**Mains hook:** Ethical conduct at the human interface is produced by values durably contained in institutions that reduce discretion, raise transparency and empower the citizen — provided the political market does not reward illegitimate gain (qualified conclusion).
**Back:** *"Values need containers: cut discretion, add light, empower the citizen, punish the breach."*

### Flashcard ETH-1002 — Ethics: etymology and definition
**Front:** Define "ethics" as the 2nd ARC uses it, with its Greek root.
**Definition (learn):** Ethics is a set of standards that society places on itself to guide behaviour, choices and actions. The standards must be practised, not merely declared.
**Breakdown:**
- Greek *ethikos* = "arising from habit" (so ethics is internalised through practice).
- ARC 1.1 separates "bold words" from "adoption in action".
- Standards alone do not ensure ethical conduct; they need a robust culture of integrity, sanctioned violations and competent disciplinary machinery.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.1 (✅ owner 01 and owner 01 Advanced §2).
**Links:** ETH-1001, ETH-1003 (corruption as failure of ethics), ETH-1005 (ethics vs morality).
**Trap:** "Ethics" is not the same as "laws". Law is one of the sanctioning machineries; the ARC's test is conduct in action.
**Mains hook:** Define ethics as habit-based standards and then show why standards need culture and sanctions (qualified: standards are necessary, not sufficient).
**Back:** *"Ethics = ethikos, habit: practised standards, not declared ones."*

### Flashcard ETH-1003 — Corruption as failure of ethics
**Front:** What is the Latin root of "corruption" and how does the ARC position it relative to ethics?
**Definition (learn):** Corruption is an important manifestation of the failure of ethics.
**Breakdown:**
- Latin *corruptus* = "to break or destroy".
- Ethics = the standard; corruption = the breakdown of that standard in public office.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.2 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1002, ETH-1012 (coercive vs collusive).
**Trap:** Corruption is not defined only as bribery. The owner's definition is wider (breakdown of standards); bribery is one form.
**Mains hook:** Treat corruption as the observable failure of ethical standards and therefore as a symptom to be diagnosed through its structural causes, not only punished.
**Back:** *"Corrupt = corruptus: broken. Corruption is ethics that broke."*

### Flashcard ETH-1004 — Four levels of ethical enquiry (descriptive, normative, meta-ethics, applied)
**Front:** Distinguish descriptive, normative, meta-ethical and applied ethics. Which two dominate GS-IV?
**Definition (learn):**
- **Descriptive ethics:** empirical account of what a group actually believes (example: survey of officials on gift-taking).
- **Normative ethics:** what one ought to do, argued from a theory (duty, consequence, virtue).
- **Meta-ethics:** what moral words mean and whether moral judgments can be true (objectivism vs relativism).
- **Applied ethics:** normative theory worked out for a field (administrative, medical, environmental, AI, business).
**Breakdown:** GS-IV is overwhelmingly normative and applied; meta-ethics gets at most one line in an answer.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Classification as in owner 01 (⚠️ analytical classification; standard philosophical taxonomy, not a UPSC-printed table).
**Links:** ETH-1002, ETH-1003; normative theories in the `08` owner (Deontology, Consequentialism, Virtue Ethics).
**Trap:** Descriptive ≠ normative. "Most officials accept gifts" is a description; "officials should refuse gifts" is a normative claim. Examiners test this shift.
**Mains hook:** Use descriptive evidence (survey, trend) to diagnose, normative theory to justify the standard, applied ethics to design the rule.
**Back:** *"Describe what is, judge what ought, test what 'ought' means, apply it to the desk."*

### Flashcard ETH-1005 — Ethics vs morality
**Front:** How do "ethics" and "morality" differ in the owner's usage?
**Definition (learn):** Ethics = the systematic, reasoned study or standard of right conduct. Morality = the beliefs and practices actually held by a person or society.
**Breakdown:**
- Ethics is reasoned and systematic; morality is practised and held.
- In Mains answers the two are often used interchangeably, but precision is rewarded when the question demands it.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 01 definition (analytical, not a fetched source).
**Links:** ETH-1004, ETH-1006 (legality, morality, propriety).
**Trap:** Saying "ethics and morality are always identical" is the owner's named trap.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish ethics (reasoned standard) from morality (held practice) to show why a society's morality can lag or exceed its formal ethics.
**Back:** *"Ethics is the reasoned rulebook; morality is the practice people actually keep."*

### Flashcard ETH-1006 — Legality, morality and propriety
**Front:** Define the three tests — legality, morality and propriety — and give the owner's example of their gap.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Legality:** conformity to enacted law.
- **Morality:** conformity to a standard of right conduct.
- **Propriety:** conformity to what the office and occasion make appropriate.
**Breakdown:**
- The three come apart. A lavish private dinner from a bidder may break no law and involve no bribe, yet fails propriety in a reasonable citizen's eyes.
- ARC Box 2.3 (US Senate Ethics Manual) states the gap: conduct that "offend[s] the spirit of the law but do[es] not violate its letter".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Box 2.3 (✅ owner 01 quoting ARC; the US Senate Ethics Manual is cited by the ARC, not fetched here).
**Links:** ETH-1005, ETH-1052 (Potter Stewart line, file 02), `16` owner (codes of conduct).
**Trap:** "If it is legal, no ethical question arises" is the explicit trap. ARC's Box 2.3 quotes the warning that "morality has become identical with legality" precisely to reject it.
**Mains hook:** Legal compliance is a floor, not the ethical ceiling; propriety tests appearance and office, which is why codes of conduct regulate gifts and hospitality even when no statute is broken.
**Back:** *"Legal is the floor, moral the standard, proper the look of the office."*

### Flashcard ETH-1007 — Human interface (definition)
**Front:** What is the "human interface" in public administration?
**Definition (learn):** The point of contact between a public official's private interest and public duty or citizen need — the place where ethical behaviour is actually tested.
**Breakdown:**
- Interface = official ↔ citizen, official ↔ contractor, official ↔ political principal.
- Pressure points: permits, transfers, procurement, grievance redress.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 01 working definition (analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1001, ETH-1008 (asymmetry), ETH-1012 (coercive vs collusive).
**Trap:** The human interface is not a moral essay; the owner says always anchor answers in a concrete Indian administrative moment.
**Mains hook:** Diagnose ethics at the interface by naming the discretionary act (a mutation, a bill, a tender) rather than speaking of "character" in the abstract.
**Back:** *"Where the official's private temptation meets the citizen's need."*

### Flashcard ETH-1008 — Asymmetry of power (ARC 1.6)
**Front:** What asymmetry of power does the ARC describe, and what figures does it use?
**Definition (learn):** The structural imbalance between citizen and state, which "reduces societal pressure to conform to ethical behaviour".
**Breakdown:**
- "Nearly 90% of our people are in the unorganized sector" (ARC 1.6, 2007 framing).
- "Nearly 70% of the organized workers with job security and regular monthly wage are employed by the state" directly or through PSUs (2007 framing).
- Effect: citizens lack social leverage to demand ethical conduct.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.6 (✅ owner 01). **Dated 2007 figures**, not current labour statistics (owner Advanced §8). ⚠️ Do not cite as today's data.
**Links:** ETH-1017 (three historical aggravators), ETH-1015 (citizen empowerment as corrective), ETH-1014 (centralisation).
**Trap:** The 90% figure is a 2007 illustrative claim, not a present-day labour-force statistic.
**Mains hook:** Argue the asymmetry structurally, then update with a current Economic Survey / PLFS-type dataset when the question asks for present analysis.
**Back:** *"Ninety unorganised, seventy on state payroll: the 2007 imbalance that dulled pressure."*

### Flashcard ETH-1009 — Two classical explanations: the "decline of values" view
**Front:** Contrast the two classical explanations of ethical failure that ARC 1.4 sets up.
**Definition (learn):**
- **"Decline of values":** corruption stems from erosion of character; nothing improves until values are restored.
- **"Deviant minority":** most people are decent; a small self-interested minority needs punishment-based deterrence.
**Breakdown:**
- Values view → policy implication: moral education; weak on incentive design (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Deviant minority view → policy implication: enforcement; ignores systemic and cultural drivers (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.4 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1011 (ARC synthesis), ETH-1017 (structural aggravators), ETH-1016 (prevention + punishment).
**Trap:** Both views are partial. A "values-only" answer is as weak as an "enforcement-only" answer.
**Mains hook:** Neither explanation alone explains persistence; the synthesis (values inside institutions) is the defensible position.
**Back:** *"Blame the soul or blame the few — the ARC blames the structure that carries both."*

### Flashcard ETH-1010 — The "deviant minority" explanation of ethical failure
**Front:** What is the "deviant minority" explanation, and what policy does it imply?
**Definition (learn):** Most people are decent; a small self-interested minority causes the problem and needs punishment-based deterrence.
**Breakdown:**
- Policy implication: enforcement and deterrence (owner analysis).
- Blind spot: ignores systemic and cultural drivers such as discretion and monopoly.
- ARC 1.4 sets it beside the "decline of values" view; ARC 1.5 synthesises both (ETH-1011).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.4 (✅ owner 01). Policy implications ⚠️ owner analysis.
**Links:** ETH-1009, ETH-1011, ETH-1016 (prevention and punishment).
**Trap:** Treating deterrence as sufficient; the ARC rejects a purely punitive answer.
**Mains hook:** Deterrence is necessary against a deviant minority, but structural reform is needed to stop the minority being created (qualified).
**Back:** *"Few bad actors need the stick; the system must stop breeding them."*

### Flashcard ETH-1011 — ARC's own synthesis: values and institutions together
**Front:** What is the ARC's own position on ethical failure (ARC 1.5)?
**Definition (learn):** Both values and institutions matter; values need institutional "containers" to be durable.
**Breakdown:**
- Values alone are weakened by weak institutions.
- Institutions alone lack the internal commitment that makes rules observed.
- Owner's answer thesis: values durably contained within institutions that reduce discretion, increase transparency and empower the citizen.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.5 (✅ owner 01). Mains prompt in owner: "Values without institutional support will soon be weakened and dissipated" (10 marks).
**Links:** ETH-1001, ETH-1009, ETH-1010 (Deviant minority).
**Trap:** Quoting the ARC as "values matter most" misses the institutional half of its synthesis.
**Mains hook:** "Values without institutional support will soon be weakened and dissipated" — agree, and show the mechanism (discretion reduction, transparency, citizen charters); qualify that institutions too decay without a supportive values culture.
**Back:** *"Values alone wilt; institutions alone hollow. Contain values in institutions."*

### Flashcard ETH-1012 — Coercive vs collusive corruption
**Front:** Distinguish coercive corruption from collusive corruption (ARC 1.13).
**Definition (learn):**
- **Coercive corruption:** the citizen is an unwilling victim of extortion for an entitled service (the citizen pays to get what should be provided).
- **Collusive corruption:** bribe-giver and bribe-taker jointly benefit at society's cost (procurement, tax evasion, adulteration, evidence-tampering).
**Breakdown:**
- Coercive: revenue clerk delaying a mutation until "speed money" is paid (⚠️ owner example).
- Collusive: contractor and inspecting engineer certify substandard road work (⚠️ owner example).
- Owner Advanced: coercive corruption is said to have higher conviction rates in the ARC's analysis (owner Advanced §7 claim; ⚠️ not checked against the ARC text).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.13 (✅ owner 01). Legal classification in owner 19 (`19_Corruption-Legal-Framework`).
**Links:** ETH-1001, ETH-1006, ETH-1019 (legal framework, file 19 companion).
**Trap:** The citizen is not always "at fault" for paying a bribe; coercive corruption makes the citizen a victim. Collusive corruption makes both parties culpable.
**Mains hook:** Use the wedge to classify the dilemma before prescribing the remedy: coercive cases need protection and reporting routes; collusive cases need detection and deterrence.
**Back:** *"Coerced payer is a victim; colluding payer is a partner."*

### Flashcard ETH-1013 — Monopoly, discretion, competition and transparency (ARC 1.8)
**Front:** What does ARC 1.8 say about monopoly, discretion, competition and transparency, and what sectors does it cite?
**Definition (learn):** Monopoly plus discretion increases corruption; competition plus transparency reduces it.
**Breakdown:**
- Evidence: post-1991 liberalisation in telephones, steel, cement, sugar and two-wheelers (owner lists these sectors; the owner's Section 6 names five).
- Mechanism: removing discretion removes the official's rent-seeking point.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.8 (✅ owner 01). 1991 liberalisation reference (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1014 (centralisation), ETH-1015 (empowerment), file 01 Advanced §5 boundary case (automation; Bhoomi/Gyandoot).
**Trap:** Liberalisation is not a claim that ethics is irrelevant; ARC explicitly credits competition and reduced discretion.
**Mains hook:** Liberalisation shows structural correctives can work without moral revival, but its gains depend on regulatory capture being prevented.
**Back:** *"Monopoly + discretion = rent. Competition + light = fall."*

### Flashcard ETH-1014 — Over-centralisation and the accountability gap (ARC 1.9)
**Front:** Why does over-centralisation damage the human interface according to ARC 1.9?
**Definition (learn):** Over-centralisation widens the gap between authority and accountability, because more functionaries stand between the citizen and the final decision-maker.
**Breakdown:**
- More layers → more discretion points → more diffused responsibility.
- Local government's slow "taking root" keeps the problem alive after economic liberalisation (advanced owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.9 (✅ owner 01 and Advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-1013, ETH-1015, ETH-1017.
**Trap:** Decentralisation is not automatically an ethics fix; the owner's advanced trap warns it can create many small local monopolies of discretion.
**Mains hook:** Decentralisation narrows the authority–accountability gap only when matched by transparency, audit and capacity; otherwise it relocates discretion.
**Back:** *"More layers, more hands, less blame — decentralise with audit."*

### Flashcard ETH-1015 — Citizen empowerment as corrective (ARC 1.10)
**Front:** Which instruments does ARC 1.10 name as citizen-empowerment correctives?
**Definition (learn):** Citizen empowerment is the democratic corrective to the asymmetric interface.
**Breakdown:**
- Right to Information (RTI).
- Effective citizens' charters.
- Stakeholder involvement in service delivery.
- Public consultation and social auditing.
- ARC says these "dramatically curbed corruption and promoted integrity and quality of decision making" (owner Advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.10 (✅ owner 01 and Advanced §4). RTI Act, 2005 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1008, ETH-1014, `15_Transparency-RTI` owner, `17_Citizens-Charters` owner.
**Trap:** Empowerment tools presuppose literacy, awareness and organisational capacity; elite capture can reproduce the asymmetry inside the tool.
**Mains hook:** Empowerment converts a passive citizen into an active check, but its effect depends on literacy and administrative responsiveness (qualified).
**Back:** *"RTI, charter, social audit, consultation: the citizen's four keys."*

### Flashcard ETH-1016 — Prevention and punishment, not either/or (ARC 1.11)
**Front:** Does ARC 1.11 treat prevention or punishment as the main corrective against corruption?
**Definition (learn):** Enforcement of rule of law and deterrent punishment remain necessary even when systemic reform succeeds.
**Breakdown:**
- Prevention: systemic reform (ETH-1013, ETH-1015).
- Punishment: deterrent enforcement.
- Both are required: "enforcement of rule of law and deterrent punishment against corruption are critical to build an ethically sound society".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.11 (✅ owner 01 and Advanced §4).
**Links:** ETH-1009, ETH-1011, ETH-1020 (ARC terms of reference: vigilance and corruption).
**Trap:** "Prevention is enough" and "punishment is enough" are both wrong per the ARC.
**Mains hook:** The corrective is preventive and punitive; a qualified conclusion must say which lever a given case needs.
**Back:** *"Prevent the temptation, punish the breach — never one without the other."*

### Flashcard ETH-1017 — Three historical aggravators of Indian corruption (ARC 1.6–1.7)
**Front:** Name the three Indian-specific aggravating structural factors in the ARC's account.
**Definition (learn):**
1. Colonial legacy of unchallenged authority.
2. Asymmetry of power between citizen and official.
3. Early post-Independence over-regulation (licence-permit scarcity economy) that made citizens dependent on official discretion.
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced owner: policy choice (over-regulation, near-monopoly, subsidy-driven patron-client relations) turned "public servant into patron and master" and "citizens into mendicants" (ARC 1.7).
- Self-reinforcing incentive structure, not individual moral failing alone.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.6–1.7 (✅ owner 01 and Advanced §3). Phrases "patron and master", "mendicants" (✅ owner 01 Advanced).
**Links:** ETH-1008, ETH-1013, ETH-1018.
**Trap:** Do not attribute all three to "bad character" — the ARC treats them as structural drivers.
**Mains hook:** India's ethics problem is historically produced; reform must therefore redesign the scarcity and dependency structure, not only moralise.
**Back:** *"Colonial habit, unequal power, licence scarcity: the three roots the ARC digs."*

### Flashcard ETH-1018 — Political market: "most important determinant" (ARC 1.12)
**Front:** What does ARC 1.12 call "the most important determinant" of societal integrity?
**Definition (learn):** The quality of politics — whether politics rewards integrity and competence, or rewards private gain-seeking.
**Breakdown:**
- If integrity is incompatible with political survival, corruption migrates to sectors immune from competition (natural monopolies, discretionary permissions).
- Advanced owner: the "political market" conditions whether structural correctives hold.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.12 (✅ owner 01 and Advanced §2, §6).
**Links:** ETH-1019 (displacement corollary), ETH-1013, ETH-1016.
**Trap:** Structural reform (competition, IT) is not sufficient if the political demand for illegitimate funds persists; a "ceiling" applies to purely administrative reform.
**Mains hook:** Administrative reform has a ceiling set by political finance; qualified verdict: pair structural reform with political-finance reform.
**Back:** *"Quality of politics decides whether the good levers hold."*

### Flashcard ETH-1019 — Displacement corollary: corruption migrates, not vanishes
**Front:** What is the "displacement corollary" in the owner's advanced analysis?
**Definition (learn):** Reducing corruption in competitive sectors while leaving natural-monopoly sectors (land, policing, discretionary permissions) untouched relocates corruption rather than reducing the aggregate.
**Breakdown:**
- ARC 1.12 warning (✅ owner 01 Advanced §3).
- Advanced probable question: "Corruption migrates rather than disappears."
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Advanced §3 (analytical corollary).
**Links:** ETH-1018, ETH-1013, file 01 Advanced §5 boundary case.
**Trap:** Liberalisation success in telephones does not imply success in land records; the sector matters.
**Mains hook:** Corruption migrates to where discretion survives competition; a qualified reform map should target the surviving monopolies.
**Back:** *"Squeeze the balloon in one place and it bulges in another."*

### Flashcard ETH-1020 — 2nd ARC 4th Report "Ethics in Governance" (2007): terms of reference
**Front:** Which report is the owner's source, when was it released, and what three areas did its terms of reference cover on ethics?
**Definition (learn):** The 2nd Administrative Reforms Commission's 4th Report, *Ethics in Governance*, dated January 2007.
**Breakdown:** Terms of reference (ARC 1.18):
- (a) vigilance and corruption;
- (b) relationship between the political executive and the permanent civil service;
- (c) codes of conduct for different organs of government.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 2007 (✅ owner 01). ARC terms of reference (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Links:** ETH-1016, `16_Codes-of-Ethics` owner, `21_Protecting-Honest-Officials-and-Vigilance`, `16_Codes-of-Ethics` owners.
**Trap:** The 4th Report is a 2nd ARC report; do not confuse it with the 1st ARC (1966–70; ⚠️ dates from memory) or any other "4th report".
**Mains hook:** Treat the ARC's three ToR items as the checklist for any reform answer on ethics in governance.
**Back:** *"Vigilance, executive-civil service, codes: ARC's three ethics terms."*

### Flashcard ETH-1021 — International correctives cited by ARC (UNCAC, ADB-OECD, World Bank, IMF-World Bank, RTI)
**Front:** Which international and domestic correctives does ARC 1.14–1.15 cite, and what has changed since 2007?
**Definition (learn):** The ARC names the UN Convention against Corruption, the ADB-OECD Anti-Corruption Action Plan for Asia and the Pacific, the World Bank's refusal to fund projects tainted by corrupt practices, the 2006 IMF–World Bank Singapore joint statement, and the RTI Act, 2005.
**Breakdown:**
- UNCAC adopted by UNGA resolution 58/4 on **31 October 2003** (owner Advanced §4).
- India endorsed the ADB-OECD Action Plan on **30 November 2001** (owner Advanced §4).
- India **signed** UNCAC on **9 December 2005** and **ratified** it on **9 May 2011** (owner Advanced §4, UN Treaty Collection/UNODC per owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Advanced §4 (UNCAC dates); signature and ratification dates carried from owner — ⚠️ re-check against UN Treaty Collection before quoting.
**Links:** ETH-1022 (OECD Anti-Bribery Convention trap), ETH-1020, `19_Corruption-Legal-Framework`.
**Trap:** The 2007 "signed but not ratified" position is now historical; quote the 2011 ratification.
**Mains hook:** International instruments give the legal architecture; ratification changes obligations only when domestic institutions implement them.
**Back:** *"UNCAC 2003 adopted, 2005 signed, 2011 ratified; ADB-OECD 2001 endorsed."*

### Flashcard ETH-1022 — Trap: UNCAC vs OECD Anti-Bribery Convention (1997)
**Front:** Is India a party to the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention, 1997?
**Definition (learn):** No. India is not a party to the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention, 1997; it is a separate instrument from UNCAC and from the ADB-OECD Action Plan.
**Breakdown:**
- UNCAC: UN instrument, adopted 2003, India ratified 2011.
- ADB-OECD Action Plan for Asia and the Pacific: India endorsed 2001.
- OECD Anti-Bribery Convention 1997: India not a party (owner Advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Advanced §4.
**Links:** ETH-1021.
**Trap:** Students conflate the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention with the ADB-OECD Action Plan. Both carry "OECD" in the name, but they are different instruments.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the instrument before citing it; a correct citation shows the candidate understands the legal basis of international anti-corruption.
**Back:** *"OECD in the name is not OECD in the treaty: India is not a party to the 1997 Convention."*

### Flashcard ETH-1023 — Trap: "decentralisation always reduces corruption"
**Front:** Why is "decentralisation always reduces corruption" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Decentralisation reduces the distance between authority and accountability but does not guarantee accountability unless it is matched by transparency and capacity.
**Breakdown:**
- Without audit it can create many small local monopolies of discretion (advanced §6).
- Decentralised schemes like MPLADS-type discretion can recreate coercive/collusive interfaces (advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 Advanced §8.
**Links:** ETH-1014, MPLADS-style discretion (file 18 owner).
**Trap:** Exam option: "decentralisation eliminates corruption" → false.
**Mains hook:** Decentralisation is necessary, not sufficient; its ethics payoff needs transparency and capacity (qualified).
**Back:** *"Closer is not cleaner unless it is audited."*

### Flashcard ETH-1024 — Comprehensive National Power and the ethics linkage (2020 GS-IV Q1(a))
**Front:** What are the five CNP components the owner uses, and how do ethics and values strengthen each?
**Definition (learn):** Comprehensive National Power (CNP) is a composite index — not military strength alone.
**Breakdown:**
- **Economic:** ethical governance (predictable rule of law, low graft) raises investor trust (✅ ARC 1.8 logic).
- **Military/strategic:** procurement integrity; apolitical professionalism (⚠️ owner analysis).
- **Socio-cultural/soft power:** citizen-empowerment-driven social harmony (✅ ARC 1.10 logic).
- **Technological/scientific:** research integrity; transparent AI-governance norms (⚠️ owner analysis; see `13`).
- **Diplomatic:** keeping treaty and humanitarian commitments builds credibility (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-IV Q1(a) (✅ owner 01 PYQ routing ledger).
**Links:** ETH-1013, ETH-1015, ETH-1021, ETH-1050 (Kalam chain, leader exemplar).
**Trap:** Treating CNP as a synonym for military strength is the named trap. Also avoid a generic "ethics is good" platitude; name the mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Ethics multiplies the credibility of each CNP component; social harmony is where values contribute most directly (qualified).
**Back:** *"CNP is five powers, each with an ethics credibility multiplier."*

### Flashcard ETH-1025 — Directive decoding in GS-IV ethics answers
**Front:** Match the directive words (Discuss/Examine; Explain; Critically examine/Evaluate; Illustrate) to the examiner's test and the structural move.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Discuss/Examine:** balanced multi-dimensional treatment with a reasoned position. Move: proposition → 2–3 dimensions → evidence per dimension → one counterpoint → reasoned conclusion.
- **Explain:** mechanism clarity. Move: define → show the mechanism → one Indian illustration.
- **Critically examine/Evaluate:** strengths and limitations. Move: claim → supporting evidence → limitation → weighted verdict.
- **Illustrate:** a named example doing analytical work. Move: abstract claim → precise example → what it demonstrates.
**Breakdown:**
- 10-mark ≈ 150 words; 20-mark case study uses the full eight-element architecture in file 22.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 §12 (directive architecture; GS-IV 10 + 10 marks format in PYQ ledger).
**Links:** ETH-1026 (claim→evidence→analysis→qualification), `22_Case-Study-Method` owner.
**Trap:** "Explain" is not "list"; "Illustrate" is not "name an example without analysis".
**Mains hook:** Use the directive to decide whether the answer's centre of gravity is mechanism, balance, evaluation or example.
**Back:** *"Discuss weighs, explain mechanises, evaluate judges, illustrate proves by example."*

### Flashcard ETH-1026 — Answer pattern: claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification
**Front:** What four-step pattern does the owner's evidence pool use for each Ethics answer point?
**Definition (learn):** Claim → named evidence/example → analysis (significance) → qualification (limitation). Use 1–2 per answer, not the whole pool.
**Breakdown:**
- Claim: discretion + monopoly raise corruption risk. Evidence: ARC 1.8. Significance: a structural corrective. Limitation: regulatory capture.
- Claim: citizen empowerment corrects asymmetry. Evidence: RTI Act 2005 + charters. Limitation: literacy/awareness dependency.
- Claim: over-centralisation widens accountability gap. Evidence: ARC 1.9. Limitation: decentralisation can relocate discretion.
- Counterpoint template: "While [X mechanism] explains most of the observed pattern, it does not fully account for [limitation]; on balance [verdict], provided [safeguard]."
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 §12.
**Links:** ETH-1025, ETH-1013, ETH-1014, ETH-1015.
**Trap:** Stating a claim with evidence but no limitation loses the "critically examine" mark.
**Mains hook:** The qualification is the examiner's signal of structural understanding.
**Back:** *"Claim, cite, reason, qualify — the four steps of an examiner-grade point."*

### Flashcard ETH-1027 — Structural vs dispositional causation
**Front:** How does the ARC's diagnosis rank structural and dispositional (character-based) causes of corruption?
**Definition (learn):** Structural causes (discretion, monopoly, centralisation) are privileged over a purely dispositional ("bad people") account, while both are conceded to operate together.
**Breakdown:**
- Liberalisation changes incentives, not character; if corruption were purely dispositional, liberalisation should not have reduced it. ARC 1.8 says it did (owner Advanced §3).
- Advanced owner: "few bad apples" theory is critiqued by this.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical inference (Advanced §3); ARC 1.8 evidence (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** ETH-1009, ETH-1013, ETH-1011.
**Trap:** "The ARC denies character matters" is wrong; it concedes both operate together.
**Mains hook:** Use the liberalisation test as evidence that incentive design, not only moral exhortation, moves conduct.
**Back:** *"Incentives move conduct even when character is constant."*

### Flashcard ETH-1028 — Constitutional morality as a product of civil education (2025 GS-IV Q1(b))
**Front:** What does the 2025 GS-IV Q1(b) demand say about constitutional morality?
**Definition (learn):** Constitutional morality is "not a natural sentiment but a product of civil education and adherence to the rule of law" for a public servant.
**Breakdown:**
- Anchor: institutions-over-exhortation argument (ETH-1011).
- Use as the opening paragraph for "why do public servants behave unethically" questions (owner §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q1(b) (✅ owner 01 Advanced §10 and PYQ routing ledger).
**Links:** ETH-1011, ETH-1016, `10_Sources-of-Ethical-Guidance`.
**Trap:** Treating constitutional morality as innate character. The PYQ wording frames it as learned.
**Mains hook:** Constitutional morality is institutionally cultivated (civil education + rule of law), so the state can build it; qualified because sentiment still needs internalisation.
**Back:** *"Constitutional morality is taught and enforced, not felt by nature."*

### Flashcard ETH-1029 — PYQ anchors for the Human Interface owner (2020, 2024 GS-IV)
**Front:** Which PYQs anchor the Human Interface owner, and what does each demand?
**Definition (learn):**
- 2020 GS-IV Q1(a): role of ethics and values in enhancing the three major components of CNP and social harmony.
- 2020 GS-IV Q1(b): education as a pervasive tool for individual development and social transformation (NEP 2020).
- 2024 GS-IV Q1(a): AI as a reliable input for administrative decisions. 2024 GS-IV Q1(b): dimensions of ethics guiding responsible behaviour.
**Breakdown:** All are 10 + 10 marks, 150 words each (✅ owner 01 PYQ tables).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 01 PYQ ledgers.
**Links:** ETH-1024 (CNP), ETH-1027, ETH-1028, `13_Emerging-Ethics` owner (AI).
**Trap:** Cross-check: 2020 Q1 is a two-part question; the CNP part is (a), NEP part is (b).
**Mains hook:** Answer the 2024 Q1(b) dimensions with the ETH-1025 structure (define, mechanism, example, counterpoint) and the 2024 Q1(a) AI question with a human-accountability caveat (qualified).
**Back:** *"Twenty-twenty asks CNP and NEP; twenty-four asks AI and ethical dimensions."*

### Flashcard ETH-1030 — Trap: "legal = no ethical question" (quotation check)
**Front:** Which quotation in the Human Interface owner states that legality and morality should not be equated?
**Definition (learn):** ARC Box 2.3 (US Senate Ethics Manual) warns that "morality has become identical with legality", rejecting the equation.
**Breakdown:**
- The ARC quotes this to reject collapsing morality into compliance.
- The gap is operational: propriety fails where law passes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Box 2.3 (✅ owner 01 §7). Quotation accuracy: ⚠️ taken from the owner; not fetched.
**Links:** ETH-1006, ETH-1005, file 10 owner.
**Trap:** Students cite "legal, therefore ethical" in propriety questions.
**Mains hook:** Use the three-test distinction to show why a compliant act can still be unethical.
**Back:** *"Legal is not automatically moral; the ARC says identity is a mistake."*

---

### GROUP — 02 Human Values and Lessons from Leaders

### Flashcard ETH-1031 — Human values and lessons from leaders: core theme
**Front:** What is the core theme of the Values and Leaders owner, and its answer thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Values are not innate; they are transmitted through family, education, religion/culture and lived example, then tested and reaffirmed or corroded by institutional incentives.
**Breakdown:**
- Sources of values → value → administrative translation (owner's table: truth → accurate reporting; compassion → empathy; courage → whistleblowing; self-discipline → resisting temptation; duty → devotion without craving credit).
- Exemplar-based learning: GS-IV quotation questions test translation of a teaching into a present-context administrative lesson.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 4th Report Box 2.8 and Annexure-I(2) (✅ owner 02). National Colloquium speech of ARC Chairman M. Veerappa Moily, 1 September 2006 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** ETH-1032 (values), ETH-1033 (transmission channels), ETH-1057 (quotation trap), ETH-1058 (quotation architecture), ETH-1011 (values + institutions).
**Trap:** The owner does not say that leaders' lives guarantee ethics. Values must be translated into rules under discretion and pressure.
**Mains hook:** A civil servant's values are inherited, but become administratively useful only when consciously translated into standards of conduct under discretion (qualified: exemplars need institutional backing).
**Back:** *"Inherit the value, translate it, test it under pressure."*

### Flashcard ETH-1032 — Values (definition) vs attitude
**Front:** Define "values" as the owner uses it and state how it differs from attitude.
**Definition (learn):** Values are enduring beliefs about what is desirable or worthwhile, guiding choice across situations. Attitude (file 03) is object-specific.
**Breakdown:**
- Value: general, enduring, cross-situational (e.g., honesty).
- Attitude: a stance toward one object (e.g., one particular policy or community).
- Value education: deliberate cultivation through family, schooling and institutional training.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 02 working definitions (analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1063 (attitude vs value vs belief vs aptitude), ETH-1061 (ABC model), ETH-1060.
**Trap:** Value and attitude are not synonyms; "I dislike this policy" is an attitude, "honesty matters" is a value.
**Mains hook:** Values set the standards; attitudes apply them to specific objects. Training must address both levels.
**Back:** *"Value is the rule you carry; attitude is the verdict on a particular case."*

### Flashcard ETH-1033 — Channels of value transmission
**Front:** Name the value-transmission channels the owner marks as commonly tested.
**Definition (learn):** Family, school/teachers, religion, peer group, media, and direct exemplar or role-model learning.
**Breakdown:**
- Family: early conditioning.
- Education/teachers: formal value education.
- Religion/culture: moral vocabulary.
- Personal role models and life experience: later correction.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 02 §5 (commonly tested list, analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1031, ETH-1034, ETH-1071 (formation channels, file 03).
**Trap:** Students often list only "family and religion". The owner's list includes media and peer groups, which matter for administrators.
**Mains hook:** Since values are transmitted through multiple channels, reform should use education, role models and media deliberately, not leave them to chance.
**Back:** *"Family, school, faith, peers, media, heroes: six doors values enter by."*

### Flashcard ETH-1034 — Nishkama karma (Bhagavad Gita)
**Front:** What is nishkama karma, and what GS-IV demand has tested it?
**Definition (learn):** Duty performed without attachment to its fruit (result).
**Breakdown:**
- Devotion to task without craving personal credit.
- 2025 GS-IV Q5(a): quotation on devotion to duty ("one who is devoted to one's duty attains highest perfection in life"), to be analysed through "sense of responsibility and personal fulfilment".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 (2025 GS-IV Q5(a) as routed). Concept ✅ Bhagavad Gita (standard reference; ⚠️ no verse number given in owner).
**Links:** ETH-1031, ETH-1046 (Kautilya), ETH-1047 (realism vs idealism).
**Trap:** Nishkama karma does not mean indifference to outcomes. It means no attachment to personal reward for the outcome; the duty must still be performed well.
**Mains hook:** Nishkama karma supports impartial, outcome-focused public service, but must be paired with accountability for results to avoid becoming an alibi for poor performance.
**Back:** *"Work for the work, not the wage: duty without craving."*

### Flashcard ETH-1035 — Gandhi's Seven Social Sins (ARC Box 2.8)
**Front:** List Gandhi's Seven Social Sins as printed in ARC Box 2.8, and the source the ARC gives.
**Definition (learn):** Seven "social sins" that decouple ends from ethical means, sourced by the ARC to Gandhi's *Young India*, 1925.
**Breakdown:** (1) politics without principles; (2) wealth without work; (3) leisure without conscience; (4) knowledge without character; (5) commerce without morality; (6) science without humanity; (7) worship without sacrifice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Box 2.8 (✅ owner 02). ⚠️ Owner-vs-source conflict: the *Young India* note of 22 October 1925 (Collected Works, Vol. XXVIII) reads "**pleasure** without conscience", not "leisure". Owner's own source check, not fetched here.
**Links:** ETH-1036 (provenance trap), ETH-1018 (political market = politics without principles), ETH-1024 (CNP).
**Trap:** Students quote "leisure" as the original. The text is "pleasure" in *Young India*; the ARC printed "leisure".
**Mains hook:** The list is a checklist of means-ends decoupling; it becomes a governance tool only when paired with codes and enforcement (qualified).
**Back:** *"P-W-L-K-C-S-W: each good cut from its ethical partner (principles, work, conscience, character, morality, humanity, sacrifice)."*

### Flashcard ETH-1036 — Trap: who composed the Seven Social Sins?
**Front:** Did Gandhi compose the Seven Social Sins list?
**Definition (learn):** Gandhi published and endorsed the list in *Young India* (1925), but introduced it as one a correspondent wanted readers to know, not as his own composition.
**Breakdown:**
- Cite it as "a list Gandhi published and endorsed".
- Do not assert he is documented as the original composer.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §5 and Advanced §3 (provenance nuance).
**Links:** ETH-1035, ETH-1057 (attribution caution).
**Trap:** "Gandhi's own seven sins" in an answer is a provenance error.
**Mains hook:** Accurate attribution is part of the answer; distinguish "endorsed" from "composed" to show evidence discipline.
**Back:** *"Published, endorsed, not necessarily composed."*

### Flashcard ETH-1037 — Vivekananda: "Learn everything that is good from others" (2024 GS-IV Q3(a))
**Front:** What does Vivekananda's quoted line on learning from others convey, and what is its source?
**Definition (learn):** "Learn everything that is good from others, but bring it in, and in your own way absorb it; do not become others."
**Breakdown:**
- Source: *Complete Works*, Vol. III, "The Common Bases of Hinduism", Lahore, 1897 (✅ owner 02).
- Administrative lesson: adapt global models (e.g., e-governance from Estonia or Singapore) to local conditions rather than copying wholesale.
- Limitation: over-applied, it can be used to resist legitimate reform.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-IV Q3(a) (✅ owner 02 PYQ ledger). Source traced (✅ owner 02, traceability per owner).
**Links:** ETH-1038 (second Vivekananda quote), ETH-1031, ETH-1058.
**Trap:** Selective borrowing is not "refuse all foreign ideas". The line asks for absorption in one's own way.
**Mains hook:** Use it for civil-service reform answers: borrow mechanisms, not uncritically transplant institutions; qualify that adaptation must not dilute reform substance.
**Back:** *"Borrow the good, make it your own, never become the other."*

### Flashcard ETH-1038 — Vivekananda: "The strength of a society is not in its laws" (2025 GS-IV Q3(c))
**Front:** What is the untraced Vivekananda line from 2025, and what is the nearest documented statement?
**Definition (learn):** "The strength of a society is not in its laws, but in the morality of its people." Set by UPSC as Vivekananda's; **not traceable** to the *Complete Works* (owner 02).
**Breakdown:**
- Nearest documented theme: "Our Duty to the Masses" (23 June 1894): "No country on earth has so many laws, and in no country are they so little regarded." (✅ owner 02 Advanced §3).
- Coherent position with ETH-1037: selective borrowing plus primacy of societal morality.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q3(c) (✅ owner 02). Provenance: ⚠️ not traced to primary text.
**Links:** ETH-1037, ETH-1057 (attribution trap), ETH-1011 (values + institutions).
**Trap:** Do not manufacture a citation. Answer the proposition; note UPSC's attribution.
**Mains hook:** Law is necessary but not sufficient; societal morality is the deeper safeguard. But the ARC's own diagnosis says institutions are essential too (qualified: the claim understates institutions).
**Back:** *"Laws are the fence; morality is the field they guard."*

### Flashcard ETH-1039 — Sardar Patel: "Faith is of no avail in the absence of strength" (2024 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What does the 2024 Sardar Patel quotation convey, and what is its provenance status?
**Definition (learn):** "Faith is of no avail in the absence of strength. Faith and strength, both are essential to accomplish any great work."
**Breakdown:**
- Lesson: conviction (values) must be paired with capacity and resolve to achieve results.
- Echoes the values-plus-institutions synthesis.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 02). ⚠️ No primary speech or letter traced; treat as UPSC's attribution.
**Links:** ETH-1011, ETH-1057.
**Trap:** Quoting it as a verified Patel speech.
**Mains hook:** Values need capacity; a civil servant who holds the right principle but lacks institutional power produces no outcome (qualified).
**Back:** *"Faith without strength is a prayer; strength without faith is a tyrant."*

### Flashcard ETH-1040 — Thiruvalluvar: equanimity in adversity (2025 GS-IV Q3(a); Tirukkural 623)
**Front:** What is the Thiruvalluvar line for 2025 Q3(a), and what does it teach administratively?
**Definition (learn):** "Those who in trouble untroubled are, will trouble trouble itself." This is a modern paraphrase (owner) of Tirukkural 623.
**Breakdown:**
- Tirukkural Chapter 63, *Iṭukkaṇ Aḻiyāmai* ("Not Despairing in Trouble" per the Wikipedia Tirukkural page; owner glosses "not being distressed by adversity"), couplets 621–630 (✅ Wikipedia Tirukkural, fetched 2026-11-01).
- Pope translation quoted by owner: "Who griefs confront with meek, ungrieving heart, / From them griefs, put to grief, depart" — ⚠️ not confirmed by the fetched page (Pope's rendering of this couplet is not shown there).
- Administrative lesson: composure defuses crisis; equanimity is not passivity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chapter 63, 621–630 (✅ Wikipedia). Kural 623 numbering (✅ owner 02 §3 cites 623; ⚠️ kural number not checked against text). Pope rendering ⚠️.
**Links:** ETH-1031, file 02 §4 DC example (ETH-1126 covers the 2025 DC case), ETH-1059 (PYQ map).
**Trap:** The owner says "paraphrase, not a named historic translation". Do not present it as a verbatim translation.
**Mains hook:** Composure under adversity is an administrative asset, but must be paired with decisive action; equanimity without action is a failure, not a virtue.
**Back:** *"Keep your head in trouble: Kural 623's calm defuses the crisis, not the crisis's reasons."*

### Flashcard ETH-1041 — William James: "discovery of my generation" (2025 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What does the 2025 William James quotation teach, and what provenance caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** "The greatest discovery of my generation is that a human being can alter his life by altering his attitudes."
**Breakdown:**
- Lesson: attitude is malleable and chosen, linking to file 03.
- Provenance: widely attributed to James, **not traceable** to his writings. Popularised by Norman Vincent Peale (1952) (✅ owner 02).
- Genuine James line on the theme (owner): "Believe that life is worth living, and your belief will help create the fact" (owner dates it 1895; ⚠️ James's "The Will to Believe" lecture was delivered 1896, so re-check the date before quoting).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 02). Peale 1952 (✅ owner 02). Date of the genuine line: ⚠️ owner says 1895, conflicts with the 1896 lecture date from memory.
**Links:** ETH-1057, ETH-1065 (Hovland-Yale), ETH-1064 (dissonance and attitude change).
**Trap:** "Life is 10% what happens and 90% how you react" style misattribution. Only the idea is safe for the exam.
**Mains hook:** Attitude is malleable, so training and reform should target attitude over career (qualified: malleability does not guarantee durable behaviour).
**Back:** *"Alter the attitude, alter the life — quote the idea, not the card."*

### Flashcard ETH-1042 — Mahavir: five Mahavratas and the Jain doctrines (2025 GS-IV Q4(b))
**Front:** List Mahavir's five great vows and the two associated Jain doctrines.
**Definition (learn):** Five Mahavratas: Ahimsa (non-violence), Satya (truth), Asteya (non-stealing), Brahmacharya (celibacy/chastity), Aparigraha (non-possessiveness). Associated doctrines: Anekantavada (many-sidedness of truth) and Syadvada (conditional predication).
**Breakdown:**
- Parshvanatha's four vows vs Mahavir's five (⚠️ owner: brahmacharya was earlier understood within aparigraha).
- Syat = "in some respect / from a standpoint".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §5. 2025 GS-IV Q4(b) (✅ owner 02 PYQ ledger).
**Links:** ETH-1043 (Anekantavada trap), ETH-1044 (aparigraha), `Philosophy/paper-1/indian/Jainism.md` (owner cross-link).
**Trap:** Brahmacharya is not "invented by Mahavir"; the owner says the tradition enumerated it separately.
**Mains hook:** Mahavir's teachings give ethical grammar for multi-stakeholder decisions and for limiting possession in office (qualified: use them as frameworks, not as religious claims).
**Back:** *"Ahimsa, Satya, Asteya, Brahmacharya, Aparigraha: five vows, two lenses (Anekanta, Syad)."*

### Flashcard ETH-1043 — Trap: Anekantavada is not moral relativism
**Front:** Does Anekantavada mean "no view is ever correct"?
**Definition (learn):** No. Anekantavada = truth is many-sided; no single perspective is complete. It is an epistemic doctrine, not moral relativism.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative use: structured multi-stakeholder consultation before a land acquisition or environmental clearance (⚠️ owner Advanced §3 application).
- Syadvada: conditional predication, "in some respect".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §6 and Advanced §2, §8.
**Links:** ETH-1042, ETH-1044.
**Trap:** The owner lists "no view is correct" as an explicit wrong reading.
**Mains hook:** Many-sidedness justifies consultation as an epistemic discipline, not indecision; decisions must still be taken and reasoned.
**Back:** *"Many sides to the truth, not no truth at all."*

### Flashcard ETH-1044 — Aparigraha and conflict of interest
**Front:** How does Aparigraha (non-possessiveness) translate into public-office ethics in the owner's advanced reading?
**Definition (learn):** Non-attachment to personal gain from office. Aparigraha maps onto conflict-of-interest and anti-nepotism norms.
**Breakdown:**
- The ethical core of probity is non-attachment to personal gain from office (⚠️ owner Advanced §3; cross-link to file 14).
- Aparigraha cautions against accumulation and nepotism in office (basic §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §3 (teaching); ⚠️ mapping to conflict-of-interest norms is the owner's analytical inference.
**Links:** ETH-1042, `14_Probity-Concept` owner.
**Trap:** Aparigraha is not asceticism. It is about not accumulating through the office.
**Mains hook:** Probity needs both rules on conflicts and an internal disposition of non-attachment (qualified: the rule is the floor).
**Back:** *"Possess nothing through power: non-attachment is probity's root."*

### Flashcard ETH-1045 — Kautilya: honey, poison and the birds' path (Arthashastra 2.9.32–34)
**Front:** What three Arthashastra propositions does the ARC quote, and what do they show?
**Definition (learn):** (1) "Just as it is not possible not to taste honey or poison placed on the surface of the tongue..." (Kangle 2.9.32); (2) embezzlement is as unobservable as a fish drinking water (2.9.33); (3) "It is possible to know even the path of birds flying in the sky, but not the ways of officers moving with their intentions concealed" (2.9.34).
**Breakdown:**
- Shows that opportunity and discretion breed corruption, not only character.
- Source: ARC Annexure-I(2), National Colloquium speech of 1 September 2006.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Advanced §2 (Kangle numbering per owner).
**Links:** ETH-1046 (remedy), ETH-1047 (realism vs idealism), ETH-1021 (ARC correctives).
**Trap:** The quotes are from the *Arthashastra*, not from the ARC's own text. They are quoted by the ARC.
**Mains hook:** Kautilya's point is one of the earliest structural theories of corruption; it supports reducing discretion over character-only reform.
**Back:** *"Honey on the tongue, fish in water, birds in sky: opportunity betrays the officer."*

### Flashcard ETH-1046 — Kautilya's remedy: disgorgement, reassignment, permanence for the honest
**Front:** What remedy does the owner attribute to Kautilya for officials who embezzle or who serve well?
**Definition (learn):** Officials who amassed money wrongfully should disgorge it and be moved to other work; those who increase the king's wealth "in just ways" should be made permanent in their offices.
**Breakdown:**
- Pairs vigilance with positive incentives (permanence) — a balanced design, not purely punitive.
- Anticipates modern rotation and performance-linked vigilance (⚠️ owner analytical).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Advanced §3 (Annexure-I(2)).
**Links:** ETH-1045, ETH-1047, `21_Protecting-Honest-Officials-and-Vigilance` owner.
**Trap:** "Kautilya advocates unrestrained surveillance of officials" is wrong; the remedy pairs vigilance with rewards.
**Mains hook:** Honest officials need security of tenure and positive incentives; rotation must be rule-bound or it becomes punishment (qualified).
**Back:** *"Disgorge the wrong, shift the wrongdoer, make the just permanent."*

### Flashcard ETH-1047 — Comparison: Kautilya's realism vs Gandhi's character idealism
**Front:** Compare Kautilya's assumption of temptation with Gandhi's emphasis on character.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Kautilya:** temptation is near-universal; design controls accordingly (systemic realism).
- **Gandhi:** character and conscience restrain power, wealth and knowledge (personal idealism).
**Breakdown:**
- Systemic design answers: rotation, audit, reward for honesty.
- Personal conduct answers: values education, role models, conscience.
- Owner's limit: Kautilyan realism alone can justify demoralising surveillance; Gandhian idealism alone under-weights discretion (ARC 1.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 Advanced §3, §6.
**Links:** ETH-1045, ETH-1046, ETH-1035, ETH-1009.
**Trap:** Presenting them as opposites. The owner says a strong answer uses both registers depending on framing.
**Mains hook:** Use Kautilya for vigilance-system design and Gandhi for internalised conduct; a balanced verdict pairs both (qualified).
**Back:** *"Kautilya designs for the tempted; Gandhi trusts the conscience. Use both."*

### Flashcard ETH-1048 — B.R. Ambedkar: constitutional morality and social democracy (doctrine)
**Front:** How does the owner present Ambedkar's teaching, and why does it attach no quotation?
**Definition (learn):** Constitutional morality, social democracy and the rejection of graded inequality connect liberty, equality and fraternity to everyday institutional conduct.
**Breakdown:**
- Officials must test neutral procedure against equal citizenship, dignity and the position of excluded groups.
- Personal benevolence cannot substitute for institutional justice.
- The owner deliberately does not attach an internet quotation (not verifiable).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §3 (doctrine, no quotation).
**Links:** ETH-1028 (constitutional morality PYQ), `06_Indian-Moral-Thinkers` (Ambedkar in depth).
**Trap:** Quoting an unverified Ambedkar line on ethics. Use the doctrine instead.
**Mains hook:** Constitutional morality is a learned discipline that makes procedure just in practice, not only in text (qualified).
**Back:** *"Liberty, equality, fraternity: the constitution works only through conduct."*

### Flashcard ETH-1049 — Savitribai Phule: Bhide Wada girls' school (1848)
**Front:** What did Savitribai Phule establish in 1848, with whom, and where?
**Definition (learn):** With husband Jyotirao Phule, she co-founded India's first girls' school at Bhide Wada, Pune, in 1848.
**Breakdown:**
- Faced social hostility for teaching girls (owner).
- Later worked on widow-remarriage support and against child-marriage and caste discrimination (owner).
- Conventionally regarded as India's first woman teacher/headmistress in the modern sense (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1848, Bhide Wada, Pune (✅ owner 02 §8a; standard historical fact, ✅ widely documented).
**Links:** ETH-1050 (Kalam), `04` owner (gender cross-link, 2024 Q6(a)), `06` owner (Indian thinkers).
**Trap:** The 2020 Q5(a) demand is about the "main factors" for gender inequality; a school alone is not the whole answer.
**Mains hook:** Structural gender inequality is corrected by institution-building (school, shelter, support) not sentiment alone; qualify that one institution does not solve generational inequality.
**Back:** *"Bhide Wada, 1848: a school for girls, built against the grain."*

### Flashcard ETH-1050 — A.P.J. Abdul Kalam: righteousness-to-peace chain (2019 GS-IV Q6(c))
**Front:** State the righteousness-to-peace chain and its attribution history.
**Definition (learn):** "Where there is righteousness in the heart, there is beauty in the character. When there is beauty in the character, there is harmony in the home. When there is harmony in the home, there is order in the nation. When there is order in the nation, there is peace in the world."
**Breakdown:**
- Bottom-up theory: individual integrity → institutional and national order.
- Kalam popularised it (owner: e.g., April 2007 address to the European Parliament) and credited it there to Sathya Sai Baba (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-IV Q6(c) (✅ owner 02 §8a PYQ ledger). April 2007 address (✅ owner 02, not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-1051, ETH-1057, ETH-1024 (CNP social harmony).
**Trap:** Do not claim Kalam composed it. The owner's attribution caution is explicit.
**Mains hook:** Use as an illustrative (not empirically proven) chain for why personal ethics has systemic consequences (qualified).
**Back:** *"Heart, character, home, nation, world: Kalam's chain, Sai Baba's source."*

### Flashcard ETH-1051 — A.P.J. Abdul Kalam: corruption-free nation (2022 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What does the 2022 Kalam prompt ask, and what is its provenance status?
**Definition (learn):** Reflection on a saying attributed to Kalam on building a corruption-free nation or society.
**Breakdown:**
- Kalam spoke often on a "corruption-free India" as a function of youth empowerment, education and integrity (owner).
- Owner: the precise wording is UPSC's paraphrase unless a primary citation is verified.
- Owner names *Indomitable Spirit* as an echo (⚠️ not checked).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 02 PYQ ledger). Provenance ⚠️.
**Links:** ETH-1050, ETH-1054 (Mission Karmayogi).
**Trap:** Quoting an exact Kalam sentence. Answer the idea.
**Mains hook:** Anti-corruption is a generational, education-led social movement as well as a top-down enforcement task (qualified).
**Back:** *"A youth-led, educated, integrity-based attack on corruption."*

### Flashcard ETH-1052 — Potter Stewart: "right to do" vs "right thing" (2022 GS-IV Q3(a))
**Front:** What line is attributed to Potter Stewart, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** "Ethics is knowing the difference between what you have a right to do and what is right to do." Widely attributed to Potter Stewart (US Supreme Court Justice).
**Breakdown:**
- The legality-ethics gap in one line.
- Use with an Indian example of a lawful but improper benefit (owner: lavish "gift" from a bidder, file 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-IV Q3(a) (✅ owner 02). ⚠️ No primary judicial opinion, speech or writing traced.
**Links:** ETH-1006 (legality vs morality vs propriety), ETH-1057.
**Trap:** Stating "Potter Stewart said in *X* case" is unsupported. Say "widely attributed".
**Mains hook:** Legal permission is not ethical clearance; propriety and public trust are further tests (qualified: law sets a floor).
**Back:** *"Right to do is law's question; right thing is ethics' question."*

### Flashcard ETH-1053 — Dalai Lama: judge success by what you gave up (2022 GS-IV Q3(c))
**Front:** What does the Dalai Lama line on success and sacrifice redefine, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** The most circulated matching quotation: "Judge your success by what you had to give up in order to get it." Widely attributed public teaching, not primary-source-cited.
**Breakdown:**
- Redefines success away from pure output toward what was not compromised (integrity, due process, colleagues' rights).
- Direct check against results-only performance appraisal.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-IV Q3(c) (✅ owner 02). ⚠️ Provenance not verified.
**Links:** ETH-1046 (remedy), ETH-1057, ETH-1059 (PYQ map).
**Trap:** Quoting it as a verified primary Dalai Lama text.
**Mains hook:** A results-only evaluation rewards shortcuts; a process-weighted measure of success protects integrity (qualified: outputs still matter).
**Back:** *"Score success by the price you refused to pay."*

### Flashcard ETH-1054 — Mission Karmayogi / NPCSCB: value and competency delivery
**Front:** What is Mission Karmayogi (NPCSCB), when was it approved, and what delivers its training?
**Definition (learn):** National Programme for Civil Services Capacity Building, builds behavioural competencies (values, attitude, integrity) into civil-service training.
**Breakdown:**
- Cabinet approval: 2 September 2020 (✅ owner 02 Advanced §4).
- Capacity Building Commission established 1 April 2021 (✅ owner 02 Advanced §4).
- Framework: FRAC (Framework of Roles, Activities and Competencies) (✅ owner).
- Platform: iGOT-Karmayogi.
- Scale per owner, as reported to Parliament with data as on 6 February 2026: over 1.49 crore users, 4,342 courses, 7.26 crore course completions, 23 languages (⚠️ time-varying; owner-dated).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates per owner (✅ owner 02). Scale figures are dated (6 Feb 2026) and must be re-checked before use.
**Links:** ETH-1055 (Mission Karmayogi PYQ 2024 Q6(b)), ETH-1081 (training design), `04` owner.
**Trap:** Do not claim a general APAR linkage without a service-specific DoPT order (owner).
**Mains hook:** The channel exists; its effect on conduct depends on linking competencies to appraisal and posting (qualified).
**Back:** *"Karmayogi: FRAC, iGOT, 2020 approval, 2021 commission, dated scale."*

### Flashcard ETH-1055 — PYQ map for Values and Leaders (2024, 2025 GS-IV)
**Front:** Which 2024 and 2025 GS-IV questions are routed to the Values and Leaders owner?
**Definition (learn):**
- 2024 Q2: (a) belief in peace, not merely talk; (b) global warming and human greed.
- 2024 Q3(a)–(c): Vivekananda, Sardar Patel, and a third quotation the owner says is Kant (⚠️ the owner's teaching table does not show a Kant entry; check against the paper).
- 2024 Q6: (a) equal opportunity despite gender identity in Indian culture; (b) Mission Karmayogi.
- 2025 Q3(a)–(c): Thiruvalluvar, William James, Vivekananda; 2025 Q4(b): Mahavir; 2025 Q5(a): devotion to duty.
**Breakdown:** All 10 + 10 marks, 150 words each (✅ owner 02 ledgers).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 PYQ ledgers. Kant item ⚠️.
**Links:** ETH-1037, ETH-1039, ETH-1040, ETH-1041, ETH-1042, ETH-1034, ETH-1054.
**Trap:** Counting 2024 Q3 as two quotations. It has three parts.
**Mains hook:** Each quotation answer should turn the teaching into a present administrative lesson and an Indian example.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four asks Vivekananda, Patel, Kant; twenty-five asks Valluvar, James, Vivekananda."*

### Flashcard ETH-1056 — Trap: values are fixed once formed
**Front:** Is it true that values are fixed once formed?
**Definition (learn):** No. The owner's William James teaching holds that attitudes and values can be altered by conscious effort.
**Breakdown:**
- Values are transmitted early but are tested and revised through institutional experience.
- Training, exemplar learning and institutional incentives can reshape them (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §6.
**Links:** ETH-1041, ETH-1074, ETH-1080.
**Trap:** Exam option "values cannot be changed in adulthood" is a false claim under the owner's framework.
**Mains hook:** Values are durable but revisable, which justifies periodic values training for serving officials (qualified).
**Back:** *"Formed early, still revisable by conscious effort."*

### Flashcard ETH-1057 — Trap: every printed quotation is an authenticated saying
**Front:** Does UPSC printing a quotation prove the thinker said it?
**Definition (learn):** No. A paper proves UPSC's attribution, not authenticity. The owner distinguishes traced, untraced and widely attributed lines.
**Breakdown:**
- Traced: Vivekananda 2024 Q3(a), *Complete Works* Vol. III (1897).
- Untraced: Patel 2024 Q3(b); James 2025 Q3(b); Vivekananda 2025 Q3(c).
- Widely attributed: Potter Stewart (2022); Dalai Lama (2022).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §6.
**Links:** ETH-1037–ETH-1041, ETH-1052, ETH-1053.
**Trap:** Inventing a citation to satisfy a quotation question loses marks; answer the idea.
**Mains hook:** Show evidence discipline by naming the provenance caveat in one line, then move to the lesson.
**Back:** *"UPSC attributes; the answer applies. Never invent the citation."*

### Flashcard ETH-1058 — Quotation answer architecture (five steps)
**Front:** What is the five-step structure for a quotation question ("what does it convey to you in the present context")?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Restate the idea in one line; (2) thinker's context in one line; (3) translate into a named administrative mechanism; (4) one Indian example; (5) one-line normative takeaway (owner §8).
**Breakdown:**
- Do not spend words on biography; examiners reward application.
- Add a provenance caveat in one line where relevant.
- 10-mark ≈ 150 words.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 §8 and §10.
**Links:** ETH-1025, ETH-1057, ETH-1026.
**Trap:** A quotation answer that is only a biography scores low.
**Mains hook:** The quotation is the anchor; the administrative mechanism is the answer.
**Back:** *"Idea, context, mechanism, example, takeaway: five beats per quote."*

### Flashcard ETH-1059 — PYQ map for file 02 Advanced and historical demand (2020, 2022)
**Front:** Which 2020 and 2022 GS-IV items route to the Values and Leaders owner?
**Definition (learn):**
- 2020 Q5(a): main factors for gender inequality in India and Savitribai Phule's contribution; 2020 Q5(b): internet expansion and cultural values.
- 2022 Q3(a): Potter Stewart; Q3(b): APJ Kalam on corruption-free nation; Q3(c): Dalai Lama on success and sacrifice.
**Breakdown:** All routed as 10 + 10 (+10) marks, 150 words each (✅ owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 02 historical ledger.
**Links:** ETH-1049, ETH-1051, ETH-1052, ETH-1053, ETH-1072 (social media, file 03).
**Trap:** The 2020 Q5(b) internet question is a values question, not a technology question.
**Mains hook:** Answer 2020 Q5(b) with transmission channels (ETH-1033): media and peer effects on values.
**Back:** *"Twenty: Phule and internet. Twenty-two: Stewart, Kalam, Dalai Lama."*

---

### GROUP — 03 Attitude: Content, Structure and Persuasion

### Flashcard ETH-1060 — Attitude: core theme (structure, persuasion, moral vs political)
**Front:** What is the core theme of the Attitude owner, and what is its answer thesis?
**Definition (learn):** An attitude is a relatively stable evaluative disposition toward a specific object, person, event or idea. It has three components, can be changed by persuasion, and does not mechanically determine behaviour.
**Breakdown:**
- Structure: cognitive, affective, behavioural (ABC).
- Function and change: persuasion (source, message, audience, channel) and cognitive dissonance.
- Gap: attitude-behaviour gap moderated by situational pressure and incentives.
- Syllabus clause: "moral and political attitudes" (owner §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner grounded in Rosenberg-Hovland (1960), Festinger (1957), Hovland-Janis-Kelley (1953), Central Civil Services (Conduct) Rules 1964 Rules 5, 7 (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** ETH-1061–ETH-1064, ETH-1065, ETH-1073, ETH-1074.
**Trap:** Attitude is not a value (ETH-1063); a positive attitude does not guarantee conduct (ETH-1067).
**Mains hook:** Attitude is changeable through targeted persuasion, but durable ethical conduct needs institutional reinforcement (qualified).
**Back:** *"Attitude: three parts, one object, changeable, not destiny."*

### Flashcard ETH-1061 — The ABC model: cognitive, affective, behavioural (Rosenberg-Hovland)
**Front:** Name the three components of an attitude in the ABC model and give one example of each.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Cognitive:** beliefs, knowledge and information about the object.
- **Affective:** the emotional charge (like/dislike, comfort/discomfort).
- **Behavioural (conative):** predisposition or intention to act toward it.
**Breakdown:** The model is associated with Rosenberg and Hovland (1960) (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rosenberg-Hovland (1960) (✅ owner 03). ⚠️ Year cited from owner; not fetched.
**Links:** ETH-1060, ETH-1062, ETH-1064.
**Trap:** Mixing components: "I believe corruption is wrong" is cognitive; "I feel angry at corruption" is affective; "I will report it" is behavioural.
**Mains hook:** Diagnose which component a scenario targets before choosing a persuasion lever (qualified: components are analytical, not separable in practice).
**Back:** *"Think, feel, do: cognitive, affective, behavioural."*

### Flashcard ETH-1062 — The affective component: why training must target feelings
**Front:** Why does the owner say training must target the affective, not only the cognitive, component?
**Definition (learn):** Attitudes that persist are often sustained by affect, so information alone does not change them.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: a field officer's negative attitude toward a community formed by social conditioning distorts service even where rules are neutral (owner §4).
- Training design: simulated citizen interaction and case-based learning (owner §10 unit 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §4 and §10 (analytical application; ABC model is ✅ theory).
**Links:** ETH-1061, ETH-1081 (training design), ETH-1054 (Mission Karmayogi).
**Trap:** Lecture-only training targets the cognitive component alone; the owner's warning.
**Mains hook:** Effective attitude training combines knowledge with affective engagement, and institutional rules close the residual gap (qualified: long-held biases fade without reinforcement).
**Back:** *"Teach the heart, not just the head."*

### Flashcard ETH-1063 — Four-way distinction: attitude, value, belief, aptitude
**Front:** Distinguish attitude, value, belief and aptitude.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Value:** general, enduring, cross-situational standard (e.g., honesty).
- **Attitude:** evaluative stance toward one specific object (e.g., "I dislike this policy").
- **Belief:** a single factual or causal claim held true.
- **Aptitude:** the trainable capacity to perform a role or task well (see file 04).
**Breakdown:** Value is broad; attitude is object-specific; belief is a claim; aptitude is capacity.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 03 §2 working definitions (analytical). Aptitude cross-reference to file 04 ✅.
**Links:** ETH-1032 (value vs attitude), ETH-1061, ETH-1084 (aptitude vs attitude).
**Trap:** Attitude and value are not the same; aptitude is neither. Keeping them apart is a frequently tested distinction (owner §5).
**Mains hook:** Declared values may be stable while object-specific attitudes vary; aptitude determines whether the attitude becomes competent performance (qualified).
**Back:** *"Value rules, attitude judges, belief claims, aptitude performs."*

### Flashcard ETH-1064 — Cognitive dissonance (Festinger, 1957) and its three resolution paths
**Front:** What is cognitive dissonance, and what are Festinger's three resolution paths?
**Definition (learn):** Psychological discomfort from holding two inconsistent cognitions, or from acting against one's own attitude.
**Breakdown:** Three paths:
1. Change the attitude to match the behaviour.
2. Change future behaviour to match the attitude.
3. Add a justifying cognition (rationalisation). This is the ethically risky path in administration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Festinger, 1957 (✅ owner 03 §2 and Advanced §7).
**Links:** ETH-1077 (rationalisation risk), ETH-1061, ETH-1067 (gap).
**Trap:** Dissonance is not always resolved by changing behaviour toward the ethical attitude; rationalisation is often easier.
**Mains hook:** Dissonance can drive principled objection or self-justifying misconduct; the direction is not fixed by the theory alone (qualified).
**Back:** *"Change the mind, change the act, or lie to yourself: the third is the risk."*

### Flashcard ETH-1065 — Hovland-Yale persuasion variables (source, message, audience; channel added later)
**Front:** Name the core persuasion variables of the Hovland-Yale approach and state where the channel fits.
**Definition (learn):** The communicator/source (credibility, expertise), the message (content and framing), and the audience/recipient (predispositions). The channel or medium is a later addition from wider communication research (Lasswell's formula), not part of the original Yale triad.
**Breakdown:**
- Source credibility: trusted messenger shifts attitude.
- Message framing.
- Audience predisposition; in algorithmic media the audience variable is micro-targeted.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Hovland, Janis and Kelley, *Communication and Persuasion*, 1953 (✅ owner 03 Advanced §7). Lasswell attribution for the channel (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Links:** ETH-1078 (trusted messenger), ETH-1072 (social-media audience), ETH-1068.
**Trap:** Including "channel" as an original Yale variable is a common error.
**Mains hook:** Use the variables to structure persuasion design; note algorithmic targeting changes the audience variable (qualified).
**Back:** *"Source, message, audience first; channel was added later."*

### Flashcard ETH-1066 — LaPiere (1934): attitudes vs actions
**Front:** What did LaPiere's 1934 study show about the attitude-behaviour gap?
**Definition (learn):** LaPiere toured the United States with a young Chinese couple; they were refused service only once in some 250 establishments. Yet most of the same establishments later said in writing that they would not serve Chinese guests.
**Breakdown:**
- Study title "Attitudes vs. Actions", *Social Forces* (✅ owner 03 Advanced §7).
- Founding case of the attitude-behaviour gap literature.
- Limit: a general stated attitude predicts a specific act poorly; it does not show attitudes never shape behaviour.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1934 (✅ owner 03 Advanced §7). Count "some 250" ⚠️ owner figure, not fetched.
**Links:** ETH-1067 (gap trap), ETH-1064, ETH-1074.
**Trap:** The study does not show attitudes are irrelevant; it shows general and specific measures differ.
**Mains hook:** Declared commitments (training, codes) must be backed by binding constraints to predict conduct (qualified).
**Back:** *"Said 'no' in writing, served them anyway: the gap is old and measurable."*

### Flashcard ETH-1067 — Trap: positive attitude guarantees ethical behaviour
**Front:** Why is "a positive attitude guarantees consistent ethical behaviour" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Situational pressures and institutional incentives can override an attitude. Attitude is necessary but not sufficient.
**Breakdown:**
- Official who favours transparency (attitude intact) withholds information under superior pressure (behaviour overridden).
- Institutional design (RTI mandate, whistle-blower protection) supplies a binding external constraint (owner Advanced §3).
- Caveat: "situational pressure" can become an excuse; genuine change must still be assessed (owner §10 unit 5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 03 §6 and Advanced §3.
**Links:** ETH-1066, ETH-1064, ETH-1082 (binding constraint), `15` and `19` owners.
**Trap:** The exam option stating that a positive attitude "ensures" ethics is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Attitude-based training plus binding constraints; the one without the other fails (qualified).
**Back:** *"Attitude is the compass; institutions are the brakes."*

### Flashcard ETH-1068 — Persuasion vs manipulation vs propaganda
**Front:** Distinguish persuasion, manipulation and propaganda using the owner's criteria.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Persuasion:** reasons and relevant evidence offered while the audience keeps meaningful choice; uses truthful argument.
- **Manipulation:** choice formally remains, but information, emotion or defaults are covertly arranged to bypass reflective judgment.
- **Propaganda:** repetition and emotional framing at scale, often state-sponsored, to bypass critical evaluation.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative test for persuasion: disclose public purpose, use accurate claims, allow questions and review.
- Test for manipulation: reject deception, hidden sponsorship, fabricated urgency.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 03 §3 and Advanced §2 (analytical distinction).
**Links:** ETH-1065, ETH-1069, ETH-1072.
**Trap:** Persuasion is not automatically manipulation; ethics depends on truthfulness and autonomy.
**Mains hook:** Judge a campaign by transparency of intent and audience autonomy, not by its desirable goal alone (qualified).
**Back:** *"Persuasion shows its reasons; manipulation hides its hand; propaganda floods the room."*

### Flashcard ETH-1069 — Coercion: lawful authority, necessity and proportionality
**Front:** When is coercion ethically acceptable in administration, per the owner's test?
**Definition (learn):** Coercion is a threat, penalty or control over an essential benefit that removes meaningful choice. It is acceptable only under lawful authority, necessity and proportionality, with recorded reasons and safeguards.
**Breakdown:**
- Persuasion keeps choice; manipulation disguises it; coercion removes it.
- Test: lawful authority, necessity, proportionality, recorded reasons, safeguards.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 03 §3 (analytical boundary table).
**Links:** ETH-1068, ETH-1012 (coercive corruption is a different use of the word).
**Trap:** Coercive corruption (ETH-1012) is extortion, while lawful coercion is state power under law. Do not confuse the two uses of "coercive".
