- Formula drafted in consultation with Gandhi and published in *Harijan* (1952) (✅ owner 06 §10B).
- Companion test: Antyodaya / talisman, the poorest face (⚠️ owner §10B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Harijan* 1952 (✅ owner 06).
**Links:** ETH-1145, ETH-1148, ETH-1163 (Art 39(b),(c)).
**Trap:** Trusteeship is not abolition of private ownership; the formula keeps a livelihood right.
**Mains hook:** Use the formula's regulation and income limits as a model for fiduciary public-service standards (qualified).
**Back:** *"Transform, don't abolish; livelihood yes, ownership as trust."*

### Flashcard ETH-1148 — Seven-step administrative ladder of trusteeship (2026 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** Give the seven domains of the trusteeship ladder and one administrative act for each.
**Definition (learn):**
1. Office and authority: refuse favour-based postings; no personal use of office.
2. Public money: budget is the trust corpus; treat under-utilisation as breach.
3. Discretion: record reasons; disclose and recuse.
4. Information: proactive RTI §4 disclosure of criteria and sanctions.
5. Natural resources and commons: consent, compensation, restoration; future beneficiaries.
6. Prioritisation: allocate scarce goods to the least advantaged first; record the criterion.
7. Exit and succession: complete handover; no "my scheme" branding.
**Breakdown:** Steps 1–3 secure procedural fairness; 4 and 7 accountability; 5 and 6 distributive and intergenerational fairness (owner §10B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 06 pointer). Ladder is owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-1147, ETH-1149, ETH-1100, `14`, `15`, `18`, `13` owners.
**Trap:** A definitional paragraph scores poorly; the question asks "in what ways".
**Mains hook:** Name rungs with acts, then state one limitation (qualified).
**Back:** *"Office, money, discretion, disclosure, commons, priority, handover."*

### Flashcard ETH-1149 — Trusteeship limitations and the reply that saves it
**Front:** State three limitations of trusteeship and the reply that saves the doctrine.
**Definition (learn):**
1. Voluntary and moral, not self-enforcing; without conflict-of-interest rules, audit, RTI and grievance, it becomes benevolent discretion.
2. Can legitimise existing concentrations of wealth and power; Ambedkar's structural critique is the standing objection.
3. A trustee can be honest and still unaccountable.
**Breakdown:** Reply: trusteeship as internal disposition, paired with external scaffolding of audit, disclosure and sanction ("disposition without scaffolding is sentiment; scaffolding without disposition is evasion").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 06 §10B limitations (analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1148, ETH-1153, ETH-1156 (institutions), `11` owner.
**Trap:** An unqualified "trusteeship solves it" answer reads as exhortation.
**Mains hook:** Disposition plus scaffolding; neither alone is fairness (qualified).
**Back:** *"Honest is not accountable; disposition needs scaffolding."*

### Flashcard ETH-1150 — Trap: Art 39(b) and 39(c) as trusteeship analogue (2024 caution)
**Front:** What constitutional analogue does the owner give for trusteeship, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Articles 39(b) and 39(c) of the Directive Principles: ownership and control of "the material resources of the community" to subserve the common good, and no concentration of wealth to the common detriment. These are the nearest constitutional restatement of a trusteeship ethic.
**Breakdown:**
- Caution: nine-judge Bench in *Property Owners' Association v. State of Maharashtra* (2024) narrowed the older expansive reading (owner §10B; ⚠️ not fetched).
- Do not assert all private property is covered.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Articles 39(b), 39(c) (✅ owner 06 §10B; text not fetched). 2024 judgment (⚠️ owner-cited).
**Links:** ETH-1147, ETH-1148, `Polity` owner (DPSP).
**Trap:** Citing 39(b) as settled authority over private property.
**Mains hook:** Use 39(b)/(c) as a directive analogue, not a settled property rule (qualified).
**Back:** *"Common resources serve the common good; private assets need care."*

### Flashcard ETH-1151 — Vivekananda vs Basava vs Gandhi: charity model vs dignity model
**Front:** How do Vivekananda and Basava both refuse a charity model of welfare?
**Definition (learn):** The charity model makes the giver superior to the receiver. Vivekananda's "Daridra Narayana" and Basava's Kayaka both use a dignity model in which service to the deprived, or the deprived's own labour, carries equal spiritual and civic worth.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative translation: avoid stigmatising paperwork (public shaming, humiliating proof of eligibility).
- Convergence: both shape how compassion is operationalised, not only that it exists.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner advanced §2–§3 (analytical comparison).
**Links:** ETH-1142, ETH-1152, ETH-1100, ETH-1091.
**Trap:** Treating Vivekananda's service as "charity" misses its dignity emphasis.
**Mains hook:** Dignity-based design reduces stigma and increases take-up (qualified: take-up evidence not owned here).
**Back:** *"Charity lifts from above; dignity serves as an equal."*

### Flashcard ETH-1152 — Basava: Anubhava Mantapa, Kayaka and Dasoha
**Front:** What do Basava's Kayaka, Dasoha and the Anubhava Mantapa mean for public ethics?
**Definition (learn):** Kayaka (*kayakave kailasa*): dignified honest work is itself the divine abode; all honest labour carries equal worth. Dasoha: sharing one's surplus with the community. Anubhava Mantapa: a participatory assembly of Lingayat *sharanas* open across caste and gender.
**Breakdown:**
- 12th century; in the Kalachuri kingdom under Bijjala (✅ owner 06 §5; ⚠️ dating and setting not fetched).
- Anticipates dignity of labour and anti-discrimination norms (⚠️ owner advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12th century, Kalachuri, Bijjala (✅ owner 06 §5 as stated; treat as tradition-attested, ⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-1151, ETH-1153, ETH-1162 (trap), ETH-1158.
**Trap:** Do not call it "the first parliament in the world" (owner's named caution).
**Mains hook:** Basava's assembly is a historical analogy to participatory governance, not an identity claim (qualified).
**Back:** *"Honest labour is divine; sharing is duty; the assembly is open to all."*

### Flashcard ETH-1153 — Guru Nanak: Naam Japna, Kirat Karo, Vand Chhako
**Front:** What are Guru Nanak's three pillars as taught, and what public-resource ethic do they support?
**Definition (learn):** Naam Japna (remembrance of the divine name); Kirat Karo (honest labour); Vand Chhako (sharing with others). The scriptural basis is the Guru Granth Sahib (owner cites Angs 8 and 1245).
**Breakdown:**
- Kirat Karo opposes rent-seeking; Vand Chhako supports equitable allocation.
- Public finance analogue: honest revenue collection (Kirat Karo) with equitable expenditure (Vand Chhako) (advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner basic §5: "three pillars" phrasing is later pedagogical packaging, not a verbatim triad.
**Links:** ETH-1152, ETH-1151, ETH-1154, `18` owner.
**Trap:** Quoting "three pillars" as Nanak's own verbatim triad.
**Mains hook:** Honest earning plus sharing is a public-resource ethic (qualified).
**Back:** *"Name, earn honestly, share: Nanak's three."*

### Flashcard ETH-1154 — Ambedkar: constitutional morality (4 November 1948)
**Front:** What does Ambedkar mean by constitutional morality, and when did he invoke it?
**Definition (learn):** Invoked in the Constituent Assembly on 4 November 1948, quoting George Grote: "a paramount reverence for the forms of the Constitution, enforcing obedience to authority acting under and within these forms yet combined with the habit of open speech... and unrestrained censure of those very authorities as to all their public acts."
**Breakdown:**
- Warning: in India "democracy is only a top-dressing on an Indian soil, which is essentially undemocratic" (owner §10).
- Constitutional morality must be diffused through society, not assumed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 November 1948 (✅ owner 06 §10; ⚠️ not fetched; CAD reference from memory).
**Links:** ETH-1028 (2025 Q1(b)), ETH-1155, ETH-1153, `advanced/10` owner.
**Trap:** Quoting the Grote definition as Ambedkar's own phrase.
**Mains hook:** Constitutional morality must be practised and diffused; law alone does not produce it (qualified).
**Back:** *"Reverence for forms, open speech, censure of the powerful: diffused, not assumed."*

### Flashcard ETH-1155 — Ambedkar, 25 November 1949: equality, fraternity and "life of contradictions"
**Front:** What three ethical claims does the 25 November 1949 speech make?
**Definition (learn):**
- **Equality:** India enters "a life of contradictions": political equality (one person, one vote) with continuing social and economic inequality, which must be removed "at the earliest possible moment or else those who suffer from inequality will blow up the structure of political democracy."
- **Fraternity:** "a sense of common brotherhood of all Indians" is needed, because liberty and equality without fraternity would require "a policeman" to enforce them.
- **Institutional safeguards:** minority and SC-ST safeguards, judicial review and fundamental rights distrust majoritarian goodwill alone.
**Breakdown:** Quoted with 25 November 1949 date (✅ owner 06 §10; verify long quotations against CAD Vol. XI before reproducing).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 November 1949 (✅ owner 06 §10 and 10A).
**Links:** ETH-1153, ETH-1154, ETH-1156, ETH-1157.
**Trap:** Treating the "life of contradictions" as a ready policy formula; it is a diagnosis.
**Mains hook:** Ambedkar's triad resists formal equality that ignores disadvantage and majoritarian pressure that overrides principle (qualified).
**Back:** *"Equal votes, unequal lives: fraternity makes the law stick without a policeman."*

### Flashcard ETH-1156 — Civil disobedience: the conditional test ("Grammar of Anarchy", 2026 Q3(a))
**Front:** State Ambedkar's conditional argument on civil disobedience (25 November 1949).
**Definition (learn):** Constitutional methods must be held fast; the bloody methods of revolution and civil disobedience, non-cooperation and satyagraha are excluded. Where no constitutional method was open, there was "a great deal of justification" for unconstitutional methods; where constitutional methods are open, there is no justification, and these methods are "nothing but the Grammar of Anarchy".
**Breakdown:**
- The condition: availability of constitutional channels.
- The examiner's phrasing ("equates to endorsing anarchy") states the claim unconditionally; the top-band answer corrects the premise.
- Two companion warnings: bhakti or hero-worship leads to dictatorship; social-economic contradictions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 November 1949 speech (✅ owner 06 §10A; ⚠️ verify verbatim wording against CAD).
**Links:** ETH-1155, ETH-1157, ETH-1158, ETH-1160.
**Trap:** Presenting the caution as an unconditional ban (ETH-1162).
**Mains hook:** A democracy earns the right to invoke the warning only by keeping constitutional channels genuinely open (qualified).
**Back:** *"Grammar of anarchy only where the door is open; where it is shut, the calculus changes."*

### Flashcard ETH-1157 — Comparison: Ambedkar, Rawls and King on civil disobedience
**Front:** Compare Ambedkar (1949), Rawls (*A Theory of Justice* §§55–59) and King (*Letter from Birmingham Jail*, 1963) on civil disobedience.
**Definition (learn):**
- **Trigger:** Ambedkar: only where constitutional methods are closed. Rawls: substantial clear injustice; normal appeals tried in good faith and failed; not so widespread as to impair constitutional efficacy. King: four steps (collect facts; negotiate; self-purification; direct action).
- **Addressee:** Ambedkar: political community and institutions; Rawls: sense of justice of the majority; King: conscience of the community.
- **Fidelity to law:** Ambedkar: constitutional forms; Rawls and King: accept legal consequences.
- **Risk named:** Ambedkar: anarchy, hero-worship, dictatorship; Rawls: excessive disobedience; King: misread as lawlessness.
**Breakdown:** Convergence: exhaustion of institutional channels is the pivot; violence is excluded (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 06 §10A (references as cited).
**Links:** ETH-1156, ETH-1158, ETH-1160.
**Trap:** Rawls's account applies to a nearly just society only (advanced owner objection reply).
**Mains hook:** The disagreement is over how confidently channels can be declared exhausted (qualified).
**Back:** *"All three pivot on exhausted channels; none tolerates violence."*

### Flashcard ETH-1158 — Civil disobedience: definitional elements and four exclusions
**Front:** What definitional elements must be kept precise, and what should civil disobedience not be confused with?
**Definition (learn):** Lawbreaking; direct (breaking the law objected to) vs indirect (breaking another law to protest); principledness; conscientiousness; publicity; non-violence; communicative appeal to fellow citizens.
**Breakdown:** Do not confuse with: (i) ordinary crime; (ii) violent insurrection; (iii) conscientious objection (seeks personal exemption, not policy change); (iv) a public servant's institutional dissent (must run through recorded internal channels).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 06 §10A (definitional elements, analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1156, ETH-1157, ETH-1074, `10` owner.
**Trap:** Equating a civil servant's dissent with civil disobedience (owner's named trap).
**Mains hook:** Use the element list to classify any protest or dissent before judging it (qualified).
**Back:** *"Direct or indirect, open, principled, peaceful, appealing: and never ordinary crime."*

### Flashcard ETH-1159 — Protest rights: Amit Sahni (2020) and MKSS (2018)
**Front:** What do *Amit Sahni* (2020) and *Mazdoor Kisan Shakti Sangathan* (2018) hold about protest?
**Definition (learn):**
- *Amit Sahni v. Commissioner of Police* (2020, the Shaheen Bagh matter): the right to protest is real but not absolute; public ways cannot be occupied indefinitely; protests must occur in designated places.
- *Mazdoor Kisan Shakti Sangathan v. Union of India* (2018): the right must be balanced against residents' rights; authorities must frame reasonable, published guidelines, not blanket prohibitions.
**Breakdown:** Both cases are cited as ✅ owner 06 §10A.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 and 2018 (✅ owner 06 §10A; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-1160, ETH-1156, `Polity` owner (Art 19).
**Trap:** Treating protest rights as absolute or as banned; both extremes are wrong.
**Mains hook:** Protect the right, regulate the manner, publish the criteria (qualified).
**Back:** *"Protest is a right; the road is a shared space; rules must be published."*

### Flashcard ETH-1160 — Two-sided modern test and the seven-step administrative spine
**Front:** What two-sided test does the owner set for modern democracies, and what seven steps follow?
**Definition (learn):**
- State: show channels are genuinely open (accessible, effective, not captured).
- Movement: show good-faith prior use, non-violence, publicity, proportionality of disruption, acceptance of legal consequences.
- Seven steps: distinguish grievance from method; test channel availability first; protect the right and regulate the manner; police proportionately; keep a live negotiation channel; record reasons; review policy on merits.
**Breakdown:** Rewarding disruption teaches that channels are pointless (owner §10A step 7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 06 §10A (analytical framework). Articles 19(1)(a), 19(1)(b), 19(2), 19(3), 32, 226 (✅ owner; text not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-1156, ETH-1159, ETH-1157, `15` and `17` owners.
**Trap:** Writing only about policing protesters omits the state's side.
**Mains hook:** The modern answer is a dual duty: keep channels open and keep protest non-violent (qualified).
**Back:** *"Open the channel, police the violence, decide on the merits."*

### Flashcard ETH-1161 — Social capital and good governance (Putnam: bonding vs bridging)
**Front:** Define social capital and explain the bonding-bridging distinction and its governance role.
**Definition (learn):** Networks of trust, reciprocity and civic norms that enable collective action without constant enforcement. Bonding capital links people within a close group; bridging capital links across groups.
**Breakdown:**
- Lowers transaction costs of public action; supports social audits and corruption reporting.
- Indian application: MKSS-style social audits and *jan sunwai* public hearings.
- Limit: bonding-only capital can entrench caste or community nepotism; it helps governance only when bridging and inclusive.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 GS-IV Q6(b) (✅ owner 06 §11 ledger). Putnam distinction (⚠️ owner-named standard usage, not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-1159 (MKSS), ETH-1155, ETH-1152 (Anubhava Mantapa as inclusive assembly), `11` owner.
**Trap:** Social capital is not automatically good; bonding can exclude.
**Mains hook:** Build bridging capital through participatory institutions so trust serves governance, not gatekeeping (qualified).
**Back:** *"Bond within, bridge across: only bridging makes governance honest."*

### Flashcard ETH-1162 — Trap: Ambedkar is anti-Gandhi on principle
**Front:** Is Ambedkar's civil-disobedience caution an objection to satyagraha as such?
**Definition (learn):** No. His objection is to the method's use after a constitution exists, not a denial that satyagraha was justified against colonial rule.
**Breakdown:** The conditional structure (ETH-1156) makes the caution context-specific.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 06 §10A traps.
**Links:** ETH-1156, ETH-1140.
**Trap:** Naming Ambedkar and Gandhi as opposites on protest.
**Mains hook:** The post-constitutional context is the variable; colonial injustice had no constitutional channel (qualified).
**Back:** *"Colonial satyagraha: justified then. Constitutional disobedience: conditional now."*

### Flashcard ETH-1163 — Traps: Kautilya's Arthashastra, Basava's assembly, and Gandhi's trust
**Front:** Name the three named traps in the Indian thinkers owner that are not single-line errors.
**Definition (learn):**
1. Kautilya is purely realpolitik with no ethics (wrong; rajadharma and incentives).
2. Gandhi's trusteeship means the powerful keep full control (wrong; fiduciary, for society).
3. Buddhist Middle Path means indecision or compromise for its own sake (wrong; principled balance).
**Breakdown:** Each trap has an owner-named correction (basic §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 06 §6.
**Links:** ETH-1141, ETH-1147, ETH-1143.
**Trap:** Each misreading yields a one-sided answer.
**Mains hook:** Correct each misreading in one sentence before the main argument (qualified).
**Back:** *"Ethics in the treasury, trust not ownership, balance not indecision."*

### Flashcard ETH-1164 — Guru Nanak and Basava: sharing as public-resource ethic (cross-check)
**Front:** Why do Nanak's Vand Chhako and Basava's Dasoha count as classical antecedents of bridging social capital?
**Definition (learn):** Both embed sharing of surplus with the community, a precondition for bridging trust.
**Breakdown:**
- Basava: Dasoha, sharing surplus.
- Nanak: Vand Chhako, sharing with others.
- Owner cross-link to social capital (basic §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner basic §11 (analytical).
**Links:** ETH-1161, ETH-1152, ETH-1153.
**Trap:** Describing sharing as charity rather than community bridging.
**Mains hook:** Sharing norms create bridging trust, which supports social audit (qualified).
**Back:** *"Share the surplus, bridge the trust."*

### Flashcard ETH-1165 — Trap: Kautilya as purely statecraft with no ethical content
**Front:** Is the *Arthashastra* a purely realpolitik treatise?
**Definition (learn):** No. It frames the ruler's rajadharma and prescribes integrity-linked incentives.
**Breakdown:** Foundational source for administrative ethics, not just realpolitik (owner §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 06 §6.
**Links:** ETH-1141, ETH-1046.
**Trap:** Option "no ethical content" is false.
**Mains hook:** Use Kautilya as an ethical source for incentive design (qualified).
**Back:** *"Statecraft with a conscience of the treasury."*

### Flashcard ETH-1166 — PYQ map for the Indian Moral Thinkers owner (2018–2026)
**Front:** Which GS-I and GS-IV questions route to the Indian Moral Thinkers owner?
**Definition (learn):**
- 2018 GS-I Q3: significance of Gandhi's thought today (10 marks).
- 2018 GS-IV Q6: (a) quotation on weighing evil and good (attribution unclear in OCR); (b) Gandhi on anger and intolerance; (c) Tirukkural on falsehood.
- 2019 GS-IV Q6: (a) Socrates on unexamined life; (b) Gandhi on thoughts shaping character; (c) Kalam on righteousness (ETH-1050).
- 2020 GS-IV Q3: (a) Buddha's relevance today; (b) will to power and rationality (IR context). 2020 GS-IV Q6: Vivekananda; Gandhi; Socrates.
- 2021 GS-IV Q3: Vivekananda on perseverance; Dalai Lama on inner peace; Erikson.
- 2023 GS-IV Q3: power of kindness; awakening women; Vivekananda on love and hatred. 2023 GS-IV Q6: Guru Nanak (a); social capital (b).
- 2024 GS-IV Q3: three quotations. 2025 GS-IV Q3: Thiruvalluvar, James, Vivekananda; 2025 Q4(b): Mahavir.
- 2026 GS-IV Q3(a): Ambedkar and civil disobedience (ETH-1156). 2026 GS-IV Q3(b): trusteeship (ETH-1148).
**Breakdown:** 10 + 10 + 10 marks, 150 words each, except noted (✅ owner 06 ledgers and pointer).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 06 ledgers. 2026 ledger in separate file (owner pointer).
**Links:** ETH-1140, ETH-1050, ETH-1037–ETH-1041, ETH-1156, ETH-1148, ETH-1161.
**Trap:** Quotation items can share an owner with Values file 02; answer per stem.
**Mains hook:** Route each demand to a named thinker and mechanism before writing (qualified).
**Back:** *"Eighteen Gandhi, nineteen Kalam, twenty-three social capital, twenty-six civil disobedience and trusteeship."*

### Flashcard ETH-1167 — Gandhi's significance in present times (2018 GS-I Q3)
**Front:** What does the 2018 GS-I Q3 demand expect on Gandhi's thought?
**Definition (learn):** The significance of Mahatma Gandhi's thought in present times (10 marks, 150 words).
**Breakdown:** Cross-cutting GS-I question with ethical anchors (owner §5 ledger). Use the Seven Social Sins, trusteeship and ends-means unity as anchors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-I Q3 (✅ owner 06 ledger).
**Links:** ETH-1145, ETH-1146, ETH-1147, ETH-1035.
**Trap:** Biography is not significance; name a mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Gandhi matters today through ends-means integrity and fiduciary office (qualified).
**Back:** *"Means for ends, trust for power, sins for checks."*

## ETHICS — Part 2: Western Thinkers, Moral Theories, Public-Service Values, Ethical Guidance and Accountability

> Flashcards for Ethics owner files 07–11 (basic and advanced). IDs ETH-2001 onward.
> Verification tags: ✅ confirmed from a fetched page or repository owner; ⚠️ unverified or analytical inference from the owner. Owner-only confirmations are tagged `(✅ owner NN)`.

---

### GROUP — Western Moral Philosophers and Thinkers

### Flashcard ETH-2001 — Western moral philosophy: three reasoning methods plus a justice-design test
**Front:** What three reasoning methods does Western moral philosophy give GS-IV, and what fourth tool tests institutions rather than acts?
**Definition (learn):** Western moral philosophy supplies three classical decision procedures: virtue (Aristotle, character), duty (Kant, rule) and consequence (Mill, outcome). Rawls adds a justice-design test that evaluates the rule or institution itself, applied before individual acts are judged.
**Breakdown:**
- Virtue: "would a person of good character do this?" (Aristotle)
- Duty: "is the act consistent with a duty or universalisable maxim?" (Kant)
- Consequence: "does the outcome give the greatest good for the greatest number?" (Mill)
- Justice design: "would this rule survive scrutiny by someone who did not know their own position?" (Rawls, veil of ignorance)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rawls, *A Theory of Justice*, first presented in 1971 (✅ fetched Wikipedia "Justice as fairness"). Methods framing (✅ owner 07 §1, advanced §1).
**Links:** ETH-2017 (veil of ignorance), ETH-2032 (three procedures compared), ETH-2034 (three families in Ethics 08), ETH-2113 (sources of guidance).
**Trap:** The three methods are not trivia facts to be listed. They give different answers to the same dilemma; GS-IV rewards showing convergence or divergence.
**Mains hook:** Use at least two lenses in a values dilemma, then test the underlying rule with Rawls. Qualified: convergence strengthens a verdict, divergence needs a stated tie-break.
**Back:** *"Character, duty, consequence, then design: test the act, then the rule that licenses it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2002 — Socrates: the unexamined life
**Front:** What does Socrates' "unexamined life" claim mean, and how is it used administratively?
**Definition (learn):** The claim, in Plato's *Apology* (38a), is that the unexamined life is not worth living. It asks for continual self-examination of one's beliefs and reasons before one acts.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: Plato's *Apology* 38a (✅ owner 07 §3, quoting the passage).
- Administrative use: reflective self-scrutiny before exercising discretion; a discipline against unconscious bias (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Linked PYQ: 2019 GS-IV Q6(a) (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Plato's *Apology* 38a (✅ owner 07). 2019 GS-IV Q6(a) (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** ETH-2003 (know thyself), ETH-2004 (2020 Socratic PYQ), ETH-2041 (motive vs intention).
**Trap:** Do not present the line as a generic call to "think more". It is a standard of reasoned self-audit, not a sentiment.
**Mains hook:** Explain reflective self-audit, reason-giving, bias correction and institutional review. Qualified: self-examination reduces bias but does not replace external review or records.
**Back:** *"Examine before you exercise: the unexamined discretion is not worth exercising."*

### Flashcard ETH-2003 — "Know thyself": a Delphic maxim, not a Socratic sentence
**Front:** Who authored "know thyself", and what does its administrative use look like?
**Definition (learn):** "Know thyself" is a Delphic maxim associated with the Socratic tradition. It is not a sentence authored by Socrates.
**Breakdown:**
- Attribution: Delphic maxim, associated with the Socratic tradition (⚠️ owner 07 §2).
- Administrative use: rigorous self-examination of assumptions and biases (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Attribution caution from owner 07 §2 (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2002 (unexamined life), ETH-2026 (attribution caution in quotations).
**Trap:** Writing "Socrates said 'know thyself'" in a Mains answer is an attribution error. Say "the Delphic maxim, associated with Socrates".
**Mains hook:** Use it to introduce self-audit before discretion, but attribute it precisely.
**Back:** *"Delphi said it, Socrates lived it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2004 — Socrates-attributed rejection of morality based on relative emotional values (2020 GS-IV Q6(c))
**Front:** What did the 2020 GS-IV Q6(c) quotation reject, and how should an answer handle its source?
**Definition (learn):** The Socrates-attributed quotation rejects morality grounded only in transient emotional or relative values. The answer should separate reasoned moral justification from preference, while keeping empathy as relevant evidence.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Discuss, 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Source: the exact classical source is unverified (⚠️ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Answer move: reasoned justification versus transient preference, with empathy admitted as evidence.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-IV Q6(c) (✅ owner 07). Source unverified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2002, ETH-2044 (intuition vs reasoning), ETH-2026 (attribution caution).
**Trap:** Do not cite a Socratic dialogue for the line. The owner records that the exact source is unverified.
**Mains hook:** Reasoned moral justification must be publicly defensible, and emotion is evidence to be tested, not a verdict in itself. Qualified: empathy corrects abstraction but does not decide the case alone.
**Back:** *"Feeling is evidence; reasons are the verdict."*

### Flashcard ETH-2005 — Plato: philosopher-king and justice as harmony
**Front:** What do Plato's philosopher-king ideal and tripartite-soul justice imply for public appointments and self-discipline?
**Definition (learn):** The philosopher-king ideal says rule should go to those with wisdom and competence, not wealth or birth. In tripartite justice, reason rules over spirit and appetite, and justice is harmony within the soul and the state.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutional translation: merit-based, competence-driven appointments (⚠️ owner 07 §3, cross-link to Nolan's objectivity in ETH-2087).
- Personal translation: reason over appetite, which maps onto resisting temptation (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Specialisation of function is the state-level reading (⚠️ owner 07 §1 table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only analytical translation (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2006 (Aristotle), ETH-2087 (objectivity), ETH-2085 (selflessness).
**Trap:** Plato is not a modern democrat; the merit argument must be restated in a democratic register (recruitment by transparent criteria), not as rule by an elite.
**Mains hook:** Use Plato for merit-based recruitment and self-discipline, then qualify with the risk of elitism in a democracy.
**Back:** *"Wisdom rules the soul; competence should rule the office."*

### Flashcard ETH-2006 — Aristotle: virtue as the mean, eudaimonia and habituation
**Front:** What is Aristotle's Golden Mean, and how does virtue relate to eudaimonia?
**Definition (learn):** Virtue is a habituated disposition lying between two vicious extremes (for example, courage between cowardice and recklessness). The good life (eudaimonia, human flourishing) is achieved by practising virtues, not by following rules or calculating outcomes alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Golden Mean: deficiency and excess as the two vicious extremes (✅ owner 07 §5).
- Habituation: virtues are formed by repeated practice (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Generosity example: between wastefulness and miserliness (✅ owner 07 §3).
- Administrative use: proportionate, non-extremist judgment (⚠️ owner 07 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Golden Mean and eudaimonia (✅ owner 07 §2, §5).
**Links:** ETH-2007 (phronesis), ETH-2035 (virtue ethics theory), ETH-2080 (role-sensitive virtue).
**Trap:** The mean is not a numerical midpoint. It is context-relative and needs practical wisdom to locate (⚠️ owner 07 §11).
**Mains hook:** A district magistrate calibrating force between excess and under-reaction applies the mean. Qualify that the mean must be found by judgment, not by formula.
**Back:** *"Courage sits between cowardice and recklessness; good administration sits between excess and neglect."*

### Flashcard ETH-2007 — Aristotle's phronesis and the context-sensitivity debate
**Front:** What is phronesis (practical wisdom), and why does it contrast with Kant's universal rule?
**Definition (learn):** Phronesis is the capacity to judge which mean is right in a particular situation. Virtue ethics is therefore a trained, context-sensitive judgment rather than a fixed formula.
**Breakdown:**
- Kant's universalisability can struggle with conflicting duties (truth-telling versus protecting an innocent person) (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
- Phronesis asks what a practically wise administrator would judge in this case (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
- Limit: phronesis without codes risks inconsistency between officials (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only analysis (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §2–3).
**Links:** ETH-2006, ETH-2008 (Kant universalisability), ETH-2033 (procedure comparison), ETH-2092 (codes).
**Trap:** Phronesis is not "gut feeling". It is practical reasoning in context, and it needs codes to stay consistent.
**Mains hook:** Case-study answers cannot be solved by one formula. Use phronesis to adjudicate, then pair it with codes of conduct for consistency.
**Back:** *"Rules say what; phronesis says what, here."*

### Flashcard ETH-2008 — Kant's Categorical Imperative: universal law formula
**Front:** State Kant's universal-law formulation of the Categorical Imperative.
**Definition (learn):** Act only according to that maxim whereby you can at the same time will that it should become a universal law. A maxim that is self-contradictory when universalised is forbidden.
**Breakdown:**
- Test: universalise the maxim of your act and check for contradiction.
- Perfect duties forbid maxims that become contradictory when universalised (✅ Wikipedia "Categorical imperative").
- Example: a lying promise fails the test.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *Groundwork* of the Metaphysics of Morals, 1785; universal-law formula cited at Akademie 4:421 (✅ Wikipedia "Categorical imperative"). Owner: two formulations (✅ owner 07 advanced §2).
**Links:** ETH-2009 (humanity formula), ETH-2010 (perfect/imperfect), ETH-2029 (Kant and lying trap), ETH-2035 (deontology).
**Trap:** Universalisability is a test of consistency, not of outcome. Do not reduce it to "follow the rules".
**Mains hook:** Use the test to show why an officer cannot justify concealment by claiming a good project outcome. Qualify: the test can be rigid in genuine conflicts of duty.
**Back:** *"Could everyone do it without contradiction? Then it is duty."*

### Flashcard ETH-2009 — Kant's humanity formula: persons as ends, never merely means
**Front:** State the humanity formulation of the Categorical Imperative and its administrative use.
**Definition (learn):** Act so that you treat humanity, in yourself or in anyone else, never merely as a means to an end, but always at the same time as an end.
**Breakdown:**
- Persons carry dignity; they may not be used as mere instruments.
- Administrative use: a citizen cannot be sacrificed for aggregate convenience alone (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Applies to AI-driven welfare eligibility as a test case (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Humanity formula at Akademie 4:429 (✅ Wikipedia "Categorical imperative").
**Links:** ETH-2008, ETH-2015 (Mill harm principle), ETH-2035.
**Trap:** Treating people "as ends" does not mean they cannot be asked for contributions; it means their consent and dignity are the limit.
**Mains hook:** Use it to block policies that sacrifice individuals for aggregate gain. Qualify with the need for trade-offs in public policy.
**Back:** *"Never a mere tool; always a person in the end."*

### Flashcard ETH-2010 — Kant: perfect versus imperfect (wide) duties
**Front:** Distinguish perfect from imperfect duties in Kant, with examples.
**Definition (learn):** A perfect duty admits no exception in one's own favour. An imperfect (wide) duty, such as beneficence, must be adopted as an end but allows latitude over when, how much and to whom it is discharged.
**Breakdown:**
- Perfect: do not make a lying promise (⚠️ owner 07 §2).
- Imperfect: beneficence; cultivating talents (✅ Wikipedia "Categorical imperative" example).
- Neglect of imperfect duties does not bring blame, but neglect of perfect duties does (✅ Wikipedia).
- Imperfect duties are typically not legally enforced (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Doctrine of Virtue, *Metaphysics of Morals* (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Links:** ETH-2009, ETH-2022 (Singer and imperfect duty), ETH-2066 (charity vs duty).
**Trap:** An imperfect duty is still a duty. "Not legally enforced" is not "optional".
**Mains hook:** Justify why helping others is a duty while discharge stays discretionary. This is Kant's middle position in the duty-charity debate.
**Back:** *"Perfect duties are no-exception; imperfect duties are must-adopt, may-choose-how."*

### Flashcard ETH-2011 — Kant's 2024 quotation: legal versus ethical guilt, with provenance caution
**Front:** What does the 2024 GS-IV Q3(c) Kant-attributed line claim, and what is its provenance?
**Definition (learn):** The line reads: "In law, a man is guilty when he violates the rights of others. In ethics, he is guilty if he only thinks of doing so." It shows that ethics demands a stricter standard than law: a harboured wrongful intention matters even without an external violation.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal guilt: an external breach of others' rights.
- Ethical guilt: a wrongful intention or disposition, even without an act.
- Use: the ethical dilemma is not the same as a legal violation (see ETH-2093).
- Provenance: not from the *Groundwork*, the second *Critique* or the *Metaphysics of Morals*; closest source is Louis Infield's 1930 English *Lectures on Ethics*, reconstructed from student notes (⚠️ owner 07 §3, advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-IV Q3(c), 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 07 PYQ table). Infield 1930 provenance (⚠️ owner analysis; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2093 (dilemma vs violation), ETH-2012 (acting from duty), ETH-2133 (law vs ethics PYQ), ETH-2069 (Pogge later).
**Trap:** Do not cite a Kantian treatise for the sentence. Say "the paper attributes to Kant" and argue the idea.
**Mains hook:** Use it to show a lapse with no broken rule (intention to favour a contractor) is still an ethical failure. Qualify that the line must not be used to convict an involuntary passing thought.
**Back:** *"Law punishes the act; ethics also judges the intent."*

### Flashcard ETH-2012 — Kant: acting from duty gives moral worth (advanced)
**Front:** In Kant's *Groundwork*, what gives an action moral worth?
**Definition (learn):** Moral worth attaches to acting from duty, not merely in conformity with duty. Motive and maxim, not just the outward act, are the objects of moral appraisal.
**Breakdown:**
- Acting in conformity with duty: outwardly right but not from duty.
- Acting from duty: the maxim itself is the reason.
- Institutional link: codes of ethics reach intention-level failure, while codes of conduct reach completed acts (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kant's *Groundwork* doctrine (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3; textual claim as stated by owner).
**Links:** ETH-2011, ETH-2041 (motive vs intention vs consequence), ETH-2033 (codes mapping).
**Trap:** "Good outcome from a good act" is not automatically moral worth in Kant. Motive and maxim must be right.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish an officer who acts from duty from one who acts only in conformity with the rules. Qualify that outward compliance is still needed.
**Back:** *"Right act, wrong reason, no worth; right reason, moral worth."*

### Flashcard ETH-2013 — Utilitarianism: greatest happiness and higher and lower pleasures
**Front:** What is utilitarianism, and what did Mill add to Bentham's version?
**Definition (learn):** An act or policy is right if it produces the greatest good (happiness or welfare) for the greatest number. Mill added a qualitative distinction between higher and lower pleasures and protection for individual liberty.
**Breakdown:**
- Bentham and Mill: consequentialist theory (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Mill: higher pleasures (intellectual, moral) outrank lower ones (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Policy use: aggregate welfare justifies trade-offs (⚠️ owner 07 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Utilitarianism as consequentialism (✅ owner 08 §2). Mill's distinction (✅ owner 07 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2014 (act vs rule), ETH-2015 (harm principle), ETH-2036 (consequentialism theory card).
**Trap:** Utilitarianism is not "maximise money". Welfare and happiness are the measure, and quality of pleasure matters for Mill.
**Mains hook:** Use it for aggregate-welfare policy, then state its limit: the calculus must still respect rights.
**Back:** *"Greatest good for the greatest number, with higher pleasures weighing more."*

### Flashcard ETH-2014 — Act versus rule utilitarianism (advanced)
**Front:** Distinguish act utilitarianism from rule utilitarianism, and say why the second suits public administration.
**Definition (learn):** Act utilitarianism judges each act by its individual consequences. Rule utilitarianism judges the rule an act follows by the consequences of everyone following that rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Act: "is this particular act the best outcome?"
- Rule: "what happens if this rule is generally followed?"
- Public administration reasons this way because a policy is a rule applied repeatedly (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §2).
- Rule utilitarianism still justifies the rule by its consequences; deontology justifies it by inherent rightness (✅ owner 07 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only distinction (✅ owner 07 advanced §2, §7).
**Links:** ETH-2013, ETH-2016 (rule-util reply to punishing the innocent), ETH-2079 (rule utilitarianism as compromise), ETH-2081 (trap).
**Trap:** Rule utilitarianism is not rule-worship; it is a consequentialist justification of rules.
**Mains hook:** A policy is judged as a rule, not a single act. Qualify that exceptions still need a reasoned, reviewable process.
**Back:** *"Act judges the deed; rule judges the policy."*

### Flashcard ETH-2015 — Mill's harm principle and its exemptions
**Front:** State Mill's harm principle, and identify the groups it exempts.
**Definition (learn):** Power may be rightfully exercised over a member of a civilised community, against his will, only to prevent harm to others. His own good, physical or moral, is not a sufficient warrant.
**Breakdown:**
- Anti-paternalist premise: coercion of a competent adult needs harm to others as its ground.
- Exemptions: children and those not in "the maturity of their faculties" (✅ owner 07 §2; ✅ Wikipedia "Harm principle" lists minors and mentally ill people as excluded).
- Mill's bridge example: stopping a man from a damaged bridge he does not know about is soft paternalism (✅ Wikipedia "Paternalism").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *On Liberty*, 1859 (✅ owner 07 §2; ✅ Wikipedia "Harm principle").
**Links:** ETH-2053 (Dworkin paternalism), ETH-2054 (soft vs hard), ETH-2016 (liberty constraint), ETH-2057 (beneficence vs paternalism).
**Trap:** The principle does not forbid all paternalism. Soft paternalism for non-voluntary choosers is its exempted space.
**Mains hook:** Use it to sort inform-enable, nudge and override. Qualify with capacity assessment as the pivot.
**Back:** *"Harm to others licenses force; your own good does not."*

### Flashcard ETH-2016 — Mill's liberty principle as a constraint on utilitarian policy (advanced)
**Front:** Why is Mill's liberty principle a built-in limit on utilitarian reasoning, and how does rule utilitarianism answer the "punish the innocent" objection?
**Definition (learn):** A purely aggregate-welfare justification for coercion must also clear the harm test. Rule utilitarianism replies that the rule "punish only the guilty", judged by its long-run effects, beats an act-level exception, though whether this fully escapes act-level exceptions is disputed.
**Breakdown:**
- Coercive public-health or surveillance measures need harm-to-others grounding, not just aggregate benefit (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
- Rule-utilitarian reply to the mob-placating-punishment objection (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
- Residual dispute: act-level exceptions (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-2015, ETH-2014, ETH-2018 (Rawls difference principle).
**Trap:** Utilitarianism does not automatically override rights, because Mill's liberty principle constrains it. Do not state the trap without this qualification.
**Mains hook:** Test a coercive measure against both aggregate benefit and the harm principle. Qualify that the rule-utilitarian reply is not a full answer.
**Back:** *"Aggregate gain is necessary, not sufficient, for coercion."*

### Flashcard ETH-2017 — Rawls: original position and the veil of ignorance
**Front:** What is the original position, and what does the veil of ignorance hide?
**Definition (learn):** In the original position, parties choose the principles that govern society's basic structure. Behind a veil of ignorance they do not know their own ethnicity, social status, gender or conception of the good, so their choices are impartial.
**Breakdown:**
- Thought experiment, not a historical procedure (✅ Wikipedia "Justice as fairness").
- Design-stage tool: test whether a proposed rule is fair to the worst-off (✅ owner 07 advanced §3).
- Primary goods: liberties, opportunities, income, social bases of self-respect (✅ owner 07 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *A Theory of Justice*, 1971 (✅ Wikipedia "Justice as fairness").
**Links:** ETH-2001, ETH-2018 (two principles), ETH-2031 (trap), ETH-2020 (reflective equilibrium), ETH-2039 (justice approaches).
**Trap:** The veil is not literal ignorance of facts before a decision. It is a heuristic (see ETH-2031).
**Mains hook:** Use it for welfare eligibility, reservation or subsidy design. Qualify that it models mutually disinterested parties, which invites communitarian critiques.
**Back:** *"Design the rule as if you might be its worst-off beneficiary."*

### Flashcard ETH-2018 — Rawls: two principles, lexical order and the difference principle
**Front:** State Rawls's two principles of justice in their lexical order.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Equal basic liberties for all, compatible with the same liberties for others, with priority. (2) Social and economic inequalities are allowed only if attached to positions open to all under fair equality of opportunity, and to the greatest benefit of the least advantaged (difference principle).
**Breakdown:**
- Priority: liberty cannot be traded for economic gain (✅ owner 07 advanced §2).
- Fair equality of opportunity sits before the difference principle (✅ Wikipedia "Justice as fairness").
- Inequalities must benefit the worst-off (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lexical order: liberty, fair opportunity, difference principle (✅ Wikipedia "Justice as fairness").
**Links:** ETH-2017, ETH-2019 (mutual aid), ETH-2016.
**Trap:** The difference principle does not permit any inequality that happens to help a majority. It is a test on the least advantaged.
**Mains hook:** Use the lexical order to argue that an economic gain never justifies cutting basic liberties. Qualify with the practical problem of identifying the least advantaged.
**Back:** *"Liberty first, fair chances next, inequality only if it lifts the bottom."*

### Flashcard ETH-2019 — Rawls: natural duty of mutual aid and civil disobedience
**Front:** What is Rawls's natural duty of mutual aid, and where does his account of civil disobedience appear?
**Definition (learn):** The natural duty of mutual aid is the duty to help another in need or jeopardy when this costs little to oneself. Rawls's civil-disobedience account is in §§55–59 of *A Theory of Justice*.
**Breakdown:**
- Mutual aid: *A Theory of Justice* §19 and §51 (⚠️ owner 07 §3, owner-only citation).
- Used in the duty-charity debate (see ETH-2022, ETH-2066).
- Civil disobedience: §§55–59, compared with Ambedkar in Ethics 06 §10A (⚠️ owner 07 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Section references are owner citations, not fetched (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2010, ETH-2022, ETH-2066 (charity vs duty), ETH-2018.
**Trap:** Rawls's mutual aid is a duty of low-cost help, not an unlimited obligation. Do not present it as demanding as Singer's strong principle.
**Mains hook:** A non-utilitarian corroboration that helping is a duty. Qualify with the low-cost threshold.
**Back:** *"Help when it costs you little; a duty of mutual aid, not a favour."*

### Flashcard ETH-2020 — Reflective equilibrium (Rawls's method)
**Front:** What is reflective equilibrium, and how can a candidate use it in a case study?
**Definition (learn):** Reflective equilibrium is the method of moving back and forth between general principles and particular judgments until they cohere.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: a "gut reaction" to a case.
- Test: state the principle that the reaction presupposes.
- Revise: adjust either the judgment or the principle until they fit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only method description (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §2).
**Links:** ETH-2017, ETH-2043 (triangulation), ETH-2044 (intuition vs reasoning).
**Trap:** Reflective equilibrium is a method of coherence. It does not guarantee that the final principle is correct.
**Mains hook:** Use it to connect the intuition flagged by the case with a stated theory, then revise. Qualify with the risk of coherence without truth.
**Back:** *"Judgment and principle, adjusted until they agree."*

### Flashcard ETH-2021 — Singer: "Famine, Affluence, and Morality" and the drowning child
**Front:** What did Peter Singer argue in "Famine, Affluence, and Morality" (1972), and what is the drowning-child analogy?
**Definition (learn):** Singer argued that preventing grave harm at small cost is a duty, not charity. The drowning-child analogy: if you can wade in and save a child from a shallow pond at the cost of muddying your clothes, you must do so.
**Breakdown:**
- Publication: *Philosophy & Public Affairs* 1(3), 1972, pp. 229–243 (✅ Wikipedia "Famine, Affluence, and Morality").
- Strong principle: "If it is in our power to prevent something bad from happening, without thereby sacrificing anything of comparable moral importance, we ought, morally, to do it" (✅ Wikipedia).
- Corollaries: distance is morally irrelevant; the presence of other helpers does not reduce one's duty (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Conclusion: the duty/charity distinction cannot be justified (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1972 publication, pp. 229–243 (✅ Wikipedia). Written 1971 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ETH-2022 (moderate principle), ETH-2066 (charity vs duty), ETH-2067 (demandingness), ETH-2069 (Pogge).
**Trap:** Singer is a consequentialist strand in the duty debate. Do not assume his strong principle is what the 2026 paper asks you to defend.
**Mains hook:** Use the drowning child to show that preventable grave harm at trivial cost is obligatory, then qualify the demandingness limit.
**Back:** *"Wade in to save the child: a trivial cost cannot excuse a grave harm."*

### Flashcard ETH-2022 — Singer's moderate principle and the 2026 Q6(b) qualifier
**Front:** How does the moderate Singer principle differ from the strong one, and why does the 2026 Q6(b) wording matter?
**Definition (learn):** The moderate principle says: prevent bad things when you can, without sacrificing anything morally significant. The strong principle says "anything of comparable moral importance". The 2026 Q6(b) wording, "without significantly affecting your financial stability", is the moderate, low-cost version.
**Breakdown:**
- Strong: "comparable moral importance" (✅ Wikipedia).
- Moderate: "anything morally significant" (⚠️ owner 08 §12C; wording not verified on the fetched page).
- 2026 Q6(b): the low-cost qualifier concedes the moderate version (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Say explicitly that the claim is the modest one, which is far harder to resist.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 GS-IV Q6(b) (✅ owner 08 §12C). Moderate principle wording (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2021, ETH-2067 (demandingness), ETH-2066 (charity vs duty), ETH-2075 (both-sides trap).
**Trap:** Defending the strong principle against demandingness misses the question's own qualifier.
**Mains hook:** Use the low-cost qualifier to disarm the demandingness objection, then state Kant's latitude as the balance.
**Back:** *"Modest claim, low cost: the duty is real without being ruinous."*

### Flashcard ETH-2023 — Urmson: "Saints and Heroes" and supererogation
**Front:** What is supererogation, and what did J. O. Urmson argue in "Saints and Heroes" (1958)?
**Definition (learn):** Supererogation is an act that is morally good and praiseworthy but not obligatory. Its omission earns no blame. Urmson argued that the three-fold scheme of duty, permissible and forbidden cannot capture the acts of saints and heroes.
**Breakdown:**
- Duty: obligatory, blame for omission.
- Permissible: neutral.
- Supererogatory: optional but good; praise for performance, no blame for omission (✅ owner 08 §12C deontic map).
- Forbidden: prohibited.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Urmson, "Saints and Heroes", 1958 (✅ owner 08 §12C; not fetched; ⚠️ verification pending).
**Links:** ETH-2065 (deontic map), ETH-2066 (charity vs duty), ETH-2075 (abolishing supererogation trap).
**Trap:** Supererogation does not disappear when duty is extended. Some heroic acts remain praiseworthy and optional.
**Mains hook:** Draw the line at the cost-harm ratio: below it, "charity" is duty; above it, supererogation begins.
**Back:** *"Praise for the hero, no blame for the rest; duty is minimal, goodness is open-ended."*

### Flashcard ETH-2024 — Dalai Lama and inner peace (2021 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What did the 2021 GS-IV Q3(b) quotation ask, and how should an answer handle its attribution?
**Definition (learn):** The 2021 paper asked candidates to reflect on the idea that inner peace precedes outer peace. The owner records that no primary speech or book source has been verified for the exact UPSC wording.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Discuss, 10 marks (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Administrative lesson: an official who has not regulated personal stress or bias (see Ethics 05 EQ link) cannot reliably mediate conflict.
- Composure is a precondition for, not a substitute for, just decisions (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 07). Exact wording unverified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2025 (Erikson, same paper), ETH-2026 (attribution caution), ETH-2044.
**Trap:** Do not cite a Dalai Lama text for the exact sentence; answer the idea as attributed by the paper.
**Mains hook:** Inner composure links to emotional intelligence in administration. Qualify that composure alone does not settle the dispute.
**Back:** *"Calm inside is a precondition for fair decisions outside, not a substitute for them."*

### Flashcard ETH-2025 — Erik Erikson and interdependence (2021 GS-IV Q3(c))
**Front:** What did the 2021 GS-IV Q3(c) quotation ask about interdependence, and what is Erikson's developmental framework?
**Definition (learn):** The quotation says life needs interdependence and that we need each other. Erikson's Eight Stages is a developmental model of psychosocial crises across the life course.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Discuss, 10 marks (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Administrative use: teamwork, cooperative federalism, citizen participation, reciprocal trust (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
- Interdependence does not erase individual accountability (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
- Erikson's stage model is a lens, not a protocol (⚠️ owner 07 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-IV Q3(c) (✅ owner 07). Exact sentence not verified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2024, ETH-2026, ETH-2011.
**Trap:** Interdependence is not an argument against accountability. Mutual reliance makes coordinated responsibility more important.
**Mains hook:** Use Erikson for resilience and cooperative governance. Qualify that psychological models supply a lens, not a checklist.
**Back:** *"We need each other, so each of us must still answer for our part."*

### Flashcard ETH-2026 — Attribution caution for quotation-based PYQs (Western names)
**Front:** Which quotation-based GS-IV names need an attribution caution, and what is the correct answer method?
**Definition (learn):** Several names in GS-IV quotations are public figures or figures whose widely circulated sayings are not always traceable to a primary text. Answer the idea, state the caution, and never invent a citation.
**Breakdown:**
- Dalai Lama (2021 Q3(b)): no primary source verified (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
- Erik Erikson (2021 Q3(c)): no primary source verified (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
- Potter Stewart (2022 Q3(a)): full treatment in Ethics 02 §8a, not here (✅ owner 07 §10 cross-link).
- Kant (2024 Q3(c)): provenance via Infield 1930 (⚠️ owner 07 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner attribution cautions (⚠️ owner 07 §10).
**Links:** ETH-2011, ETH-2024, ETH-2025, ETH-2003.
**Trap:** Assuming every Western name in a quotation is a professional philosopher with a traceable corpus.
**Mains hook:** A quotation answer should restate the idea, state the attribution caution, then argue the administrative mechanism.
**Back:** *"Answer the idea, flag the source, invent nothing."*

### Flashcard ETH-2027 — Abraham Lincoln: governmental policy as compound of good and evil (2018 GS-IV Q6(a))
**Front:** What did the 2018 GS-IV Q6(a) quotation claim about government policy, and what is the answer route?
**Definition (learn):** The quotation, attributed to Abraham Lincoln, says governmental policy is an inseparable compound of good and evil. The answer compares benefits, harms, distribution and rights before giving a qualified policy verdict.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Reflect, 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Verification: stem and attribution verified against the official scan (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Route: benefits, harms, distribution, rights, then a qualified verdict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-IV Q6(a) (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2016 (liberty and aggregate gain), ETH-2046 (triage), ETH-2045 (permissibility).
**Trap:** Do not treat a "compound of good and evil" line as a call to inaction. It asks for a qualified verdict.
**Mains hook:** Good policies carry costs, so the answer must identify the distributive costs and who bears them. Qualify with rights as a limit.
**Back:** *"Policy mixes good and evil; weigh who bears which before deciding."*

### Flashcard ETH-2028 — William James: altering life through attitudes (2025 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What did the 2025 GS-IV Q3(b) quotation attributed to William James claim, and what must an answer add?
**Definition (learn):** The quotation claims that people can alter their lives by altering their attitudes. The answer should explain attitude-mediated agency without denying structural constraints.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Theory, 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Verification: exact paper wording verified (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
- Add one administrative or social example and a source caution (✅ owner 07 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** ETH-2024, ETH-2026, ETH-2063 (agency under constraint in the doctor case).
**Trap:** Do not deny structure. Agency depends on attitude and on opportunity.
**Mains hook:** Attitude-based change matters in a constrained system, so the answer must still name the structures that limit agency.
**Back:** *"Change the attitude, but name the walls around it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2029 — Trap: Kant permits lying if the outcome is good
**Front:** Does Kant's deontology permit lying when it produces a good outcome?
**Definition (learn):** No. Kant's deontology rejects outcome-based justification for violating a duty, such as truthfulness. This is the opposite of utilitarian reasoning.
**Breakdown:**
- Duty of truthfulness is a perfect duty (✅ owner 07 §6; ✅ Wikipedia perfect duty).
- Universalisation of lying fails the test (ETH-2008).
- Outcome cannot override duty (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 07 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2008, ETH-2010, ETH-2030.
**Trap:** The wrong answer is "yes, for a good cause". Kant denies consequences the power to justify duty violations.
**Mains hook:** Use Kant to show why concealment "for project continuity" cannot be ethically justified. Qualify that Kant's rigidity can paralyse in genuine emergencies.
**Back:** *"No lie for a good end: the outcome never buys the duty off."*

### Flashcard ETH-2030 — Trap: utilitarianism always overrides rights
**Front:** Does utilitarianism always override individual rights for majority benefit?
**Definition (learn):** No. Mill's liberty principle constrains pure aggregate-welfare calculation, and rights-respecting consequentialism can protect rights instrumentally.
**Breakdown:**
- Mill's harm principle limits coercion (ETH-2015).
- Rule utilitarianism can protect rights because rights-protection produces better long-run results (⚠️ owner 07 §6).
- Pure aggregate calculation can still justify rights violations if the numbers are framed favourably (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 07 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2015, ETH-2016, ETH-2029.
**Trap:** Saying "utilitarians always sacrifice rights" misses the liberty constraint and the rule-utilitarian rights argument.
**Mains hook:** Test a coercive or welfare policy against rights with a utilitarian argument and a liberty check. Qualify that Mill's safeguard is partial.
**Back:** *"Utility does not always trample rights; the liberty principle keeps watch."*

### Flashcard ETH-2031 — Trap: the veil of ignorance is not literal ignorance
**Front:** Does Rawls's veil of ignorance require real, literal ignorance of facts before a policy decision?
**Definition (learn):** No. It is a thought experiment or heuristic for testing whether a rule is fair, not a literal informational restriction on decision-makers.
**Breakdown:**
- Hypothetical device (✅ owner 07 advanced §8; ✅ Wikipedia "Justice as fairness").
- Applied in policy design, not in reality (⚠️ owner 07 §6).
- Simplifying assumption: mutually disinterested parties (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 07 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2017, ETH-2018, ETH-2030.
**Trap:** Treating the veil as an administrative procedure to be implemented misreads Rawls.
**Mains hook:** Use the veil to test a subsidy rule's fairness, not to demand impossible anonymity. Qualify that the device simplifies real attachments.
**Back:** *"A test for fairness, not a blindfold for officials."*

### Flashcard ETH-2032 — Three decision procedures: Kant, Mill and Aristotle diverge
**Front:** How do the three procedures (duty, consequence, character) differ on the same dilemma, and where do they converge?
**Definition (learn):** Kant asks whether the act is right in itself, Mill asks whether the outcome is good on the whole, and Aristotle asks whether a person of good character would do it. They can produce different answers to the same dilemma.
**Breakdown:**
- Kant: "was the act right in itself?" (✅ owner 07 advanced architecture).
- Mill: "was the outcome good on the whole?" (✅ owner 07 advanced architecture).
- Aristotle: "would a person of good character act this way?" (✅ owner 07 advanced architecture).
- Rawls tests the rule or institution behind all three (✅ owner 07 advanced §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Architecture (✅ owner 07 advanced §1).
**Links:** ETH-2001, ETH-2034, ETH-2043 (triangulation), ETH-2045.
**Trap:** Treating the three as competing facts rather than decision procedures.
**Mains hook:** Show where they converge (strengthening a verdict) and where they diverge (requiring a justified tie-break), then add Rawls for the rule.
**Back:** *"Act, outcome, character: three questions, one dilemma, a reasoned tie-break."*

### Flashcard ETH-2033 — Codes of ethics and codes of conduct mapped onto Kantian layers (advanced)
**Front:** How do codes of ethics and codes of conduct map onto the Kantian intention and act layers?
**Definition (learn):** Codes of ethics are aspirational and reach intention and disposition. Codes of conduct are rule-based and reach completed acts. Their split mirrors Kant's intention-level and act-level appraisal.
**Breakdown:**
- Codes of ethics: intention, disposition, character (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §4).
- Codes of conduct: completed acts and precise rules (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §4).
- ARC 2.2.6 embeds this distinction in administrative reform (⚠️ owner 07 advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.6 (✅ owner 09). Mapping is owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2012, ETH-2092 (code of ethics vs conduct), ETH-2014.
**Trap:** The mapping is an analogy. Do not claim that the ARC used Kant.
**Mains hook:** Argue that a conduct code alone cannot reach intention-level failure, so an ethics code must cover disposition. Qualify that aspirational codes need enforcement to bite.
**Back:** *"Codes of conduct catch the act; codes of ethics catch the intent."*

---

### GROUP — Moral Theories: Deontology, Consequentialism, Virtue Ethics

### Flashcard ETH-2034 — Three families of normative ethics: the diagnostic questions
**Front:** What is the diagnostic question of each of the three normative families, and how do they differ?
**Definition (learn):** Deontology asks whether the act conforms to a rule or duty regardless of outcome. Consequentialism asks whether it produces the best overall outcome. Virtue ethics asks what a person of good character with practical wisdom would choose here.
**Breakdown:**
- Deontology: rule-focused; Kant (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Consequentialism: outcome-focused; Bentham and Mill (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Virtue ethics: character-focused; Aristotle, phronesis (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Normative theories are distinct from descriptive, meta and applied ethics (⚠️ owner 08 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner table (✅ owner 08 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2032, ETH-2035, ETH-2036, ETH-2037, ETH-2043.
**Trap:** The three are not reducible to one another. Deontology and virtue ethics are both anti-outcome in some ways but analytically distinct.
**Mains hook:** Name the dilemma, apply each lens, state convergence or divergence, then give the verdict.
**Back:** *"Rule asks is it right; outcome asks is it best; character asks would I be the kind of person who does it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2035 — Deontology: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses, administrative example
**Front:** Describe deontology as a moral theory: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses and an administrative example.
**Definition (learn):** An act is right or wrong according to its conformity with a rule, duty or right, regardless of its consequences. Kant is the paradigm deontologist.
**Breakdown:**
- Principle: duty and rights are the source of moral obligation (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Test: does the contemplated act violate a duty (truthfulness, promise-keeping, legal compliance, dignity) regardless of outcome? (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Strengths: non-negotiable floors; predictability; respect for persons (⚠️ owner 08 §3 analysis).
- Weaknesses: rigidity in emergencies and genuine conflict (✅ owner 08 advanced §6).
- Example: a land-acquisition officer's duty of honest compensation reporting (⚠️ owner 08 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only architecture (✅ owner 08 §2–6).
**Links:** ETH-2008, ETH-2009, ETH-2034, ETH-2039 (justice approaches).
**Trap:** Deontology is not the same as virtue ethics, though both reject pure outcome calculation.
**Mains hook:** Deontology sets the non-negotiable floor for administrative decisions. Qualify with the risk of paralysis when duties conflict.
**Back:** *"Rule first: the floor holds even when the outcome looks good."*

### Flashcard ETH-2036 — Consequentialism: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses, administrative example
**Front:** Describe consequentialism as a moral theory: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses and an administrative example.
**Definition (learn):** An act is right if it produces the best overall outcome or welfare compared with the alternatives. Bentham and Mill are the paradigm utilitarians.
**Breakdown:**
- Principle: outcomes and welfare are the measure of rightness (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Test: estimate aggregate welfare effects on all affected parties, including long-term precedent and trust (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Strengths: transparent aggregation; supports policy for many people (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Weaknesses: measurement problems (whose welfare counts), manipulation through framing, possible rights violations (✅ owner 08 advanced §6; ⚠️).
- Example: triage during scarcity maximises lives saved (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only (✅ owner 08 §2–6).
**Links:** ETH-2013, ETH-2014, ETH-2046 (triage), ETH-2034.
**Trap:** Consequentialism is not "outcome at any price": rule-consequentialism can protect rights.
**Mains hook:** Use it to optimise within the deontological floor, and say that the calculus is only as honest as its welfare metric.
**Back:** *"Count the outcomes; beware what the counting leaves out."*

### Flashcard ETH-2037 — Virtue ethics: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses, administrative example
**Front:** Describe virtue ethics as a moral theory: principle, test, strengths, weaknesses and an administrative example.
**Definition (learn):** An act is right if a person of good character, with practical wisdom (phronesis), would do it in that situation. Aristotle is the paradigm virtue ethicist.
**Breakdown:**
- Principle: character and practical wisdom are the source of right action (✅ owner 08 §2).
- Test: would a person with courage, justice, temperance and honesty choose this here? (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Strengths: captures nuance the other two can miss (⚠️ owner 08 §3).
- Weaknesses: circularity and inconsistency without codified criteria (✅ owner 08 advanced §6).
- Example: a just, compassionate administrator designing priority lists (⚠️ owner 08 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only (✅ owner 08 §2–6).
**Links:** ETH-2006, ETH-2007, ETH-2080 (role-sensitive), ETH-2082 (codes).
**Trap:** Virtue ethics is not a fixed formula; deontology is.
**Mains hook:** Use the virtue test to adjudicate where rules and calculations under-determine the answer. Qualify with codes to ensure consistency.
**Back:** *"Ask what the good officer would do, then check it against rules and consequences."*

### Flashcard ETH-2038 — Care ethics: relationships, dependency and vulnerability (⚠️)
**Front:** What is care ethics, and how does it correct abstraction without becoming partiality?
**Definition (learn):** Care ethics holds that moral judgment must attend to relationships, dependency, vulnerability and the unequal burdens of supposedly neutral choices. It is bounded by rights and justice, so care is not unprincipled favouritism.
**Breakdown:**
- Key figures: Gilligan and Noddings (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Adjacent to Aristotelian virtue ethics, not a variant of it (✅ owner 08 advanced §1).
- Triangulation step: identify dependency, voice and vulnerability (⚠️ owner 08 §3).
- Justice check prevents care from collapsing into partiality (⚠️ owner 08 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2039 (justice approaches), ETH-2043 (triangulation), ETH-2034.
**Trap:** Calling care ethics "emotional" and dropping it from the analysis. It supplies a distinct check.
**Mains hook:** Use care to test whether a neutral rule hides unequal burdens on the vulnerable. Qualify that care must stay within justice.
**Back:** *"Care notices who bears the cost; justice keeps care from becoming favour."*

### Flashcard ETH-2039 — Justice approaches: distributive, procedural, corrective and recognitional
**Front:** Name the four justice questions that institutions answer, and the gap each one addresses.
**Definition (learn):** Justice approaches ask whether institutions distribute benefits and burdens fairly, use fair procedures, protect equal basic status, and correct past wrongs. They answer different questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Distributive: who gets what? (⚠️ owner 08 §2)
- Procedural: is the process fair? (⚠️ owner 08 §2)
- Corrective: how are past wrongs repaired? (⚠️ owner 08 §2)
- Recognitional: whose identity and status are acknowledged? (⚠️ owner 08 §2)
- Rawls and wider justice traditions (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2018 (difference principle), ETH-2038 (care), ETH-2035.
**Trap:** Procedural fairness does not guarantee fair distribution. Each question needs its own test.
**Mains hook:** A welfare allocation can be procedurally clean and distributively unjust. Name which justice question the case raises.
**Back:** *"Who gets what, by what process, to repair what, and whose standing counts?"*

### Flashcard ETH-2040 — Ethical relativism versus absolutism and universalism
**Front:** Distinguish ethical relativism from ethical absolutism or universalism.
**Definition (learn):** Relativism holds that moral standards are relative to a culture or individual, with no universal standard. Absolutism or universalism holds that some moral standards hold across all cultures and contexts. Kantian deontology is typically absolutist about core duties.
**Breakdown:**
- Relativism: usually a foil to be critiqued in GS-IV (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Absolutism: core duties hold across contexts (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- These cut across the three normative theories (⚠️ owner 08 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2034, ETH-2008, ETH-2039.
**Trap:** Relativism and consequentialism are different axes: one concerns scope, the other the test of rightness.
**Mains hook:** Use absolutism to justify non-negotiable dignity and relativism to explain why cultural context matters in implementation. Qualify both.
**Back:** *"Scope asks where the standard holds; theory asks how to test it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2041 — Motive, intention and consequence: three separate objects of appraisal
**Front:** Distinguish motive, intention and consequence, and say why conflating them is the commonest case-study error.
**Definition (learn):** Motive is the underlying reason (career, compassion, fear). Intention is the specific outcome and means the agent deliberately chose. Consequence is what actually followed, which may diverge from both.
**Breakdown:**
- Deontology tests the maxim and chosen action (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Virtue ethics attends to character and judgment (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Consequentialism compares expected effects; actual outcomes may reflect luck (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- An officer can have a good motive, a defective intention and a bad consequence (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2011, ETH-2012, ETH-2048 (moral luck), ETH-2002.
**Trap:** Treating a good motive as cleansing a defective intention.
**Mains hook:** Separate the three before judging. Each attracts a different response: encouragement of the motive, correction of the intention, mitigation of the consequence.
**Back:** *"Why, what I aimed at, what happened: three verdicts, not one."*

### Flashcard ETH-2042 — Means versus ends: act versus rule consequentialism and Gandhi's unity
**Front:** How do act and rule consequentialism treat wrongful means, and how does Gandhi's ends-means unity differ?
**Definition (learn):** Act-consequentialism may permit a normally wrongful means when that act produces the best expected balance. Rule-consequentialism defends stable restraints for their long-run effects. Kantian deontology and Gandhi's ends-means unity reject treating a good end as cleansing wrongful means.
**Breakdown:**
- Act: permits a wrong means if this act is best (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Rule: restraint for long-run effects (⚠️ owner 08 §2).
- Kant and Gandhi: good end does not cleanse wrongful means (⚠️ owner 08 §2; Gandhi in Ethics 06).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-IV Q4(b) on means versus ends (✅ owner 08 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2014, ETH-2029, ETH-2045 (2018 Q4).
**Trap:** Naming your position is the clearest way to avoid a generic Mains answer.
**Mains hook:** State whether you hold act- or rule-consequentialism or a deontological floor, then argue. Qualify with the risk of rule worship.
**Back:** *"Act lets the end pick the means; rule and Kant keep the means honest."*

### Flashcard ETH-2043 — The triangulation procedure: five steps
**Front:** What are the five steps in applying the three theories to a dilemma?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Deontology first: identify the duty and check for violation regardless of outcome. (2) Consequentialism: estimate aggregate effects. (3) Virtue ethics: ask what practical wisdom would judge. (4) Care and justice check. (5) Reconcile: state the decisive principle where lenses diverge.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1 uses the deontological floor (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Step 2 optimises within the floor (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Step 3 captures context (✅ owner 08 §3).
- Step 4 adds care and justice (⚠️ owner 08 §3).
- Step 5 gives the verdict and its safeguards (✅ owner 08 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 §3).
**Links:** ETH-2034, ETH-2032, ETH-2038, ETH-2039, ETH-2082 (case-study architecture).
**Trap:** Running the three lenses and listing them is not triangulation. The reconciliation step is what earns credit.
**Mains hook:** Use the sequence to structure any values question: floor, optimisation, context, check, verdict.
**Back:** *"Floor, optimise, read the context, check the justice, then decide and say why."*

### Flashcard ETH-2044 — Moral intuition versus moral reasoning (2023 GS-IV Q4(b))
**Front:** Differentiate moral intuition from moral reasoning, as the 2023 GS-IV Q4(b) question asks.
**Definition (learn):** Moral intuition is a fast, largely automatic felt judgment that an act is right or wrong. Moral reasoning is slow, deliberate application of an ethical theory or explicit rule to justify the judgment.
**Breakdown:**
- Basis: intuition from experience, socialisation and affect; reasoning from explicit principles (✅ owner 08 §10; "System 1" and "System 2" framing is owner analysis, ⚠️).
- Strength of intuition: fast, early warning (✅ owner 08 §10).
- Limit of intuition: bias, in-group favouritism, hard to defend (✅ owner 08 §10).
- Strength of reasoning: transparent, defensible on appeal (✅ owner 08 §10).
- Limit of reasoning: slow, can rationalise a predetermined conclusion (✅ owner 08 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 GS-IV Q4(b), Differentiate, 10 marks (✅ owner 08 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2024, ETH-2041, ETH-2020 (reflective equilibrium), ETH-2129 (conscience).
**Trap:** Intuition is not a legal reason. "It just feels wrong" is not administratively defensible.
**Mains hook:** Name the initial intuition, then test it through explicit reasoning before recommending action. Qualify that intuition may flag a problem reasoning has not yet named.
**Back:** *"Intuition flags, reasoning justifies; use both, and defend the second."*

### Flashcard ETH-2045 — Permissibility of doing good absent express prohibition (2018 GS-IV Q4(a))
**Front:** Is a public servant permitted to do good beyond what rules require, where no rule forbids it? Give the three theoretical views and the institutional caution.
**Definition (learn):** Silence in the rulebook is not prohibition. Deontology says permissible and arguably obligatory if no duty is violated. Consequentialism says permissible if the act improves aggregate welfare without a larger downside. Virtue ethics asks what a prudent official would do in context.
**Breakdown:**
- Deontological view: permissible if no duty or right is violated, e.g. due process (⚠️ owner 08 §11).
- Consequentialist view: check precedent risk and resource diversion (⚠️ owner 08 §11).
- Virtue view: calibrated initiative, not rigid rule-citation or unchecked discretion (⚠️ owner 08 §11).
- Institutional caution: consistency and recorded reasons prevent favouritism dressed as compassion (⚠️ owner 08 §11).
- Revenue-officer waiver example: permissible if extended to all similarly placed applicants and recorded (⚠️ owner 08 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-IV Q4(a), Examine/Justify, 10 marks (✅ owner 08 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2009, ETH-2046, ETH-2058 (undue influence), ETH-2105 (cooling-off and reasons).
**Trap:** "No rule against it" is not "no cost to it".
