**Mains hook:** Yes, under consistency and recorded reasons; no, if applied selectively to a connected applicant. Qualify with the risk of ultra vires challenge.
**Back:** *"Unforbidden is not unlimited: same good for all, reasons on file."*

### Flashcard ETH-2046 — Scarce-resource triage: a dilemma-resolution framework
**Front:** How should a public-health or disaster administrator allocate scarce ventilators, vaccines or relief?
**Definition (learn):** Consequentialism says maximise lives saved. Deontology resists ranking a person's life as less valuable and favours procedural fairness. Virtue ethics asks what a compassionate, just administrator would design. The verdict is clinically anchored priority plus published, non-discretionary criteria.
**Breakdown:**
- Consequentialist position: maximise lives saved, possibly deprioritising the lowest survival chance (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Deontological position: first-come, clinical-severity triage, or lottery among similar cases (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Virtue position: blend of clinical urgency plus transparent criteria and no VIP queue-jumping (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Verdict: name all three, then recommend clinically anchored priority with published criteria (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Evidence: COVID-era hospital protocols (⚠️ owner 08 §13 unit 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️). 2021 GS-IV Q10 hospital-staffing case routed to Ethics 09 (✅ owner 09 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2036, ETH-2035, ETH-2078 (agent-relative), ETH-2077 (trolley), ETH-2111 (case-study PYQs).
**Trap:** An outcome formula alone devalues persons; a lottery alone ignores clinical need. Combine them.
**Mains hook:** Clinical urgency as the primary sort, with transparent rules and audit. Qualify with the risk that published criteria can still be gamed if enforcement is weak.
**Back:** *"Save the most, sort by urgency, publish the rules, let nobody jump the queue."*

### Flashcard ETH-2047 — Snowden-type disclosure: legal versus moral, with proportionality
**Front:** How should an answer weigh a Snowden-type disclosure that was legally prohibited but framed as a moral duty?
**Definition (learn):** The legal view is that unauthorised disclosure breaches a binding secrecy duty regardless of motive. Deontology sees a clash between public honesty and employer confidentiality, which needs a rights-based tie-break. Consequentialism weighs the public benefit of oversight against security harm. The defensible verdict turns on proportionality and necessity, after internal channels are exhausted.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal: a clear breach (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Deontological: a duty of honesty versus a duty of confidentiality; a citizen's right to know can break the tie (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Consequentialist: public benefit versus security and chilling effects (⚠️ owner 08 §12).
- Answer: state the competing values, apply institutional-channel-first, conclude on proportionality (⚠️ owner 08 §12; cross-link ETH-2127).
- Facts: 2013 disclosure of US surveillance programmes (✅ owner 08 §12 framing; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 GS-IV Q12, 20 marks (✅ owner PYQ tables in 08, 10, 22, 23).
**Links:** ETH-2129 (conscience case), ETH-2127, ETH-2043, ETH-2050.
**Trap:** Declaring the act "justified" or "unjustified" outright. The GS-IV-safe answer tests proportionality.
**Mains hook:** The act is defensible only if internal and protected channels were exhausted and the disclosure was necessary and proportionate. Qualify that judgments in real time are contestable.
**Back:** *"Not whether to disclose, but whether channels were tried and the leak was proportionate."*

### Flashcard ETH-2048 — Moral luck: Williams and Nagel, resultant and circumstantial luck
**Front:** What is moral luck, who developed it, and which two categories are usable in a GS-IV evaluation?
**Definition (learn):** Moral luck is the problem of morally judging agents for outcomes, circumstances, traits or causes partly outside their control. Resultant luck is luck in how a decision turns out. Circumstantial luck is luck in the situation one is placed in.
**Breakdown:**
- Developed by Bernard Williams and Thomas Nagel (✅ owner 08 §12A; ✅ Wikipedia "Moral luck").
- Resultant: a diligent, rule-compliant tender that fails due to an unforeseeable market shock (✅ owner 08 §12A).
- Circumstantial: an officer posted to a disaster-hit district (✅ owner 08 §12A).
- Usable in GS-IV: resultant and circumstantial (⚠️ owner 08 §12A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner attributes the 1976 papers (Proceedings of the Aristotelian Society, Supp. Vol. 50). ⚠️ Conflict: the fetched Wikipedia page cites a 1979 Nagel essay and a 1981 Williams volume and does not give 1976 venues; the 1976 citation is unverified here.
**Links:** ETH-2049 (four-part taxonomy), ETH-2050 (process test), ETH-2041, ETH-2043.
**Trap:** Moral luck does not mean outcomes never matter. It means the outcome is not the sole test.
**Mains hook:** Evaluate an official's good-faith decision by process, authority and diligence at the time, not only by the bad result. Qualify that the test cannot excuse negligence.
**Back:** *"Luck in the result, luck in the setting: judge the decision as made."*

### Flashcard ETH-2049 — Nagel's four-part taxonomy: constitutive and causal luck
**Front:** List Nagel's four types of luck and say which two are usable in an administrative answer.
**Definition (learn):** Resultant luck (how things turn out), circumstantial luck (the situation one is in), constitutive luck (one's temperament and character, shaped by upbringing), and causal luck (whether choices are themselves products of a prior causal chain).
**Breakdown:**
- Resultant and circumstantial: directly usable (⚠️ owner 08 §12A).
- Constitutive: a decision-maker's risk tolerance under pressure (✅ owner 08 §12A).
- Causal: the free-will debate, not an administrative test (✅ owner 08 §12A).
- Wikipedia attributes the four categories to Nagel alone (⚠️ conflict with owner's Williams-and-Nagel attribution).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four categories (✅ Wikipedia "Moral luck"). Attribution conflict flagged (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2048, ETH-2050.
**Trap:** Over-philosophising causal luck wastes word limit in a case answer.
**Mains hook:** Name the constitutive and causal categories briefly and spend the words on the decision.
**Back:** *"Result, setting, character, cause: use the first two in the answer."*

### Flashcard ETH-2050 — The process-over-outcome test and its limit
**Front:** How should a vigilance or evaluation process test a good-faith decision that turned out badly, and where does the test stop?
**Definition (learn):** Ask whether the officer exercised due diligence, acted within delegated authority, and used reasonable, good-faith judgment on the information available at the time. Outcome remains evidence, not the sole test. The test does not excuse negligence or an undocumented process.
**Breakdown:**
- Test: diligence, authority, good faith, information at the time (✅ owner 08 §12A; ⚠️ analysis).
- Link: the bona fides test in vigilance (see Ethics 21, outside this fragment) (✅ owner 08 §12A).
- Limit: apply only where diligence is demonstrable on the record (⚠️ owner 08 §12A).
- Negligent process with good outcome is not vindicated (✅ owner 08 §12A trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2nd report vigilance inquiry distinguishing bona fide error from misconduct (⚠️ owner 08 §12A).
**Links:** ETH-2048, ETH-2049, ETH-2041, Ethics 21 (outside this fragment).
**Trap:** "Outcomes should never be scrutinised" is wrong. Process is the primary basis.
**Mains hook:** Evaluate the process, record and authority at the time; then decide whether any negligence was foreseeable. Qualify that good faith must be shown, not asserted.
**Back:** *"Judge the process as it stood, not the luck that followed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2051 — Beauchamp and Childress: four principles of biomedical ethics
**Front:** Name the four principles, and state how real cases are resolved.
**Definition (learn):** Respect for autonomy, non-maleficence (do no harm), beneficence (act for the patient's good) and justice (fair distribution of benefits, risks and costs). They are prima facie principles, resolved by specification and balancing.
**Breakdown:**
- Autonomy: respect the patient's competent choice (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Non-maleficence: do no harm (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Beneficence: act for the patient's good (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Justice: fair distribution (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Specification and balancing resolve conflicts (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Beauchamp and Childress, *Principles of Biomedical Ethics* (✅ owner 08 §12B; book details not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2052, ETH-2053, ETH-2063 (doctor case).
**Trap:** Listing the four words without specifying and balancing scores as a definition, not an analysis.
**Mains hook:** Specify and weigh the principles for the given case, saying which yields and why.
**Back:** *"Autonomy, no harm, good, fairness: specify, then balance."*

### Flashcard ETH-2052 — Beneficence and non-maleficence: other-regarding action versus no harm
**Front:** Distinguish beneficence from non-maleficence.
**Definition (learn):** Beneficence is a positive duty to act for another's good: to prevent and remove harm and promote welfare. Non-maleficence is the narrower duty to do no harm.
**Breakdown:**
- Beneficence is other-regarding action (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Non-maleficence is the absence of harm (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Kant treats beneficence as an imperfect duty (ETH-2010).
- Singer and Rawls push beneficence towards duty (ETH-2066).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner definitions (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Links:** ETH-2010, ETH-2051, ETH-2057.
**Trap:** Beneficence and paternalism are not opposites; both can share a motive (ETH-2057).
**Mains hook:** A doctor who prevents a harm is beneficent; the question is whether the method respects autonomy.
**Back:** *"Non-maleficence does no harm; beneficence actively does good."*

### Flashcard ETH-2053 — Paternalism: Dworkin's three conditions
**Front:** State Gerald Dworkin's three conditions for an act to count as paternalistic.
**Definition (learn):** X acts paternalistically towards Y by doing Z when (1) Z interferes with Y's liberty or autonomy; (2) X does so without Y's consent; and (3) X does so only because X believes Z will improve Y's welfare or good. All three must hold.
**Breakdown:**
- Condition 1: interference with liberty or autonomy (✅ owner 08 §12B; ✅ Wikipedia "Paternalism" definition).
- Condition 2: absence of consent (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Condition 3: motive is the person's welfare (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Standard analysis from Dworkin's Stanford entry as cited (⚠️ the conditions themselves were not listed on the fetched page).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 §12B); Wikipedia confirms the elements (✅).
**Links:** ETH-2015 (Mill), ETH-2054, ETH-2063.
**Trap:** Dropping any condition. Without the welfare motive the act is simply coercion or pressure.
**Mains hook:** Apply all three conditions to the case, then classify the paternalism.
**Back:** *"Interferes, without consent, for the person's own good: all three or it is not paternalism."*

### Flashcard ETH-2054 — Soft versus hard paternalism
**Front:** Distinguish soft (weak) from hard (strong) paternalism.
**Definition (learn):** Soft paternalism intervenes where the choice is substantially non-voluntary (uninformed, incompetent, under duress or temporarily disabled). Hard paternalism overrides a competent, informed, voluntary choice for the chooser's good and is presumptively impermissible in a liberal order.
**Breakdown:**
- Soft: generally defensible, protects the agent's settled will (✅ owner 08 §12B; ✅ Wikipedia "Paternalism").
- Hard: presumptively impermissible (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Mill's bridge: soft (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Links:** ETH-2015, ETH-2053, ETH-2055, ETH-2063.
**Trap:** "All paternalism is wrong" is a trap. Soft paternalism is widely defensible, and the law is full of it.
**Mains hook:** The classification depends on capacity and voluntariness. Qualify that a competent patient's reluctant consent is not automatic proof of undue influence.
**Back:** *"Soft guards a shaky will; hard overrides a competent one."*

### Flashcard ETH-2055 — Pure versus impure paternalism; welfare versus moral paternalism
**Front:** Distinguish pure from impure paternalism, and welfare from moral paternalism.
**Definition (learn):** Pure paternalism restricts the very people who are protected. Impure paternalism restricts third parties to protect a class (for example, regulating manufacturers to protect consumers). Welfare paternalism aims at health, safety or material good. Moral paternalism aims to make a person morally better, a far harder position to defend.
**Breakdown:**
- Pure: the restricted and the protected are the same (✅ owner 08 §12B; ✅ Wikipedia "Paternalism").
- Impure: third parties restricted (✅ Wikipedia).
- Welfare: health, safety, material good (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Moral: making people better (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner and Wikipedia consistent (✅).
**Links:** ETH-2054, ETH-2056 (nudge), ETH-2053.
**Trap:** Calling impure paternalism "not paternalism" because third parties are restricted.
**Mains hook:** Regulating industry for consumers is impure paternalism and can be defensible; moralising a citizen's private conduct needs the strongest justification.
**Back:** *"Pure protects the restrained; impure restrains others; moral paternalism is the hardest to defend."*

### Flashcard ETH-2056 — Libertarian paternalism and the nudge (Thaler and Sunstein, 2008)
**Front:** What is libertarian paternalism or a nudge, and what limits it?
**Definition (learn):** Designing the choice architecture so the welfare-promoting option is the default or the salient one, while keeping a genuine, low-cost right to opt out. A nudge does not ban options or significantly change economic incentives.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: Thaler and Sunstein, *Nudge: Improving Decisions About Health, Wealth, and Happiness*, 2008 (✅ Wikipedia "Nudge theory").
- Choice architects: the designers of defaults (✅ Wikipedia).
- Nudge must be "easy and cheap to avoid" (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mandates are not nudges: banning junk food is not a nudge (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2008 book (✅ Wikipedia). Default renewable-energy example (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ETH-2054, ETH-2055, ETH-2105 (administrative response).
**Trap:** A default with no real opt-out is an override, not a nudge.
**Mains hook:** Nudges are the administration-friendly middle path: inform and enable, default with an opt-out, and no mandate on the competent adult.
**Back:** *"Set the default, keep the door open, never lock it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2057 — Beneficence versus paternalism: the decisive distinction
**Front:** What is the one-line test that separates beneficence from paternalism?
**Definition (learn):** Beneficence and paternalism share the same motive. They differ at one point: whether the person's autonomy is respected or overridden. Beneficence becomes paternalism when the professional's view of the person's good displaces the person's own competent choice.
**Breakdown:**
- Relation to values: beneficence promotes good through the patient's values; paternalism substitutes the professional's judgment (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Consent: sought and decisive, versus absent, bypassed or manufactured (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Information: full disclosure to the decision-maker versus routed through others (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Role: adviser versus decider (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Self-limiting: the patient can decline, versus declining is an obstacle (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner comparison table (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Links:** ETH-2052, ETH-2053, ETH-2058, ETH-2063.
**Trap:** Treating beneficence and paternalism as opposites. They share one motive.
**Mains hook:** The question is not whether the professional cared, but whether the choice stayed with the person.
**Back:** *"Same motive, different hand: autonomy respected or overridden."*

### Flashcard ETH-2058 — Voluntariness: persuasion, manipulation, coercion, undue influence and reluctant consent
**Front:** Distinguish persuasion, manipulation, coercion and undue influence, and say what "reluctant consent" signals.
**Definition (learn):** Persuasion offers reasons to a competent decision-maker who can reject them (legitimate). Manipulation alters decisions through non-rational means (illegitimate). Coercion is a credible threat leaving no reasonable alternative and voids consent. Undue influence is pressure through a relationship of dependence or authority without an explicit threat.
**Breakdown:**
- Persuasion: part of beneficence (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Manipulation: framing, exaggerated risk, engineered urgency (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Coercion: credible threat (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Undue influence: relationship-based (✅ owner 08 §12B; taxonomy full in ETH-2104).
- Reluctant consent: a diagnostic flag, not a verdict. If persuasion is routed to family rather than the patient, consent is at least suspect (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Links:** ETH-2104, ETH-2057, ETH-2063, ETH-2105.
**Trap:** Condemning persuasion as such. Persuading the patient is duty; persuading around her is not.
**Mains hook:** Argue whether reluctance tracks disagreement or pressure, then decide the voluntariness question on the facts.
**Back:** *"Reasons to her are persuasion; reasons around her are undue influence."*

### Flashcard ETH-2059 — Samira Kohli v. Dr. Prabha Manchanda (2008): consent and substituted consent
**Front:** What did *Samira Kohli* (2008) hold about consent, and what are its limits for the 2026 Q4(a) fact pattern?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court held that consent for a diagnostic laparoscopy did not authorise a hysterectomy, and that consent signed by the patient's mother while the patient was under anaesthesia was not valid substituted consent for a competent adult in a non-emergency. Consent requires competence, voluntariness and disclosure of alternatives.
**Breakdown:**
- Facts: diagnostic laparoscopy versus hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy (⚠️ owner 08 §12B; not verified).
- Holding: the patient, not the family, is the locus of consent (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Disclosure standard: modified Bolam for disclosure (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Boundary: it concerns consent never given, not reluctant consent (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Citation (2008) 2 SCC 1 (⚠️ owner 08 §12B; not verified; the fetched Indian Kanoon page was a different case).
**Links:** ETH-2060 (Montgomery comparator), ETH-2062 (family consent), ETH-2063 (doctor case).
**Trap:** Over-citing Samira Kohli as proof that reluctant consent is invalid. It is not.
**Mains hook:** Use the case for routing of consent to the patient and the disclosure standard. Argue voluntariness separately.
**Back:** *"The patient consents, not the family; reluctance is argued, not assumed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2060 — Montgomery v Lanarkshire Health Board (2015): material-risk disclosure
**Front:** What disclosure standard did the UK Supreme Court adopt in *Montgomery v Lanarkshire Health Board* (2015), and is it binding in India?
**Definition (learn):** The Court replaced the Bolam test for risk disclosure with a material-risk test. A doctor must disclose a risk that a reasonable person in the patient's position would attach significance to, or that this particular patient would.
**Breakdown:**
- Court: UK Supreme Court, decided 11 March 2015, [2015] UKSC 11 (✅ fetched Wikipedia).
- Rejected the Sidaway majority approach; a narrow therapeutic exception remains (✅ Wikipedia).
- Persuasive, not binding, in India (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2015 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ETH-2059, ETH-2063.
**Trap:** Treating a UK case as binding Indian law.
**Mains hook:** Use Montgomery as a comparator for patient-centred disclosure, labelled persuasive.
**Back:** *"Disclose what a reasonable patient would weigh, not what a peer would say."*

### Flashcard ETH-2061 — Common Cause v. Union of India (2018): autonomy as a constitutional value
**Front:** What did *Common Cause* (2018) recognise, and why does it matter for consent?
**Definition (learn):** The Court recognised self-determination and autonomy in end-of-life decision-making, reinforcing that autonomy is a constitutional value and not merely a professional courtesy.
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional value of autonomy (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Supports the competent adult's authority over decisions about own body (⚠️ owner analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 (✅ owner 08 §12B; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2059, ETH-2062.
**Trap:** Citing *Common Cause* as a general right to refuse all treatment. Its context is end-of-life decisions.
**Mains hook:** Autonomy is constitutional, so the family's supporting role is a legal as well as ethical point. Qualify with the end-of-life context.
**Back:** *"Autonomy is constitutional, not merely courtesy."*

### Flashcard ETH-2062 — Family-mediated consent in India: supporting versus substituting
**Front:** What is the normative limit of family-mediated consent for a competent adult in India, and what is the gendered point?
**Definition (learn):** Family involvement is culturally normal and often supportive. For a competent adult, the family's role is supporting, not substituting. Substituted judgment is legitimate only where the patient lacks decisional capacity.
**Breakdown:**
- Descriptive: family-centred decision-making is normal (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Normative: cultural normality does not create legal or ethical authority (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Gendered deficit: routing decisions through family transfers a woman's decision to others (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Substituted judgment: only for lack of capacity (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2059, ETH-2063, ETH-2058.
**Trap:** "The family consented" is not sufficient for a competent adult, in law or ethics.
**Mains hook:** Name the structural point: an apparently neutral practice systematically transfers decisions. Qualify that family support is often genuine.
**Back:** *"Family supports the competent woman; it does not decide for her."*

### Flashcard ETH-2063 — The 2026 GS-IV Q4(a) doctor case: five-step analysis template
**Front:** Using the five-step template, how should an answer analyse the doctor's action?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Identify the motive: beneficent, and possibly clinically correct. (2) Identify the act: persuasion routed to the family rather than to the competent adult. (3) Apply Dworkin's three conditions: all satisfied, so paternalistic. (4) Classify: hard if she is competent and informed, soft only if her preference was non-voluntary. (5) Verdict: beneficent motive, autonomy-bypassing method, defective consent.
**Breakdown:**
- The directive is "Explain the concepts of paternalism and beneficence by analysing the doctor's action" (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- Opening by condemning the doctor misreads the question (✅ owner 08 §12B).
- The doctor's remedy for an uninformed preference is better information to her, not persuasion of relatives (✅ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 GS-IV Q4(a) (✅ owner 08 §12B; primary route).
**Links:** ETH-2053, ETH-2054, ETH-2057, ETH-2058, ETH-2064, ETH-2062.
**Trap:** Declaring the doctor's outcome good and so the consent valid. The defect is procedural and relational.
**Mains hook:** A good medical outcome does not retrospectively validate a defective consent.
**Back:** *"Motive kind, method wrong: paternalism by relatives, not medicine."*

### Flashcard ETH-2064 — The ethical alternative for the doctor
**Front:** What should a clinician do instead, when she believes a surgery is medically better?
**Definition (learn):** Disclose directly and comprehensibly to the patient; offer a decision aid in her language and time to decide; offer a second opinion; involve family at her invitation in a supporting role; document consent and alternatives; and revisit where reluctance persists.
**Breakdown:**
- Direct disclosure: diagnosis, surgery, risks, benefits, recovery, medication and its adverse effects, doing neither, uncertainties (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Decision aid and time (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Second opinion offered, not resisted (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Family present at her invitation (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Documented consent; reluctance recorded (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
- Institutional safeguards: consent forms naming alternatives; clinical-ethics committee (⚠️ owner 08 §12B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2063, ETH-2059, ETH-2060.
**Trap:** Treating an audit of consent-form presence as an audit of consent quality.
**Mains hook:** Direct, documented, time-bound disclosure respects the patient and still gives the clinician a voice.
**Back:** *"Tell her, give her time, put her in charge, write it down."*

### Flashcard ETH-2065 — The deontic and axiological map: obligatory, permissible, supererogatory, forbidden
**Front:** Map the four categories of obligation and their reactions to performance and omission.
**Definition (learn):** Obligatory acts are required and their omission is blamed. Permissible acts are neutral. Supererogatory acts are optional but good: performance earns praise, omission earns no blame. Forbidden acts are prohibited.
**Breakdown:**
- Obligatory: blame on omission (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Permissible: no reaction (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Supererogatory: praise, no blame (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Forbidden: blame for doing it (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Duty is minimal (deontic); the good is open-ended (axiological) (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner map (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Links:** ETH-2023 (Urmson), ETH-2066, ETH-2010.
**Trap:** Treating supererogation as a form of duty. Its absence earns no blame.
**Mains hook:** Use the map to locate a helping act: duty below the threshold, supererogation above it.
**Back:** *"Obligatory blamed if missed; supererogatory praised if done, forgiven if not."*

### Flashcard ETH-2066 — Charity versus duty: the comparison table and the threshold thesis
**Front:** Compare charity and duty on normative status, ground, trigger, limit and relation to justice.
**Definition (learn):** Charity is supererogatory: praiseworthy, optional, grounded in the giver's virtue. Duty is obligatory: grounded in the recipient's need plus the giver's low cost, with a comparable-importance or low-cost threshold. The boundary is set by the ratio of harm prevented to cost, not by the type of act.
**Breakdown:**
- Status: optional (charity) versus obligatory (duty) (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Reaction to omission: no blame versus blame (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Ground: giver's virtue versus recipient's need (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Limit: open-ended versus threshold (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Relation to justice: outside versus bordering (Pogge would put much of it inside) (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner table (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Links:** ETH-2021 (Singer), ETH-2022, ETH-2065, ETH-2067, ETH-2075 (trap).
**Trap:** Answering "charity is good and duty is good". The question asks for a distinction and a justification.
**Mains hook:** Draw the threshold line by the cost-to-harm ratio, then state where supererogation survives.
**Back:** *"Charity asks, duty owes; the cost-to-harm ratio draws the line."*

### Flashcard ETH-2067 — Objection: demandingness, and the reply
**Front:** What is the demandingness objection to Singer, and how does the answer reply?
**Definition (learn):** Singer's strong principle seems to require giving until further giving costs something of comparable moral importance, leaving no space for personal projects, relationships and integrity (Susan Wolf, "Moral Saints", 1982; Bernard Williams on integrity).
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: strong principle is too demanding (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
- Reply 1: the question's moderate, low-cost qualifier disarms it (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Reply 2: Kant's latitude in imperfect duties preserves personal projects (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wolf 1982 and Williams (⚠️ owner citation, not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2021, ETH-2022, ETH-2010, ETH-2068.
**Trap:** Defending the strong principle against demandingness, when the paper gave the moderate version.
**Mains hook:** Concede the demandingness worry for the strong principle, then show the moderate duty remains.
**Back:** *"The paper set the modest claim; the demandingness objection does not reach it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2068 — Objections: special obligations and "this is the government's job"
**Front:** State the special-obligations and government-responsibility objections to individual duty, and their replies.
**Definition (learn):** Special obligations: we owe more to our children, parents and community, so impartial aggregation misdescribes moral life. Government duty: poverty relief is institutional; individual giving is inefficient and unaccountable.
**Breakdown:**
- Special obligations reply: priority within the resources they actually require, not indifference with the surplus (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Government reply (Singer): institutional failure does not extinguish individual duty (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Counter-concession: sustained individual giving can entrench institutional failure; the duty includes supporting reform (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2021, ETH-2066, ETH-2069, ETH-2070.
**Trap:** Ignoring the concession that individual giving may entrench institutional failure.
**Mains hook:** Individual duty and institutional reform are complementary; name both.
**Back:** *"Your children come first in the resources they need; institutions failing does not free you."*

### Flashcard ETH-2069 — Pogge's negative duty objection and the compensatory reply
**Front:** What is Pogge's objection, and how does the answer reply?
**Definition (learn):** Thomas Pogge argues that the affluent owe primarily a negative duty not to harm, meaning not to impose or benefit from an unjust institutional order. Reply: if the affluent are implicated, assistance is compensatory justice, and calling it charity is a category error.
**Breakdown:**
- Negative duty is stronger and more widely accepted (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Reply strengthens the claim (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
- Pogge would place much of duty inside justice (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2066, ETH-2068, ETH-2070.
**Trap:** Presenting negative duty as an alternative that makes positive duty unnecessary. The answer should use it to strengthen duty.
**Mains hook:** If the affluent benefit from an unjust order, aid is repayment, not charity.
**Back:** *"If you profit from the harm, the help is repayment, not generosity."*

### Flashcard ETH-2070 — Effectiveness objection: aid can distort or create dependency
**Front:** What is the effectiveness objection to duty, and how is it reconciled?
**Definition (learn):** Aid can be ineffective, distorting or dependency-creating. The reply: this constrains how the duty is discharged (effectiveness, recipient ownership, accountability), not whether it exists.
**Breakdown:**
- Objection: ineffectiveness and dependency (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Reply: a constraint on how, not whether (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Link: aid conditionality and ownership analysis (✅ owner 08 §12C referencing Ethics 12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2066, ETH-2068, ETH-2069.
**Trap:** Using effectiveness to abolish duty.
**Mains hook:** Discharge the duty through accountable, recipient-owned aid.
**Back:** *"Effectiveness shapes how you help, not whether you owe it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2071 — Dāna, tyāga and niṣkāma karma in the Indian register
**Front:** What do dāna, tyāga and niṣkāma karma contribute to the duty-charity argument?
**Definition (learn):** Dāna is graded by the manner of giving: right time, right person, without expectation of return, and without humiliating the recipient. Tyāga (renunciation of the fruits of action) and niṣkāma karma frame giving as discharge of duty rather than benevolent surplus.
**Breakdown:**
- Dāna's manner preserves recipient dignity (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
- Tyāga and niṣkāma karma: duty without attachment to fruits (⚠️ owner 08 §12C; Bhagavad Gita).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2072, ETH-2066, ETH-2064.
**Trap:** Treating niṣkāma karma as indifference to outcomes. It is detachment from the fruit, not from the duty.
**Mains hook:** The manner of giving determines whether dignity survives the transfer.
**Back:** *"Give rightly, to the right one, without humiliation: the manner is the duty."*

### Flashcard ETH-2072 — Gandhian trusteeship and Antyodaya as Indian bridges to duty
**Front:** How does Gandhi's trusteeship and the Antyodaya rule support the duty thesis?
**Definition (learn):** Trusteeship holds surplus in trust, so transferring it discharges a fiduciary duty, not generosity. Antyodaya's rule: the claim of the last person is the first claim.
**Breakdown:**
- Trusteeship: duty by a different route from Singer (✅ owner 08 §12C; cross-link Ethics 06 §10B).
- Antyodaya: allocation rule (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner cross-link (✅ owner 08; Ethics 06 not in this fragment).
**Links:** ETH-2071, ETH-2021, ETH-2042.
**Trap:** Presenting trusteeship as voluntary philanthropy.
**Mains hook:** Trusteeship and Singer reach the same conclusion from different routes.
**Back:** *"Surplus held in trust is a debt to be paid, not a gift to be praised."*

### Flashcard ETH-2073 — Article 51A(e) and 51A(j): duties not legally enforced but not optional
**Front:** What do Articles 51A(e) and 51A(j) express for the duty argument?
**Definition (learn):** Article 51A(e) asks citizens to promote harmony and the spirit of common brotherhood. Article 51A(j) asks them to strive towards excellence in individual and collective activity. They express duties that are not legally enforced but are not optional.
**Breakdown:**
- Fundamental duties (⚠️ owner 08 §12C).
- Duty without coercion: an imperfect duty can be real and non-enforceable (✅ owner 08 §12C trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Constitutional text per owner (⚠️ not fetched here).
**Links:** ETH-2010, ETH-2072, ETH-2074.
**Trap:** Assuming duty entails coercion.
**Mains hook:** The Constitution names duties that citizens are not forced to perform by sanction; their moral force is still real.
**Back:** *"Harmony and excellence: duty without a jailer."*

### Flashcard ETH-2074 — CSR under Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013: charity moving to duty
**Front:** How does CSR under Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013 illustrate the boundary moving from charity to duty?
**Definition (learn):** CSR converted a discretionary corporate practice into a codified obligation for qualifying companies, relocating an activity from the charity column to the duty column.
**Breakdown:**
- Section 135, Companies Act, 2013 (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Illustrates the boundary shifting, not proof that Singer is right (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Section 135, Companies Act 2013 (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Links:** ETH-2066, ETH-2073, ETH-2072.
**Trap:** Using CSR as proof of a moral thesis. It is an institutional illustration.
**Mains hook:** Law can move an activity across the charity-duty line.
**Back:** *"What was charity became codified: the line moves."*

### Flashcard ETH-2075 — Trap: "both charity and duty are good" and "abolishing supererogation"
**Front:** What are the two traps in duty-versus-charity answers?
**Definition (learn):** (1) The both-sides answer: the question asks for a distinction and a justification. (2) Abolishing supererogation: the thesis is that some charity is duty, not that the heroic category disappears.
**Breakdown:**
- Both-sides earns nothing (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Some charity is duty; heroic acts stay praiseworthy (✅ owner 08 §12C).
- Legal duty and moral duty are different; the claim is moral (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner traps (✅ owner 08 §12C).
**Links:** ETH-2023, ETH-2066, ETH-2076.
**Trap:** Both traps can be avoided by stating the threshold.
**Mains hook:** State the distinction, the threshold, and the moderate claim.
**Back:** *"Distinguish, justify, keep the hero."*

### Flashcard ETH-2076 — Directive decoding: Examine, Differentiate and Justify
**Front:** What structural move does each of the three theory-directive words require?
**Definition (learn):** Examine (critically examine) requires multi-theory triangulation with a verdict. Differentiate requires two concepts kept precisely apart. Justify requires a defended, not merely asserted, conclusion.
**Breakdown:**
- Examine: dilemma, deontology, consequentialism, virtue, convergence, verdict (✅ owner 08 §14).
- Differentiate: define both, contrast, one example each (✅ owner 08 §14).
- Justify: position, strongest supporting theory, strongest objection, restated conclusion (✅ owner 08 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 §14).
**Links:** ETH-2043, ETH-2044, ETH-2066.
**Trap:** Answering "Justify" with a both-sides paragraph.
**Mains hook:** Match the structure to the verb before writing.
**Back:** *"Examine triangulates, Differentiate separates, Justify defends."*

### Flashcard ETH-2077 — The trolley problem: where utilitarianism and deontology genuinely diverge (advanced)
**Front:** Why are trolley-type thought experiments the standard illustration of divergence, and where do they transfer?
**Definition (learn):** The trolley class shows utilitarianism permitting an act (redirecting harm to save more people) that deontological side-constraints would forbid. It is the canonical case of genuine theoretical divergence.
**Breakdown:**
- Utilitarian: more lives saved (✅ owner 08 advanced §2).
- Deontological: side-constraint against using a person (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §2).
- Transfer: triage and priority-list dilemmas (✅ owner 08 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 advanced §2, §7).
**Links:** ETH-2046, ETH-2078, ETH-2035, ETH-2036.
**Trap:** Thinking the trolley case is a dispute about trams. It is about whether means can be used against persons.
**Mains hook:** Use the divergence to show why a triage answer needs a deontological floor.
**Back:** *"Save more by redirecting harm? The side-constraint says no; the scale says yes."*

### Flashcard ETH-2078 — Agent-relative versus agent-neutral reasons (advanced)
**Front:** Distinguish agent-relative from agent-neutral reasons, and say which theory gives which.
**Definition (learn):** Agent-relative reasons: I must not personally violate this duty, even if my violation would prevent a worse outcome elsewhere (typical of deontology). Agent-neutral reasons: what matters is the best outcome, regardless of who brings it about (typical of consequentialism).
**Breakdown:**
- Deontology: agent-relative (✅ owner 08 advanced §2).
- Consequentialism: agent-neutral (✅ owner 08 advanced §2).
- Use: explains why a public servant cannot violate a duty for an outcome (⚠️ analysis).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 advanced §2).
**Links:** ETH-2077, ETH-2035, ETH-2036.
**Trap:** Assuming deontology denies that outcomes matter. It makes the agent's own violation the decisive question.
**Mains hook:** Use the distinction to explain why an official cannot falsify a record even when the record would have helped.
**Back:** *"Deontology says: not by my hand. Consequentialism says: what happens."*

### Flashcard ETH-2079 — Rule utilitarianism as the practical compromise in administration (advanced)
**Front:** Why does rule utilitarianism suit administrative policy, and what is its limit?
**Definition (learn):** Judge a rule by the aggregate long-run consequences of its general acceptance, then expect individual acts to follow it. This preserves predictability, trust and non-arbitrariness better than unrestricted act-level discretion. Its limit: it does not justify mechanical rule-worship.
**Breakdown:**
- Predictability and trust (✅ owner 08 advanced §3).
- Authorised, reasoned exceptions when literal compliance defeats the rule's purpose (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2014, ETH-2081, ETH-2082.
**Trap:** Equating rule utilitarianism with rule worship.
**Mains hook:** Policy as rule: stable, reviewable, with reasoned exceptions.
**Back:** *"Rules for predictability, with a reasoned exit when the rule defeats its own purpose."*

### Flashcard ETH-2080 — Role-sensitive virtue ethics and the svadharma analogy (advanced)
**Front:** How does phronesis become role-sensitive, and what is the Indian analogue with its limit?
**Definition (learn):** Practical wisdom for public office differs by role: a district collector, police officer and judge carry different responsibilities and public trust. Svadharma (role-appropriate duty) is a useful but limited analogy; the traditions are not theoretically identical.
**Breakdown:**
- Role-specific phronesis (✅ owner 08 advanced §3).
- Svadharma as analogy (⚠️ owner 08 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2007, ETH-2037, ETH-2071.
**Trap:** Treating svadharma and phronesis as identical.
**Mains hook:** The virtue required depends on the office's public trust.
**Back:** *"The role shapes the virtue; svadharma is a useful cousin, not a twin."*

### Flashcard ETH-2081 — Trap: rule utilitarianism and rule-based deontology are the same
**Front:** Are rule utilitarianism and rule-based deontology the same thing?
**Definition (learn):** No. Rule utilitarianism justifies the rule by its consequences. Deontology justifies the rule by its inherent rightness or duty, independent of consequences. They can converge in practice but diverge in edge cases.
**Breakdown:**
- Justification source differs (✅ owner 08 advanced §8; ✅ owner 07 advanced §8).
- Convergence: both favour predictable rules (✅ owner 08 advanced §8).
- Divergence: edge cases (✅ owner 08 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 08 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2014, ETH-2079, ETH-2035.
**Trap:** Identifying the two as the same merges two justifications.
**Mains hook:** Name the justification, not just the rule.
**Back:** *"Same rule, different reason: consequence or duty."*

### Flashcard ETH-2082 — Trap: a 20-mark case study is not a theory-triangulation format
**Front:** Does theory triangulation give a 20-mark case-study format?
**Definition (learn):** No. Every GS-IV Section-B case study must follow the full eight-element architecture in Ethics 22, Sec. 1. Triangulation feeds only the ethical-evaluation element.
**Breakdown:**
- Eight elements: facts, stakeholders, constraints, at least three options, ethical evaluation, decision, implementation, residual-risk mitigation (✅ owner 08 §14; owner points to Ethics 22).
- Triangulation feeds Element 5 (✅ owner 08 §14).
- Steelman the strongest objection (✅ owner 08 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 08 §14).
**Links:** ETH-2043, ETH-2076.
**Trap:** Using the three theories as a substitute for the eight-element structure.
**Mains hook:** Put triangulation inside the ethical evaluation of a full case architecture.
**Back:** *"Three theories inside one architecture, not instead of it."*

---

### GROUP — Public Service Values, Status and Ethical Dilemmas

### Flashcard ETH-2083 — Public-service values and ethical dilemmas: the concept map
**Front:** What are the three linked ideas in Ethics 09: values, the Nolan principles, and dilemma anatomy?
**Definition (learn):** Public service values are the standards that make public power legitimate. The Nolan principles state them. Ethical dilemmas arise when two or more legitimate duties or values conflict, so that honouring one compromises another.
**Breakdown:**
- Nolan principles as the standard (✅ owner 09 §1).
- Code of ethics and code of conduct as the translation (✅ owner 09 §1).
- Dilemma anatomy as the diagnostic (⚠️ owner 09 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2nd ARC 4th Report, Ch.2 (✅ owner 09 header).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2092, ETH-2101.
**Trap:** Treating values as slogans. Each must be tested against a case.
**Mains hook:** Use the principles as a checklist and the anatomy as a method.
**Back:** *"Values name the standard; dilemmas test it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2084 — The seven Nolan principles of public life, as cited by the 2nd ARC
**Front:** List the seven Nolan principles in order, with one-word meaning each.
**Definition (learn):** The seven principles, as cited by the 2nd ARC (para 2.2.5): Selflessness, Integrity, Objectivity, Accountability, Openness, Honesty, Leadership.
**Breakdown:**
- 1 Selflessness: decide solely in the public interest.
- 2 Integrity: no obligation that could influence duty.
- 3 Objectivity: appointments, contracts and benefits on merit.
- 4 Accountability: submit to scrutiny.
- 5 Openness: as open as possible.
- 6 Honesty: declare private interests.
- 7 Leadership: promote by example.
(✅ owner 09 §1; names confirmed on fetched Wikipedia "Committee on Standards in Public Life").
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Committee set up October 1994 under PM John Major; first report 1995 (✅ Wikipedia, year only). ARC reproduces the 1995 wording (✅ owner 09 §5). Leadership wording revised November 2021 in the UK text (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ETH-2085 to ETH-2091, ETH-2110 (1995 vs current UK text).
**Trap:** The ARC's wording is the 1995 version; cite the ARC as the ARC.
**Mains hook:** Use the seven as a checklist for any proposed resolution.
**Back:** *"Seven: Self, Integrity, Objectivity, Accountable, Open, Honest, Leader."*

### Flashcard ETH-2085 — Selflessness: decide solely in the public interest
**Front:** What does Nolan's selflessness principle require, and why is it demanding?
**Definition (learn):** Decisions must be taken solely in terms of the public interest. The ARC cites the strong word "solely", so even partial self-interest counts as a violation.
**Breakdown:**
- Self-interest can be financial, reputational, career or ideological (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- "Solely" is stronger than "primarily" (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2098 (status and role), ETH-2041.
**Trap:** Assuming selflessness is only about money.
**Mains hook:** A decision that also benefits the officer's reputation is a selflessness question.
**Back:** *"Solely for the public, not even partly for yourself."*

### Flashcard ETH-2086 — Integrity: a risk-based standard, not proof of bias
**Front:** What does Nolan's integrity principle require, and why does it justify precautionary recusal?
**Definition (learn):** Freedom from financial or other obligations that might influence official duty. It is a risk-based standard: potential influence is enough.
**Breakdown:**
- "Might influence": potential, not proven, bias (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Justifies disclosure and recusal without proven wrongdoing (✅ owner 09 advanced §3, §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2094, ETH-2095.
**Trap:** "No proof of misconduct, so no need to recuse" misreads a risk-based standard.
**Mains hook:** A tender evaluation involving a relative's firm justifies precautionary recusal.
**Back:** *"Might it influence? Then step aside."*

### Flashcard ETH-2087 — Objectivity: a narrow, procedural principle
**Front:** What does Nolan's objectivity principle cover, and what does it not cover?
**Definition (learn):** Appointments, contracts and the awarding of benefits must be decided on merit alone. It is procedural, not a demand for value-neutral policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Covers merit-based selection (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Does not cover value-neutrality in policy (✅ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 09 advanced §2, §8).
**Links:** ETH-2005 (Plato merit), ETH-2084.
**Trap:** Thinking objectivity requires value-neutral policymaking.
**Mains hook:** Merit-based selection protects objectivity; policy still needs values.
**Back:** *"Merit for jobs, contracts and benefits, not neutrality of every policy."*

### Flashcard ETH-2088 — Accountability: submission to scrutiny, externally facing
**Front:** What does Nolan's accountability principle require?
**Definition (learn):** Being answerable to the public and submitting to whatever scrutiny is appropriate to one's office. It is submission-based, not internal self-assessment.
**Breakdown:**
- Externally facing standard (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Distinct from responsibility (see ETH-2143).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-2143, ETH-2144, ETH-2159.
**Trap:** Treating accountability as synonyms of responsibility.
**Mains hook:** Accountability needs a forum that can scrutinise.
**Back:** *"Answerable to the forum, not only to yourself."*

### Flashcard ETH-2089 — Openness: the default reversed, secrecy needs justification
**Front:** What does Nolan's openness principle require, and how does it reverse the default?
**Definition (learn):** Be as open as possible, and restrict information only when the wider public interest clearly demands it. Secrecy must be justified; disclosure is the default.
**Breakdown:**
- Default: disclosure (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Underpins RTI's presumption of disclosure (✅ owner 09 advanced §3; Ethics 15).
- Legitimate exemptions still apply (✅ owner 09 advanced §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2177, ETH-2175.
**Trap:** Treating openness as an absolute duty to disclose everything.
**Mains hook:** Openness is a reversed default, not unlimited disclosure.
**Back:** *"Open unless the public interest clearly needs it closed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2090 — Honesty: declare private interests and resolve conflicts
**Front:** What is the specific focus of Nolan's honesty principle?
**Definition (learn):** Declare private interests and resolve conflicts to protect the public interest. It is narrower than general truthfulness and targets conflict management.
**Breakdown:**
- Declaration of interests (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Resolution of conflicts (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Links all three types of conflict (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** ETH-2094, ETH-2095, ETH-2096, ETH-2097.
**Trap:** Treating honesty as a general truthfulness norm only.
**Mains hook:** The honesty principle is the one that directly governs conflict of interest.
**Back:** *"Declare your interests; resolve the conflict for the public."*

### Flashcard ETH-2091 — Leadership: promoting principles by example
**Front:** What does Nolan's leadership principle require of senior officials?
**Definition (learn):** Promote and support the principles by leadership and example. Senior officials are responsible for modelling standards that shape subordinates and institutional culture.
**Breakdown:**
- Personal example (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Institutional culture (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2083.
**Trap:** Treating leadership as only the top official's job.
**Mains hook:** Senior example shapes subordinate conduct.
**Back:** *"Lead by what you do before you say it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2092 — Code of ethics versus code of conduct (ARC 2.2.6)
**Front:** Distinguish a code of ethics from a code of conduct, and say what makes a conduct code enforceable.
**Definition (learn):** A code of ethics states broad guiding principles of good behaviour and governance. A code of conduct states precise acceptable and unacceptable behaviour. A conduct standard is sanctionable only when the applicable law or service framework adopts it.
**Breakdown:**
- Code of ethics: aspirational (✅ owner 09 §2).
- Code of conduct: precise (✅ owner 09 §2).
- Enforcement depends on the adopting framework (✅ owner 09 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.6 (✅ owner 09 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2033, ETH-2106, ETH-2110, ETH-2093.
**Trap:** Believing that the ARC's definition alone creates a penalty.
**Mains hook:** A code of conduct is only as strong as the rule that enacts it.
**Back:** *"Ethics says the spirit; conduct says the line; law says the penalty."*

### Flashcard ETH-2093 — Ethical dilemma versus legal violation
**Front:** Distinguish an ethical dilemma from a legal violation, with an example of each.
**Definition (learn):** An ethical dilemma is a genuine conflict between two or more legitimate duties or values. A legal violation is breach of a codified rule, independent of any value conflict.
**Breakdown:**
- A dilemma can exist with no law broken, e.g. two lawful options with different ethical costs (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- A violation can occur with no dilemma, e.g. theft (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- A temptation to break a rule for gain is not a dilemma (⚠️ owner 09 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2011 (Kant), ETH-2102 (genuine vs manufactured), ETH-2125.
**Trap:** "Every case is really about whether a law was broken."
**Mains hook:** Name which type the case presents before resolving it.
**Back:** *"Two legitimate duties pull apart: dilemma. A rule broken: violation."*

### Flashcard ETH-2094 — Conflict of interest versus corruption
**Front:** Distinguish conflict of interest from corruption, and state where the ethical failure lies.
**Definition (learn):** A conflict of interest is a situation in which a private interest could reasonably be thought to compromise official judgment. Corruption requires actual abuse of office, under the PC Act an "undue advantage". The ethical failure is non-disclosure or non-recusal, not the existence of the interest.
**Breakdown:**
- Conflict: situation (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Corruption: abuse of office (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Unmanaged conflict is a corruption risk (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PC Act undue advantage (⚠️ owner 09 §2; cross-link Ethics 19).
**Links:** ETH-2095, ETH-2096, ETH-2097, ETH-2090.
**Trap:** Every conflict is corruption, or no conflict is corruption.
**Mains hook:** Manage conflicts before they become corruption.
**Back:** *"A conflict is a situation; corruption is an abuse; disclose the first."*

### Flashcard ETH-2095 — Actual conflict of interest: recusal and reassignment
**Front:** What defines an actual conflict of interest, and what response does it require?
**Definition (learn):** A private interest and official duty are in direct opposition now, with a decision about to be or already taken.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: a procurement officer evaluating a tender in which a sibling's firm bids (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
- Response: mandatory recusal, reassignment, formal declaration on record (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner taxonomy (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2086, ETH-2096, ETH-2097.
**Trap:** Treating an apparent conflict as actual, causing paralysing recusal.
**Mains hook:** Name the type first, then prescribe the response.
**Back:** *"Present, decisive, now: step aside and declare."*

### Flashcard ETH-2096 — Potential conflict of interest: proactive disclosure
**Front:** What defines a potential conflict of interest, and what is the response?
**Definition (learn):** A private interest could come into opposition with official duty in the future, but no specific decision requires action now.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: an officer holds shares in a sector they may later regulate (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
- Response: proactive disclosure at posting, standing declaration reviewed periodically (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2095, ETH-2097.
**Trap:** Ignoring potential conflicts until they become actual.
**Mains hook:** Disclosure at posting is the proactive safeguard.
**Back:** *"Could, not yet: declare early and review."*

### Flashcard ETH-2097 — Apparent conflict of interest: reasonable observer and transparency
**Front:** What is an apparent conflict, and why is transparency rather than recusal the usual response?
**Definition (learn):** No actual private interest exists, but a reasonable observer aware of the facts could suspect compromised judgment.
**Breakdown:**
- Example: a rule-bound approval for a childhood friend where any applicant would get the same outcome (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
- Response: document identical, published criteria; recuse only if confidence would otherwise be seriously undermined (⚠️ owner 09 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2095, ETH-2096, ETH-2058.
**Trap:** Treating every apparent conflict as actual causes paralysing recusal.
**Mains hook:** Use the reasonable-observer test to decide the response.
**Back:** *"Looks wrong, is not: document the rule and keep the record."*

### Flashcard ETH-2098 — Status and role morality
**Front:** Why does public status impose stricter restraint than private preference?
**Definition (learn):** Public status brings asymmetric authority, privileged information and control over public resources. Role morality therefore requires restraint, reasons and public-purpose duties beyond private preference.
**Breakdown:**
- Authority asymmetry (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Privileged information (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Control over public resources (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2085, ETH-2099, ETH-2100.
**Trap:** Treating public office as private privilege.
**Mains hook:** Role raises the bar for restraint and reasons.
**Back:** *"Power, information, resources: the office raises the bar."*

### Flashcard ETH-2099 — Political neutrality: frank advice, then lawful implementation
**Front:** What does political neutrality require of a permanent civil servant?
**Definition (learn):** The civil servant gives frank, evidence-based advice and then implements lawful elected policy without partisan favour. Neutrality is not ignorance of policy values or indifference to constitutional duties.
**Breakdown:**
- Frank advice first (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §2).
- Lawful implementation after the decision (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Recorded professional dissent is allowed (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2098, ETH-2100, ETH-2127.
**Trap:** Neutrality as silence, or as blind obedience.
**Mains hook:** Advise frankly, record dissent, implement lawfully.
**Back:** *"Advise frankly, implement lawfully, record dissent."*

### Flashcard ETH-2100 — Bounded discretion: structured judgment within limits
**Front:** What limits make discretion legitimate, and what turns discretion into arbitrariness?
**Definition (learn):** Discretion is legitimate only within legal purpose, relevant facts, equal treatment, proportionality, recorded reasons and review. Without those, discretion becomes arbitrariness.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal purpose and relevant facts (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Equal treatment and proportionality (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
- Recorded reasons and review (⚠️ owner 09 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2045, ETH-2098, ETH-2099.
**Trap:** Discretion as personal freedom to substitute private morality for public authority.
**Mains hook:** Every discretionary act needs reasons that survive review.
**Back:** *"Discretion is judgment on a leash of purpose, equality and reasons."*

### Flashcard ETH-2101 — The five-step dilemma-diagnosis anatomy
**Front:** What are the five steps of the dilemma-diagnosis anatomy, in order?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Identify competing values or duties. (2) Check for a legal floor. (3) Apply a moral-theory lens. (4) Weigh institutional role and systemic effect. (5) Decide, document and be accountable.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 2 removes illegal options from consideration (⚠️ owner 09 §3).
- Step 3 links to Ethics 08 (⚠️ owner 09 §3).
- Step 5 applies Nolan's accountability (⚠️ owner 09 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2043, ETH-2083, ETH-2102, ETH-2103.
**Trap:** Treating the anatomy as a full 20-mark answer format (see ETH-2082).
**Mains hook:** Diagnose, then feed the diagnosis into the eight-element architecture.
**Back:** *"Name the duties, clear the law, apply the lenses, weigh the role, decide and answer."*

### Flashcard ETH-2102 — Genuine versus manufactured dilemma (advanced)
**Front:** How do you tell a genuine dilemma from a manufactured one?
**Definition (learn):** A genuine dilemma survives after removing self-interest and rule-violation. A manufactured dilemma dissolves once self-interest or rule-violation is named plainly.
**Breakdown:**
- Genuine: two lawful, legitimate, competing priorities (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Manufactured: accepting a contractor's gift framed as "relationship" (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §3).
- Strong answer states which type it faces first (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2093, ETH-2101, ETH-2110.
**Trap:** Presenting bribery as "compassionate flexibility".
**Mains hook:** Name the type, then resolve.
**Back:** *"Remove the self-interest and the rule-break: does a real dilemma remain?"*

### Flashcard ETH-2103 — Nested dilemmas: the 2025 GS-IV Q7 DC Vijay case
**Front:** What three nested dilemmas does the 2025 GS-IV Q7 DC case contain?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Personal grief versus public duty (emotional intelligence). (2) Resource-allocation fairness in disaster relief (distributive justice). (3) Transparency versus maintaining public calm (openness).
**Breakdown:**
- Personal grief (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §3, ETH cross-link to Ethics 05).
- Distributive justice (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §3, ETH cross-link to Ethics 08).
- Openness (✅ owner 09 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q7, Section B, 20 marks (✅ owner 09 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2089, ETH-2046, ETH-2101, ETH-2111.
**Trap:** Treating the case as one monolithic problem.
**Mains hook:** Decompose, resolve each layer, then state the combined verdict.
**Back:** *"Three dilemmas in one case: grief, fairness, openness."*

### Flashcard ETH-2104 — Coercion versus undue influence (2023 GS-IV Q2(b))
**Front:** Differentiate coercion from undue influence, as the 2023 GS-IV Q2(b) question asks.
**Definition (learn):** Coercion compels through an explicit or implicit threat of harm, overborne by fear. Undue influence exploits a position of trust, authority or dependency to bend the target's will without an explicit threat.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: threat versus dependency (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Consent: extracted under duress versus shaped by asymmetry (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Workplace example: adverse report threatened (coercion) versus career dependence pressure (undue influence) (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Detection: incident-based versus pattern-based review (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 GS-IV Q2(b), Differentiate, 10 marks (✅ owner 09 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2058, ETH-2105, ETH-2097.
**Trap:** Labelling ordinary firm instruction as coercion, or missing undue influence disguised as courtesy.
**Mains hook:** Name the mechanism precisely before choosing the remedy.
**Back:** *"Threat overborne by fear; dependence bent without a threat."*

### Flashcard ETH-2105 — Undue influence: pattern-based review and cooling-off
**Front:** What institutional responses suit undue influence, and why is it harder to detect?
**Definition (learn):** Undue influence has no explicit threat, so it surfaces only through pattern-of-conduct review or the reasonable-observer test. Responses: cooling-off periods, rotation of postings, and mandatory recording of reasons for discretionary recommendations.
**Breakdown:**
- Detection: pattern review (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Post-retirement cooling-off (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Rotation (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
- Reasons for recommendations (⚠️ owner 09 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2104, ETH-2097, ETH-2056 (defaults), ETH-2045.
**Trap:** Cooling-off that is too short or too long can fail either way (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §12 unit 3).
**Mains hook:** Structural safeguards are needed because individual integrity cannot detect undue influence alone.
**Back:** *"No threat to catch, so watch the pattern and rotate the dependence."*

### Flashcard ETH-2106 — ARC 2.4.5: monitoring architecture for a code of ethics (advanced)
**Front:** How did the ARC recommend a code of ethics be monitored?
**Definition (learn):** Create a code of ethics in addition to the existing code of conduct for Ministers. Monitor it through dedicated units in the offices of the Prime Minister and Chief Ministers that receive public complaints, and an annual report to the legislature on observance, violations and action taken. Place the code, conduct standards and report in the public domain.
**Breakdown:**
- Dedicated units in PM and CM offices (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
- Public complaints received (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
- Annual report to legislature (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
- Documents in the public domain (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.4.5 (✅ owner 09 advanced §7). ARC 4th Report 2007 (✅ owner 09 header).
**Links:** ETH-2092, ETH-2107, ETH-2110.
**Trap:** Treating the ARC recommendation as an existing institution.
**Mains hook:** Design a monitoring loop with complaints, reports and public access.
**Back:** *"Code, complaint desk, annual report, public: the four legs."*

### Flashcard ETH-2107 — Aspirational principles without sanction: ARC 2.4.3 limitation
**Front:** Why do the Nolan principles risk becoming rhetorical, and what does the ARC say?
**Definition (learn):** The Nolan principles carry no direct sanction. Without a linked code of conduct they risk being rhetoric, and without ethics guidance they risk becoming a list of prohibitions, which the ARC (2.4.3) calls "not comprehensive".
**Breakdown:**
- No direct sanction (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §6).
- ARC 2.4.3 "not comprehensive" and "a list of prohibitions" (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §6).
- Need a linked conduct code (✅ owner 09 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.4.3 (⚠️ owner citation).
**Links:** ETH-2092, ETH-2106.
**Trap:** Treating the principles as enforceable on their own.
**Mains hook:** Principles need codes and sanctions to bite.
**Back:** *"Principles alone are rhetoric; codes give them teeth."*

### Flashcard ETH-2108 — 📰 CPGRAMS monthly report, June 2026: an answerability anchor
**Front:** What does the June 2026 DARPG CPGRAMS report illustrate, and what does it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** The DARPG CPGRAMS Monthly Report for Central Ministries and Departments (June 2026) reports the 48th consecutive month with Central Secretariat disposal above one lakh, 78,793 pending grievances, about 68% pending under 21 days, and 83,544 new registrations (✅ owner 09 advanced §9 as-of June 2026).
**Breakdown:**
- Illustrates answerability and monitoring (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §9).
- Ethical accountability also needs fair reasons, remedy quality, accessibility and non-retaliation (⚠️ owner 09 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As-of June 2026 (owner 09 advanced §9). Figures not fetched (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2088, ETH-2161.
**Trap:** Using disposal counts as proof of ethical governance.
**Mains hook:** Disposal is an output, not an ethical outcome.
**Back:** *"Disposals count the answers; ethics asks whether the answers were fair."*

### Flashcard ETH-2109 — Trap: the 2025 premise is not proof that no Indian body has a code of ethics
**Front:** Does the 2025 GS-IV Q5(a) premise prove that no Indian public body has a code of ethics?
**Definition (learn):** No. The paper gives a problem premise: a Code of Conduct already in operation and a Code of Ethics not yet put in place. Implementation varies by institution and must be verified before a general claim.
**Breakdown:**
- Premise, not a general status claim (✅ owner 09 advanced §8).
- Verify institution by institution (✅ owner 09 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q5(a) (✅ owner 09 advanced §7).
**Links:** ETH-2092, ETH-2106.
**Trap:** Generalising a paper premise into a status claim.
**Mains hook:** Use the premise as framing, then propose a model.
**Back:** *"A premise frames the question; it is not the nationwide register."*

### Flashcard ETH-2110 — Trap: the Nolan wording is the 1995 text, not today's UK wording
**Front:** Which text does the ARC quote for the Nolan principles, and how should a candidate cite it?
**Definition (learn):** The ARC quotes the Committee on Standards in Public Life's 1995 formulation. The UK's official wording has since been revised (Leadership wording updated November 2021, per Wikipedia). Cite the ARC's version as the ARC's.
**Breakdown:**
- ARC text = 1995 (✅ owner 09 advanced §8).
- UK text revised (✅ Wikipedia; 2013 review noted in owner 09 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Committee set up October 1994; ARC citation 2007 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 09).
**Links:** ETH-2084, ETH-2092.
**Trap:** Quoting the current UK text as the ARC's text.
**Mains hook:** Attribute precisely.
**Back:** *"ARC's 1995 text for the ARC; today's UK text for today's UK."*

### Flashcard ETH-2111 — Case-study PYQs routed to the Ethics method (2018–2025)
**Front:** Which GS-IV case-study PYQs route to Ethics 09, and what do they test?
**Definition (learn):** Case studies test stakeholders, dilemmas, options, justification, implementation and safeguards. Routed case studies in this owner include 2018 Q7 (Rakesh, senior-citizen health scheme), 2018 Q9 (prohibition-state SP), 2019 Q11 (frontier narcotics district SP), 2020 Q9 (tribal Rampura DC, child trafficking), 2020 Q12 (migrant workers in lockdown), 2021 Q8 (Vice Principal and cheating), 2021 Q10 (COVID hospital staff deployment), 2023 Q8 (DM and blood transfusion in landslide relief), 2024 Q9 and Q11, 2025 Q7, Q9, Q12 (✅ owner 09 PYQ tables).
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: Section B, 20 marks, 250 words (✅ owner 09).
- Required moves: stakeholders, dilemmas, options, justification, implementation and safeguards (✅ owner 09).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Years and question numbers as in owner 09 PYQ tables (✅ owner 09).
**Links:** ETH-2082, ETH-2101, ETH-2103.
**Trap:** Treating every case as a legal problem.
**Mains hook:** Use the eight-element architecture with the case's dilemma named.
**Back:** *"Twenty marks, eight moves: stakeholders, dilemmas, options, justice, action, safeguards."*

### Flashcard ETH-2112 — Trap: every case study is a legal violation
**Front:** Is every ethical problem in a GS-IV case study really about whether a law was broken?
**Definition (learn):** No. Many cases contain genuine value conflicts where no law is necessarily broken. Others test a clear violation that should not be disguised as a dilemma.
**Breakdown:**
- Genuine value conflicts (✅ owner 09 §6).
- Clear violations (✅ owner 09 §6).
- Use the genuine-versus-manufactured test (ETH-2102).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 09 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2093, ETH-2102.
**Trap:** Reducing every case to legality, or dressing a violation as a dilemma.
**Mains hook:** Name the type, then resolve.
**Back:** *"Some cases are dilemmas; some are violations in a costume."*


---

### GROUP — Sources of Ethical Guidance: Laws, Rules, Conscience

### Flashcard ETH-2113 — Sources of ethical guidance: distinct, interacting checks, not one ladder
**Front:** What are the sources of ethical guidance for a public servant, and how do they relate?
**Definition (learn):** Conscience, professional codes of ethics, codes of conduct and service rules, regulations, statutes, constitutional legality and reasoned institutional process. They are distinct, interacting checks, not one rigid hierarchy.
**Breakdown:**
- Conscience: moral alarm, motivation and reflection (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Codes of ethics: occupation-specific aspirational standards (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Codes of conduct and service rules: precise duties under a legal basis (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Regulations and delegated rules: binding within delegated authority (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Statutes: legislated duties and powers (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Constitutional legality: equality, liberty, due process (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Reasoned process: facts, consultation, recorded reasons, review and remedy (✅ owner 10 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 4th Report 2007, Box 2.3 (✅ owner 10 header).
**Links:** ETH-2114 (Fulbright), ETH-2092 (codes), ETH-2126 (response ladder), ETH-2001.
**Trap:** Treating sources as a rigid ladder where a higher source always wins.
**Mains hook:** Law is a floor; conscience, codes and process fill the rest.
**Back:** *"Conscience, codes, rules, statutes, Constitution, reasons: each checks the others."*

### Flashcard ETH-2114 — Fulbright's warning in ARC Box 2.3: morality is not mere legality
**Front:** What does Senator Fulbright's warning in ARC Box 2.3 say about the gap between law and ethics?
**Definition (learn):** Morality has become identical with legality for many influential people. But "we are certainly in a tragic plight if the accepted standard by which we measure the integrity of a man in public life is that he keep within the letter of the law." Law is a necessary floor, not a sufficient ceiling.
**Breakdown:**
- Box 2.3 of the ARC 4th Report, sourced to the US Senate Ethics Manual (2003) (✅ owner 10 §1).
- Warning: legality is not the test of integrity (✅ owner 10 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Senate Ethics Manual 2003 (✅ owner 10 §1 as cited by ARC).
**Links:** ETH-2115, ETH-2113, ETH-2137.
**Trap:** Quoting the line as a Senate or Fulbright law. It is a quotation in an ARC box.
**Mains hook:** A legal act can still fail the integrity test; that is why codes exist.
**Back:** *"Within the letter of the law is not the same as within integrity."*

### Flashcard ETH-2115 — Fulbright's "guise of friendship": the structural gap between letter and spirit (advanced)
**Front:** Why can the letter-versus-spirit gap not be closed by more detailed rules alone?
**Definition (learn):** Fulbright warns about officials who accept favours "under the guise of friendship", which offend the spirit of the law but do not violate its letter. Any finite rule-set has edge cases exploitable in letter while violating spirit, so the gap closes only through disposition (conscience, habituated integrity) alongside rules.
**Breakdown:**
- Advanced claim: structural gap between legality and ethics (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Remedy: cultivate disposition with rules (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Reinforces the ARC values-plus-institutions synthesis (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fulbright quotation (✅ owner 10 advanced §3, ARC Box 2.3).
**Links:** ETH-2114, ETH-2033 (codes of ethics reach spirit), ETH-2007 (phronesis).
**Trap:** Believing a longer rulebook solves the spirit problem.
**Mains hook:** Pair codes of ethics with rules because rules always leave edge cases.
**Back:** *"The rulebook closes the door; disposition closes the window."*

### Flashcard ETH-2116 — Law, rule and regulation: three binding sources with different authority
**Front:** Distinguish a law, a rule and a regulation.
**Definition (learn):** A law is a binding legal norm whose validity depends on the constitutional and statutory framework. A rule is usually delegated or subordinate legislation made under an enabling law and cannot exceed that law. A regulation is commonly a binding delegated norm issued by a statutory regulator or authorised body within its enabling framework.
**Breakdown:**
- Law: validity depends on the constitutional and statutory framework (✅ owner 10 §2).
- Rule: subordinate legislation; cannot exceed its enabling law's authority (✅ owner 10 §2).
- Regulation: binding delegated norm by a statutory regulator; not a synonym for every office instruction (✅ owner 10 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner definitions (✅ owner 10 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2117 (administrative instruction), ETH-2133 (2016 law vs ethics).
**Trap:** Calling any office circular a regulation.
**Mains hook:** A rule that exceeds its enabling law is unlawful, whatever its moral appeal.
**Back:** *"Law from Parliament, rule under a law, regulation by a regulator."*

### Flashcard ETH-2117 — Administrative instructions: guidance whose force depends on authority
**Front:** Is an office circular or guideline automatically delegated legislation?
**Definition (learn):** No. A circular, guideline or office memorandum may guide administration, but its legal force depends on authority and context.
**Breakdown:**
- Guides administration (⚠️ owner 10 §2).
- Legal force depends on authority (⚠️ owner 10 §2).
- Not automatically delegated legislation (⚠️ owner 10 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2116, ETH-2123.
**Trap:** Treating an office memo as binding law.
**Mains hook:** Ask whether the instruction has legal authority before treating it as binding.
**Back:** *"A memo guides; only authority binds."*

### Flashcard ETH-2118 — Conscience: internal moral judgment that can err
**Front:** What is conscience as a source of guidance, and what are its limits?
**Definition (learn):** Conscience is an individual's internal moral judgment, shaped by upbringing, social experience and reflection. It can identify a wrong not yet codified, but it may also be biased, incomplete or overconfident.
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: flags uncodified wrongs (⚠️ owner 10 §2, §8).
- Limits: bias, incomplete information, overconfidence (⚠️ owner 10 §2).
- Must be tested against facts, constitutional values, role duties and affected citizens (⚠️ owner 10 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2119, ETH-2124, ETH-2130.
**Trap:** Conscience always overrides law (see ETH-2138).
**Mains hook:** Conscience is necessary but must be channelled through institutions.
**Back:** *"Conscience sees first and can be wrong first."*

### Flashcard ETH-2119 — Constitutional morality: Ambedkar, Grote and the Constituent Assembly (advanced)
**Front:** What is constitutional morality, and what is its genealogy in Indian constitutional discourse?
**Definition (learn):** Constitutional morality is a cultivated commitment to constitutional values and procedures (equality, liberty, due process, restraint and reasoned public power), not transient majority sentiment or personal preference. Ambedkar invoked it in the Constituent Assembly on 4 November 1948, quoting George Grote.
**Breakdown:**
- Ambedkar, 4 November 1948 (✅ owner 10 advanced §7; not fetched, so ⚠️).
- Grote's definition: paramount reverence for the forms of the Constitution, obedience to authority acting within them, open speech, action subject to legal control, and unrestrained censure of authorities (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3; not fetched).
- Ambedkar's point: diffusion through society is the indispensable condition of free and peaceable government (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Ambedkar on India: "democracy is only a top-dressing on an Indian soil" (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 November 1948 (✅ owner 10 advanced §7; ⚠️ not fetched; Wikipedia page unavailable).
**Links:** ETH-2120, ETH-2139, ETH-2134.
**Trap:** Believing the phrase was coined in a 2020s Supreme Court judgment (see ETH-2139 and ETH-2120). Its genealogy runs to 1948.
