**Mains hook:** Use constitutional morality to resist both rule-worship and private conscience, anchored in constitutional text.
**Back:** *"Grote gave the words, Ambedkar gave the Assembly, the Constitution gives the test."*

### Flashcard ETH-2120 — Constitutional morality: resisting two opposite failure modes
**Front:** What two opposite failure modes does constitutional morality guard against?
**Definition (learn):** (a) Uncritical deference to whatever the political executive or majority demands. (b) Uncritical deference to personal conscience or preference untethered from constitutional text and values. Constitutional morality anchors dissent in constitutional principle.
**Breakdown:**
- Failure mode (a): majoritarian deference (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Failure mode (b): personal preference dressed as principle (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3, §6).
- Legitimate use needs grounding in specific constitutional text or values (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2119, ETH-2122, ETH-2128.
**Trap:** Invoking constitutional morality loosely for personal preference.
**Mains hook:** Name the constitutional value and the text before invoking the principle.
**Back:** *"Neither the crowd nor my own gut: the Constitution's values, named."*

### Flashcard ETH-2121 — Legal positivism versus natural law (advanced)
**Front:** What is the difference between legal positivism and natural-law theory, and where do they diverge in practice?
**Definition (learn):** Legal positivism holds that a law's validity depends on proper enactment, not its moral content. Natural-law theory holds that an unjust law lacks true legal or moral authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Positivism: "law is law; obey as written" (⚠️ owner 10 advanced architecture).
- Natural law: "an unjust law is not true law" (⚠️ owner 10 advanced architecture).
- Usually converge in ordinary administration (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8).
- Diverge at a law seen as deeply unjust, where conscience becomes salient (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2119, ETH-2140, ETH-2122.
**Trap:** Assuming the two always give opposite practical answers (see ETH-2140).
**Mains hook:** Constitutional morality is the Indian resolution that avoids both extremes.
**Back:** *"Positivism obeys the text; natural law asks if the text is just; the Constitution mediates."*

### Flashcard ETH-2122 — Civil disobedience versus institutional dissent by a public servant (advanced)
**Front:** How does a public servant's dissent differ from a citizen's civil disobedience?
**Definition (learn):** Civil disobedience is public, non-violent, legally accepted defiance of an unjust law by a citizen. Institutional dissent by a public servant operates through recorded objection, recusal, escalation or protected whistleblowing, because the servant's authority derives from, and is accountable within, the institution being questioned.
**Breakdown:**
- Citizen: public defiance with legal consequence (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2).
- Servant: recorded objection and escalation (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2).
- The servant has no general entitlement to refuse lawful duties on conscience grounds (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2121, ETH-2123, ETH-2128.
**Trap:** Treating a public servant's leak as civil disobedience.
**Mains hook:** Position dissent inside the institution, not against it.
**Back:** *"The citizen defies openly; the servant objects on file."*

### Flashcard ETH-2123 — Conscience clauses and recusal norms
**Front:** When may a public official seek recusal or substitution on conscience grounds?
**Definition (learn):** Where an applicable law, service rule or authorised procedure permits it, an official may seek recusal or substitution for a specific conflict. Indian public servants have no presumed general entitlement to refuse any lawful duty on conscience.
**Breakdown:**
- Permitted by rule or procedure (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2).
- No presumed general right to refuse (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §2).
- Recusal is the concrete form for specific conflicts (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2095, ETH-2122, ETH-2126.
**Trap:** Assuming conscience alone entitles refusal.
**Mains hook:** Check the rule that permits the objection before invoking conscience.
**Back:** *"Conscience asks for recusal only where a rule gives the door."*

### Flashcard ETH-2124 — Crisis of conscience: definition and public manifestations (2019 GS-IV Q3(b))
**Front:** What is a crisis of conscience, and how does it manifest in the public domain?
**Definition (learn):** A crisis of conscience arises when an individual's deeply held moral sense comes into acute, felt conflict with an external demand (an instruction, a law, an institutional expectation, or a prior commitment), so that compliance would require acting against what they believe is fundamentally right.
**Breakdown:**
- Manifestations: resignation, public dissent, whistleblowing, conscientious objection, visible distress or paralysis (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Becomes an administrative problem when it affects capacity to discharge the role (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- 2019 GS-IV Q3(b), Explain, 10 marks (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-IV Q3(b) (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2125, ETH-2126, ETH-2118.
**Trap:** Describing a crisis of conscience as a legal breach. It can occur within full legal compliance.
**Mains hook:** Explain the internal conflict, then show how it becomes administrative.
**Back:** *"Compliance feels like betrayal of self: the crisis is inside, then it shows outside."*

### Flashcard ETH-2125 — Crisis of conscience versus ordinary dilemma versus legal violation
**Front:** Distinguish a crisis of conscience from an ordinary ethical dilemma and from a legal violation.
**Definition (learn):** An ordinary dilemma is a conflict of two legitimate duties. A crisis of conscience is the same logical structure, but one side is felt as a near-inviolable personal commitment. A legal violation is an external, objective breach of codified law and can occur without any crisis.
**Breakdown:**
- Dilemma: two legitimate duties (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Crisis: high subjective stakes for moral identity, not a different category (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Legal violation: breach of codified law, assessed objectively (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Crisis within full compliance: an official following a lawful order that violates conviction (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2093, ETH-2124, ETH-2126.
**Trap:** Treating the intensity of feeling as proof of wrongdoing.
**Mains hook:** Separate the feeling from the legal question.
**Back:** *"Same structure, sharper stakes; a crisis can occur inside full legality."*

### Flashcard ETH-2126 — The seven-rung response ladder for a crisis of conscience
**Front:** List the response ladder in ascending order of formality, and state the starting rule.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Private reflection and reasoning check. (2) Recorded written objection or dissent note. (3) Recusal from the specific decision. (4) Escalation to superior, oversight body or judicial review. (5) Protected disclosure or whistleblowing through lawful channels. (6) Conscientious objection to a specific lawful instruction. (7) Resignation, as last resort. Start at the lowest rung that resolves the crisis.
**Breakdown:**
- Rungs 1–2: internal, cheap, recorded (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Rungs 3–5: formal, institutional (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Rungs 6–7: high cost (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner ladder (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
**Links:** ETH-2127, ETH-2128, ETH-2129, ETH-2124.
**Trap:** Jumping to resignation or public disclosure at the first discomfort.
**Mains hook:** Choose the lowest rung that genuinely resolves the crisis.
**Back:** *"Reflect, record, recuse, escalate, disclose, object, resign: climb only as far as needed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2127 — Ladder principle: escalate through, not around, institutional channels
**Front:** What is the governing principle of the response ladder, and what are the costs of its higher rungs?
**Definition (learn):** Conscience justifies escalating through the institutional ladder, not bypassing it. Unilateral defiance at the first discomfort is a failure of institutional discipline; blind compliance at the top of conviction is a failure of ethics. Conscientious objection to a lawful order is not automatically protected, and resignation abandons citizens who depend on the service.
**Breakdown:**
- Escalate through the ladder (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Conscientious objection may attract discipline unless an institutional exception exists (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
- Resignation has ethical weight of its own (⚠️ owner 10 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2126, ETH-2128, ETH-2099, ETH-2047.
**Trap:** Treating conscientious objection or resignation as cost-free.
**Mains hook:** Recommend the lowest effective rung and state why higher rungs cost more.
**Back:** *"Climb through the ladder, not around it; each higher rung costs more."*

### Flashcard ETH-2128 — Lawful dissent channels in ascending order (advanced)
**Front:** Give the five-step sequence of lawful dissent channels for a conscience-driven civil servant.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Internal, recorded written objection or note on file. (2) Recusal or substitution where authorised. (3) Escalation to a superior or oversight body. (4) Disclosure through an applicable authorised or protected mechanism. (5) Resignation, as last resort, if continued participation would require complicity in a serious wrong.
**Breakdown:**
- Legitimacy comes from staying procedurally accountable (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §3).
- Channel coverage must be verified (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §4).
- Compromised channels create a residual dilemma (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2126, ETH-2127, ETH-2131.
**Trap:** Anonymous leaks as a first resort.
**Mains hook:** Build a sequence that a returning officer or auditor could follow.
**Back:** *"Note, recuse, escalate, disclose through protection, resign only last."*

### Flashcard ETH-2129 — Snowden: conscience versus law, with the response ladder failing (2018 GS-IV Q12; 2023 GS-IV Q5(a))
**Front:** Why is the Snowden disclosure the paradigm conscience-versus-law case, and what does it show about the ladder?
**Definition (learn):** Snowden's 2013 disclosure of classified US surveillance programmes was framed as a moral obligation to inform the public, though legally prohibited. He claimed internal channels were unavailable, so the ladder's final rungs (public disclosure, self-exile) were reached.
**Breakdown:**
- Facts: 2013 US surveillance disclosure (✅ owner 08 §12 and 10 §11 framing; ⚠️ not fetched).
- Ladder failure claim (⚠️ owner 10 §11).
- 2018 GS-IV Q12, 20 marks (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
- 2023 GS-IV Q5(a): conscience as a reliable guide (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2013 disclosure (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2047 (full theory triangulation), ETH-2127, ETH-2130.
**Trap:** Treating the case as proof that conscience can override law.
**Mains hook:** The defensible question is proportionality and channel exhaustion, not the mere fact of disclosure.
**Back:** *"Conscience reached the last rung because it said the earlier ones were closed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2130 — Is conscience a reliable guide compared with law and rules? (2023 GS-IV Q5(a))
**Front:** How should an answer assess whether conscience is a reliable ethical guide compared with laws and rules?
**Definition (learn):** Conscience can flag novel wrongs that rules have not anticipated. Its limit is that it is individually calibrated and can be miscalibrated by incomplete information, bias or inflated confidence. Conscience is a necessary supplement to law, most reliable when it triggers institutional escalation rather than unilateral action.
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: responds to novel wrongs (⚠️ owner 10 §11).
- Limit: individual calibration (⚠️ owner 10 §11).
- Position: supplementary, escalated through channels (⚠️ owner 10 §11).
- 2023 GS-IV Q5(a), Discuss, 10 marks (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2118, ETH-2129, ETH-2127.
**Trap:** Answering "conscience is always reliable" or "conscience is never reliable".
**Mains hook:** Conscience is a necessary supplementary guide, reliable inside the ladder.
**Back:** *"Conscience sees the new wrong; the institution tests whether it is right."*

### Flashcard ETH-2131 — Whistleblower protection: verify the channel before advising disclosure
**Front:** What must be verified before advising a whistleblower, and what is PIDPI?
**Definition (learn):** The existence, scope, commencement and amendment of whistleblower protection must be verified from current official sources for the specific channel. PIDPI is an administrative CVC mechanism with defined scope, not a complete statutory substitute or protection for every disclosure.
**Breakdown:**
- Do not assume protection from the label "whistleblower" (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §4, §7).
- PIDPI is an administrative CVC mechanism (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §4).
- Check coverage, confidentiality duties and subject matter (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Time-sensitive; verify in Ethics 19 (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2128, ETH-2168, ETH-2141.
**Trap:** Saying every whistleblower is comprehensively protected.
**Mains hook:** Advise channel-by-channel and state the residual risk honestly.
**Back:** *"Check the channel's coverage before you call it protection."*

### Flashcard ETH-2132 — CVC Circular No. 04/08/25 (Vigilance Awareness Week 2025) — 📰
**Front:** What did the CVC circular dated 1 August 2025 schedule, and what does it not create?
**Definition (learn):** CVC Circular No. 04/08/25, dated 1 August 2025, scheduled Vigilance Awareness Week 2025 from 27 October to 2 November 2025 under "Vigilance: Our Shared Responsibility". It also set a preventive-vigilance campaign from 18 August to 17 November, focused on pending complaints and cases, capacity building, asset management and digital initiatives (✅ owner 10 advanced §9).
**Breakdown:**
- Shows shared institutional responsibility and preventive channels (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §9).
- Does not create statutory whistleblower protection or authorise external disclosure (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 August 2025; 27 October–2 November 2025; 18 August–17 November (✅ owner 10 advanced §9; not fetched). Time-bound 2025 anchor.
**Links:** ETH-2131, ETH-2168, ETH-2165.
**Trap:** Using a vigilance-week circular as a legal protection source.
**Mains hook:** Use it to show preventive channels, then add the statutory gap.
**Back:** *"A campaign is not a statute: preventive shared duty, not whistleblower law."*

### Flashcard ETH-2133 — 2016 GS-IV Q5: how law and ethics control conduct
**Front:** Discuss how law and ethics control human conduct, and show how they differ, as 2016 GS-IV Q5 asks.
**Definition (learn):** Law and ethics are two tools for controlling conduct. Law is state-backed, external, enforced minimum compliance. Ethics is conscience and socially grounded aspiration. They overlap but differ in source, enforcement, minimum standard and examples.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: state versus conscience and society (✅ owner 10 advanced §10).
- Enforcement: sanction versus self and social pressure (✅ owner 10 advanced §10).
- Minimum standard versus aspiration (✅ owner 10 advanced §10).
- Boundary cases: legal-but-unethical and ethical-but-illegal (✅ owner 10 advanced §10).
- 2016 GS-IV Q5(a)-(b) (✅ owner 10 §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2016 GS-IV Q5 (✅ owner 10 §7).
**Links:** ETH-2011, ETH-2114, ETH-2116, ETH-2137.
**Trap:** Treating law and ethics as interchangeable.
**Mains hook:** Show the overlap, then the difference, then two boundary cases.
**Back:** *"Law enforces the floor; ethics aspires above it; both steer conduct."*

### Flashcard ETH-2134 — 2025 GS-IV Q1(b): constitutional morality as cultivated restraint (cross-link)
**Front:** How does the 2025 GS-IV Q1(b) constitutional-morality framing enrich Ethics 10, and whose topic is it?
**Definition (learn):** The 2025 question frames constitutional morality as a product of civic education and adherence to the rule of law. Its primary owners are Topics 13 and 14. In Ethics 10 it enriches the calibration of legality, conscience and public reason.
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional morality as cultivated restraint tied to accountability (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §10).
- Ambedkar and Grote are optional enrichment, not a demand of the question (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §10).
- Primary routing: Topics 13 and 14 (✅ owner 10 §4, §7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q1(b) (✅ owner 10 §7).
**Links:** ETH-2119, ETH-2120, ETH-2093.
**Trap:** Claiming Ethics 10 owns the question.
**Mains hook:** Use civic education and rule of law as the explanation of constitutional morality.
**Back:** *"Constitutional morality is cultivated, not instinctive."*

### Flashcard ETH-2135 — 2024 GS-IV Q4: just and unjust are context-relative; irrational attachment to form
**Front:** What do the 2024 GS-IV Q4(a) and (b) demands ask, and how should the answer be built?
**Definition (learn):** Q4(a) asks whether just and unjust are context-relative. Q4(b) asks about irrational attachment to form that causes injustice. Each is a Section A theory question of 10 marks, 150 words (✅ owner 10 PYQ table; routed owner).
**Breakdown:**
- Build: context, core dimensions, evidence or examples, counterpoint, concise conclusion (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
- Verified demand, paper wording not restated in the owner (⚠️ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 GS-IV Q4 (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2113, ETH-2121, ETH-2137.
**Trap:** Turning a context-relative claim into pure relativism. Judge against stated standards.
**Mains hook:** Context shapes application; the standard that judges it must stay principled.
**Back:** *"Context changes the application; the standard still judges."*

### Flashcard ETH-2136 — 2020 GS-IV Q4(a): laws and rules, and the role of ethics in formulating them
**Front:** Distinguish laws from rules and explain the role of ethics in formulating them, as 2020 GS-IV Q4(a) asks.
**Definition (learn):** The question asks candidates to distinguish laws and rules, then explain how ethics informs their formulation. A rule is subordinate to a law; ethics shapes both through the purpose they serve.
**Breakdown:**
- Distinction: law and subordinate rule (✅ owner 10 §2; ✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
- Role of ethics in formulating: purpose and fairness (⚠️ owner 10 §7).
- 2020 GS-IV Q4(a)-(b), Distinguish-Discuss, 10 + 10 marks (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020 GS-IV Q4 (✅ owner 10 PYQ table).
**Links:** ETH-2116, ETH-2133, ETH-2113.
**Trap:** Treating rules as independent of the ethics that justifies them.
**Mains hook:** Rules are instruments; ethics supplies their purpose.
**Back:** *"Laws and rules are the instruments; ethics gives them their purpose."*

### Flashcard ETH-2137 — Trap: following the law is always sufficient to be ethical
**Front:** Is following the law always sufficient for ethical conduct?
**Definition (learn):** No. ARC and Fulbright explicitly reject this. A technically legal act can still fail an ethical or conscience test.
**Breakdown:**
- Fulbright's warning (ETH-2114).
- Legality, morality and propriety are three separate tests (⚠️ owner 10 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 10 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2114, ETH-2115, ETH-2133.
**Trap:** The trap is the claim that legality equals ethics.
**Mains hook:** Name the legality floor, then test propriety and conscience above it.
**Back:** *"Legal is the floor; ethics is not the same building."*

### Flashcard ETH-2138 — Trap: conscience should always override law and institutional rules
**Front:** Does conscience justify overriding law or institutional rules?
**Definition (learn):** No. Conscience justifies principled dissent through lawful channels: recusal, escalation, protected whistleblowing. It does not justify unilateral rule-breaking, and individual conscience without institutional check risks its own abuse.
**Breakdown:**
- Lawful channels (⚠️ owner 10 §6).
- Unilateral rule-breaking not justified (⚠️ owner 10 §6).
- Institutional checks guard against conscience-abuse (⚠️ owner 10 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 10 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2126, ETH-2127, ETH-2129.
**Trap:** Presenting a leak or defiance as the conscience answer.
**Mains hook:** Conscience acts through the ladder.
**Back:** *"Conscience speaks through the ladder, not over it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2139 — Trap: constitutional morality means following popular opinion on constitutional values
**Front:** Does constitutional morality mean following popular opinion about constitutional values?
**Definition (learn):** No. It means restraint of power and adherence to constitutional principles even against transient popular or majoritarian sentiment.
**Breakdown:**
- Restraint of power (✅ owner 10 §6).
- Principles even against majority sentiment (✅ owner 10 §6).
- Not a licence for personal preference (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 10 §6). Also flagged in ETH-2119: the term is not a 2020s coinage (owner 10 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2119, ETH-2120.
**Trap:** Equating constitutional morality with majority opinion. The owner's advanced trap also states the term is not a 2020s coinage.
**Mains hook:** Constitutional morality is the check on majoritarianism.
**Back:** *"Against the crowd when the Constitution says so."*

### Flashcard ETH-2140 — Trap: legal positivism and natural law always give different practical conclusions
**Front:** Do legal positivism and natural law always produce different practical conclusions?
**Definition (learn):** No. In most ordinary administrative situations they converge (follow valid law). They diverge only at a deeply unjust law, where conscience-based dissent becomes salient.
**Breakdown:**
- Convergence in ordinary cases (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8).
- Divergence at deep injustice (⚠️ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2121, ETH-2122.
**Trap:** Treating every law as an unjust-law case.
**Mains hook:** Divergence is the edge case that needs the ladder.
**Back:** *"They agree on most laws and part only at the unjust one."*

### Flashcard ETH-2141 — Trap: every whistleblower act receives comprehensive legal protection
**Front:** Does every act labelled whistleblowing receive comprehensive legal protection?
**Definition (learn):** No. Protection depends on the applicable law, channel, subject matter, confidentiality duties and institutional scope. Verify these before advising disclosure.
**Breakdown:**
- Law, channel, subject matter, confidentiality, scope (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
- Verify before advising (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 10 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2131, ETH-2128.
**Trap:** Inferring protection from the label.
**Mains hook:** State the residual risk honestly.
**Back:** *"The label is not the law; check the channel."*

### Flashcard ETH-2142 — Mains framework: calibrating a conflict of sources
**Front:** What is the step-by-step sources-calibration structure for a Mains answer?
**Definition (learn):** (1) Identify the sources in play and where they conflict. (2) Apply the legality floor. (3) Test consistency with constitutional values. (4) Route conscience through the institutional sequence. (5) Verify protection and state residual risk.
**Breakdown:**
- Legality floor first (✅ owner 10 advanced §11).
- Constitutional test next (✅ owner 10 advanced §11).
- Institutional route, not defiance (✅ owner 10 advanced §11).
- Residual risk stated (✅ owner 10 advanced §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 10 advanced §11).
**Links:** ETH-2113, ETH-2126, ETH-2128, ETH-2131.
**Trap:** Skipping the legality floor and arguing from conscience first.
**Mains hook:** Floor, constitution, channel, protection, residual risk.
**Back:** *"Floor, constitution, channel, protection, residual risk."*

---

### GROUP — Accountability and Ethical Governance

### Flashcard ETH-2143 — Responsibility versus accountability
**Front:** Distinguish responsibility from accountability.
**Definition (learn):** Responsibility is the assigned duty or obligation attached to a role, existing whether or not anyone checks it. Accountability is an identifiable actor's obligation to explain and justify conduct to a forum that can judge, correct, remedy or sanction.
**Breakdown:**
- Responsibility: "what must I do?" (⚠️ owner 11 §1).
- Accountability: "who will judge and correct what I did?" (⚠️ owner 11 §1).
- Ex ante through standards and reporting; ex post through review and sanction (⚠️ owner 11 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2144, ETH-2088, ETH-2178.
**Trap:** Treating them as synonyms (see ETH-2178).
**Mains hook:** Responsibility is assigned; accountability is answerability.
**Back:** *"Responsibility is the job; accountability is answering for the job."*

### Flashcard ETH-2144 — Accountability: the named actor, forum, standard, explanation, judgment and correction
**Front:** What analytical elements does accountability require?
**Definition (learn):** A named actor, a forum entitled to question, a prior standard, an explanation of reasons, a judgment, and a possible correction, remedy or consequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Accountability as "submit to whatever scrutiny is appropriate to their office" (ARC 2.2.5; ✅ owner 11 §2).
- Each element can fail separately (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 2.2.5 (✅ owner 11 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2088, ETH-2145, ETH-2143.
**Trap:** Collapsing accountability into punishment. A sanction without reasons or remedy is still unaccountable (⚠️ owner 11 §2A trap).
**Mains hook:** Check each element before concluding a system is accountable.
**Back:** *"Who, before whom, by what standard, why, judged, and corrected."*

### Flashcard ETH-2145 — The six-element accountability test, applied to a non-human agent
**Front:** Run the six-element test on a generative AI tool. What survives?
**Definition (learn):** A model fails the test on actor, forum, explanation, judgment and correction. It only partly supplies the standard: it can be evaluated against a standard but cannot undertake to meet one.
**Breakdown:**
- Named actor: no (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Forum: no (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Standard: partly (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Explanation: no; fluent output is not reason-giving (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Judgment: no; attaches only to the human who adopted the output (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Correction or consequence: no (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2144, ETH-2146, ETH-2148.
**Trap:** Thinking accountability can be transferred to a tool. It can only be retained, diffused or concealed.
**Mains hook:** Inserting AI creates the appearance of a second accountable party, not a real one.
**Back:** *"A tool cannot be summoned, cannot explain, cannot be sanctioned: accountability stays with the human who adopted it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2146 — Delegable task versus non-delegable duty, and the "would anything be left" test
**Front:** How does one tell a delegable task from a non-delegable duty?
**Definition (learn):** A delegable task is a step whose quality the office-holder can verify before adoption (language editing, formatting, reference-checking). A non-delegable duty is one whose whole content is the office-holder's personal judgment. The test: if the tool's output were removed, would anything remain that the office was appointed to contribute? If not, the duty was non-delegable and has been abandoned.
**Breakdown:**
- Delegable: proofreading, translation, first-pass screening, summarising a document the officer has also read (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Non-delegable: evaluative assessment, disciplinary finding, sanctioning order, bail or clearance recommendation, performance appraisal (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- The test (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2145, ETH-2151, ETH-2147.
**Trap:** Assuming polishing a machine's text is merely editing.
**Mains hook:** Ask what the office was created to contribute.
**Back:** *"Remove the machine: is any judgment left that was your job?"*

### Flashcard ETH-2147 — "With some modifications" does not cure the defect; automation bias
**Front:** Why does editing an AI output not make a civil servant or examiner its author?
**Definition (learn):** Editing an output one did not generate converts the office-holder into a reviewer of the machine, while the office required an originator of judgment. Superficial modification produces automation bias: the human anchors on the machine's framing, and the check becomes ratification.
**Breakdown:**
- Review, not judgment (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Automation bias (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Trap: "he modified it, so he owns it" (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2146, ETH-2150, ETH-2152.
**Trap:** Treating modification as authorship.
**Mains hook:** The modification is cosmetic if the reasoning was never the office-holder's.
**Back:** *"Changing the machine's words does not make the reasoning yours."*

### Flashcard ETH-2148 — Responsibility gap and the problem of many hands
**Front:** What are the responsibility gap and the problem of many hands, in AI-assisted decisions?
**Definition (learn):** A responsibility gap: harm occurs, but no actor both made the decision and can be held to answer. The problem of many hands: designers, procurers, deployers and adopters each contribute too little to ground blame, while the aggregate harm is real.
**Breakdown:**
- Developer, deployer and model point at each other (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Each contribution looks too small (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2145, ETH-2149, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** Thinking a diffuse chain has no one to blame; the institution must name the adopter.
**Mains hook:** Assign named responsibility at each stage.
**Back:** *"Everyone touched it, so no one answers, unless the chain is named."*

### Flashcard ETH-2149 — Moral crumple zone
**Front:** What is a moral crumple zone?
**Definition (learn):** The least powerful human in the loop, such as a junior assistant or data-entry clerk, absorbs blame for a failure structurally produced by the automated system and by the senior officer who adopted it.
**Breakdown:**
- Least powerful absorbs the blame (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Structural cause sits above (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2148, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** Blaming the junior operator for a senior adoption decision.
**Mains hook:** Blame follows the decision's authority, not the keyboard.
**Back:** *"The junior takes the bruise; the adopter owns the decision."*

### Flashcard ETH-2150 — Misrepresentation and unexplainable explanation: an integrity breach
**Front:** Why is submitting machine-generated assessment as one's own an integrity breach, even if the output is correct?
**Definition (learn):** Presenting machine output as one's own scholarly or official judgment is a false implicit claim about authorship of reasoning. It breaches integrity, not productivity, and survives even if the output is substantively correct. Related: the officer cannot answer "why did you conclude this?" because the reasons were never theirs.
**Breakdown:**
- Misrepresentation of authorship (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Unexplainable explanation (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Correct output does not cure it (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2147, ETH-2151, ETH-2144.
**Trap:** Judging by output quality alone.
**Mains hook:** Two distinct wrongs: non-delegable judgment and misrepresentation.
**Back:** *"Correct words, false authorship: an integrity breach."*

### Flashcard ETH-2151 — Permissibility matrix for AI assistance in a duty-bearing role
**Front:** Which uses of AI are permissible in an evaluative or decisional role, and what test governs each?
**Definition (learn):** Permissible with verification: language polishing of one's own assessment (with disclosure where required); similarity screening or evidence retrieval flagged to the institution with a human final call; summarising a long record the officer has read. Not permissible: AI drafts the substantive evaluation and the human modifies and signs; AI output adopted for a decision affecting rights with no disclosure and no reasoning on record.
**Breakdown:**
- Governing test: judgment remains human; output verifiable line by line (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Impermissible: non-delegated judgment and no explanation (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2146, ETH-2150, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** Blanket technophobia; the line is use-specific.
**Mains hook:** Verifiable assistance yes; substitution of the assigned judgment no.
**Back:** *"Assist the verification; never substitute the verdict."*

### Flashcard ETH-2152 — Objections and replies: scarcity of time, and "the output was checked"
**Front:** How does a Mains answer reply to the objections "time scarcity" and "the output was checked"?
**Definition (learn):** Scarcity: the pressure is real, and the institutional answer is workload reform (caps, deadlines, honoraria, right to decline). Silent workarounds conceal the deficit. "Checked": accountability requires the reasons that actually operated; a check shows absence of detected error, not authorship of judgment, and under automation bias it is weaker.
**Breakdown:**
- Declining is the honest response to overload (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- A check does not establish authorship (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2147, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** Treating workload pressure as licence for undisclosed assistance.
**Mains hook:** Decline or seek extension; do not fabricate capacity.
**Back:** *"Overload is declined, not disguised; a check is not authorship."*

### Flashcard ETH-2153 — Objections and replies: "the conclusion is correct" and "tools have always helped"
**Front:** How does a Mains answer reply to "if the conclusion is correct, the process is a formality" and "tools have always assisted"?
**Definition (learn):** In evaluative offices the process is the product, and a verdict whose reasons cannot be defended cannot be corrected on appeal. The real distinction is not novelty but substitutability of judgment: a calculator executes a step the user specified; a generative model supplies the conclusion itself.
**Breakdown:**
- Process is the product in evaluative offices (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Substitutability, not novelty (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2146, ETH-2145, ETH-2151.
**Trap:** Calling any tool an equivalent of a calculator.
**Mains hook:** Ask whether the tool supplies the office's own contribution.
**Back:** *"A calculator does your step; a generative model does your judgment."*

### Flashcard ETH-2154 — Six institutional responses to AI-assisted evaluation
**Front:** List the six measures an institution should put in place for AI-assisted evaluation.
**Definition (learn):** (1) A published AI-use policy naming permitted, restricted and prohibited uses. (2) A mandatory disclosure statement on evaluative outputs. (3) A reasoned-record requirement in the office-holder's own analysis. (4) Capacity reform: caps, realistic timelines and a penalty-free right to decline. (5) Audit and sampling plus a grievance route. (6) Training on automation bias and on delegable versus non-delegable tasks.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutional rather than private-conscience line (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Makes the honest route also the feasible route (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2146, ETH-2151, ETH-2148, ETH-2175.
**Trap:** Publishing a policy without capacity reform.
**Mains hook:** Policy, disclosure, record, capacity, audit, training.
**Back:** *"Policy, disclose, record, relieve, audit, train."*

### Flashcard ETH-2155 — ICMJE authorship: the four criteria and the accountability criterion
**Front:** What are the four ICMJE authorship criteria, and why does the fourth matter for AI?
**Definition (learn):** Substantial contribution; drafting or critical revision; final approval; and agreement to be accountable for all aspects of the work. The fourth criterion is the one a machine cannot satisfy.
**Breakdown:**
- ICMJE Recommendations, "Defining the Role of Authors and Contributors", §4 on AI (✅ owner 11 §2A).
- Authors should not list AI as author (✅ owner 11 §2A).
- Biomedical publishing; analogy only for thesis evaluation (✅ owner 11 §2A caveat).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ICMJE §4 (✅ owner 11 §2A; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2145, ETH-2156.
**Trap:** Presenting an ICMJE rule as binding on examinations.
**Mains hook:** Use the fourth criterion as an analogy for accountability for the whole work.
**Back:** *"Contribute, draft, approve, and answer for all of it: a machine cannot."*

### Flashcard ETH-2156 — UNESCO Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research (2023)
**Front:** What does UNESCO's 2023 guidance say about accountability and disclosure?
**Definition (learn):** Human accountability may not be ceded to the tool; AI use should be disclosed; institutions should publish permitted and prohibited uses.
**Breakdown:**
- Non-cession of human accountability (✅ owner 11 §2A).
- Disclosure of AI use (✅ owner 11 §2A).
- Institutional publication of permitted and prohibited uses (✅ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UNESCO 2023 (✅ owner 11 §2A; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2154, ETH-2155, ETH-2157.
**Trap:** Citing it as a binding national rule.
**Mains hook:** Non-cession of accountability is an international anchor.
**Back:** *"Accountability stays human; disclose the use; publish the rules."*

### Flashcard ETH-2157 — UGC Academic Integrity Regulations 2018: DAIP, IAIP and two cautions
**Front:** What do the UGC (Promotion of Academic Integrity and Prevention of Plagiarism) Regulations, 2018 establish, and what must be checked before quoting them?
**Definition (learn):** The regulations establish a Departmental Academic Integrity Panel (DAIP) and an Institutional Academic Integrity Panel (IAIP), with graded consequences rising with similarity level. They address plagiarism and similarity, not undisclosed generative-AI use as such.
**Breakdown:**
- DAIP and IAIP (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- Tiered similarity slabs: check the gazette text before quoting (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- AI use handled through institutional policy, not a settled national threshold (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018 (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Links:** ETH-2156, ETH-2158, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** Quoting similarity percentages from secondary sources.
**Mains hook:** Use the panels as the institutional route for plagiarism, and institutional policy for AI.
**Back:** *"Two panels for similarity; AI rules sit in institutional policy."*

### Flashcard ETH-2158 — Trap: "UGC AI rules" as a binding national instrument
**Front:** Is there a binding national UGC instrument on generative-AI use?
**Definition (learn):** No binding national UGC instrument on generative-AI use has been verified in this repository. Do not assert one.
**Breakdown:**
- Coaching-material claim is unverified (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
- The 2018 regulations do not define undisclosed AI use as an offence (⚠️ owner 11 §2A).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner caveat (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2157, ETH-2156.
**Trap:** Quoting a non-existent UGC AI rule.
**Mains hook:** State what the rules cover, and what they do not.
**Back:** *"Do not cite a UGC AI rule you have not read."*

### Flashcard ETH-2159 — Internal control: CVC, CVO and CBI
**Front:** What are the internal control bodies, and what does each do?
**Definition (learn):** Departmental vigilance and CVOs are internal administrative controls. The CBI investigates specified offences. The statutory CVC performs vigilance, advisory and superintendence functions but is not itself a prosecuting body.
**Breakdown:**
- CVO: departmental vigilance (✅ owner 11 §2).
- CBI: investigates specified offences (✅ owner 11 §2).
- CVC: advisory and superintendence; not prosecutor (✅ owner 11 §2).
- Powers and locations differ (see Ethics 20, outside this fragment) (✅ owner 11 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 11 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2168, ETH-2169, ETH-2180, ETH-2162.
**Trap:** Treating the CVC as a prosecuting agency (see ETH-2180).
**Mains hook:** Internal control catches early misconduct; its independence is the question.
**Back:** *"CVO in the department, CBI investigates, CVC supervises: none of them is the whole system."*

### Flashcard ETH-2160 — External control: CAG and an independent judiciary
**Front:** What external controls does the ARC Colloquium identify, and what is their weakness?
**Definition (learn):** The CAG and the independent judiciary are mechanisms outside executive control. Their weakness is time-lag: audit exposure and judicial process come after the act.
**Breakdown:**
- CAG audit (✅ owner 11 §2).
- Judiciary (✅ owner 11 §2).
- Time-lag (✅ owner 11 §3, §6 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Colloquium Annexure-I(2) (✅ owner 11 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2167, ETH-2165, ETH-2162.
**Trap:** Believing CAG audit alone ensures ethical use of public funds (see ETH-2179).
**Mains hook:** Independent but late: pair with concurrent control.
**Back:** *"Independent but late: CAG and courts."*

### Flashcard ETH-2161 — Social control: RTI, media, civil society and citizens' charters
**Front:** What is social control, and what are its dependencies?
**Definition (learn):** Social control is accountability by civil society, media and citizens acting where formal mechanisms are weak. The ARC cites Hong Kong's ICAC public-education model. It depends on civil-society capacity and access to information.
**Breakdown:**
- RTI, media, civil society, citizens' charters (✅ owner 11 §1, §3).
- ICAC public-education model (✅ owner 11 §2).
- Depends on literacy and information access (⚠️ owner 11 §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Colloquium Annexure-I(2) (✅ owner 11 §2).
**Links:** ETH-2172, ETH-2170, ETH-2108, ETH-2162.
**Trap:** Treating social control as a substitute for internal or external control.
**Mains hook:** Social control sustains pressure between audit cycles.
**Back:** *"Citizens watch between the audits, if they can see and speak."*

### Flashcard ETH-2162 — The three control layers reinforce each other; none substitutes for another
**Front:** Why must internal, external and social control reinforce each other rather than substitute?
**Definition (learn):** Each control layer catches different failure modes: collusion is best exposed externally or socially; administrative lapses are best caught internally. Effective ethical governance needs all three.
**Breakdown:**
- Internal: early misconduct (⚠️ owner 11 §3).
- External: propriety after the fact (⚠️ owner 11 §3).
- Social: pressure between cycles (⚠️ owner 11 §3).
- Complementary, not substitutable (✅ owner 11 §6 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅/⚠️ as tagged).
**Links:** ETH-2159, ETH-2160, ETH-2161, ETH-2182.
**Trap:** Strengthening vigilance as if it replaces CAG or RTI.
**Mains hook:** Name the layer, its failure mode, and the complementary layer.
**Back:** *"Inside catches the lapse, outside checks the propriety, citizens keep watch."*

### Flashcard ETH-2163 — The ARC's five mechanisms of internal-control failure (advanced)
**Front:** Name the five mechanisms of internal-control failure that the ARC Colloquium identifies.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Collusion at multiple levels sharing corruption's gains. (2) Investigating and prosecuting agencies not independent of the executive. (3) Wilful sabotage of investigation even where cases proceed. (4) The final prosecution or departmental-action decision resting with the executive, letting collusive networks protect the accused. (5) Excessive procedural stages: the ARC cites twelve in a typical departmental action.
**Breakdown:**
- Each is independently addressable (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §3).
- Statutory independence, fixed timelines, insulated sanction decision (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five mechanisms; twelve stages (✅ owner 11 advanced §3, §7).
**Links:** ETH-2159, ETH-2168, ETH-2169.
**Trap:** Naming "corruption" generically without the mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Name one mechanism and one specific reform.
**Back:** *"Collude, capture, sabotage, sanction-by-executive, stall: five levers."*

### Flashcard ETH-2164 — Judicial accountability: activist on scams, slow on retail corruption (advanced)
**Front:** Why does PIL-driven judicial activism not solve retail corruption, and what estimates does the owner cite?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court, through PIL and Article 142, is visibly activist on high-profile scams. Retail corruption, which is more common, must go through ordinary trial courts where delay blunts deterrence. Owner-cited Public Affairs Centre estimates (a 2000s-era estimate): roughly 1 in 4 in Chennai, 1 in 8 in Bangalore, 1 in 17 in Pune reported a bribe payment in specified service interactions.
**Breakdown:**
- Scams: PIL and Article 142 (✅ owner 11 advanced §2, §3).
- Retail: trial-court delay (✅ owner 11 advanced §3).
- Article 311 safeguards, as cited by the Santhanam Committee, protracting proceedings (✅ owner 11 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Public Affairs Centre estimates (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2; dated 2000s; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2165, ETH-2181, ETH-2163.
**Trap:** Saying PIL activism has solved corruption. See ETH-2181.
**Mains hook:** Reform trial-court capacity for retail corruption, not only high-profile activism.
**Back:** *"Courts strike at scams; retail corruption needs trial-court capacity."*

### Flashcard ETH-2165 — Four conditions for effective judicial accountability (ARC Colloquium)
**Front:** Name the four conditions for effective judicial accountability, and say where India lags.
**Definition (learn):** Judicial independence, judicial enforcement, free access to the judiciary, and judicial effectiveness (speed and organisational efficiency). India satisfies the first three robustly and lags on the fourth, the chronic case backlog.
**Breakdown:**
- Independence, enforcement, access (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2).
- Effectiveness: backlog (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC Colloquium address (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2).
**Links:** ETH-2164, ETH-2160.
**Trap:** Assuming courts' independence means effectiveness.
**Mains hook:** Backlog is the binding constraint on judicial accountability.
**Back:** *"Independent, enforced, accessible, and still too slow."*

### Flashcard ETH-2166 — Accountability diffusion and over-centralisation (ARC 1.9)
**Front:** What is accountability diffusion, and how does over-centralisation cause it?
**Definition (learn):** The more layers between decision and citizen, the harder it is to pin responsibility on an identifiable individual. ARC 1.9 traces this to over-centralisation, which widens the distance between authority and accountability.
**Breakdown:**
- Layers dilute responsibility (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2).
- Over-centralisation as cause (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2, ARC 1.9 per Ethics 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.9 (⚠️ owner cross-reference to Ethics 01).
**Links:** ETH-2143, ETH-2144, ETH-2148.
**Trap:** Thinking more layers mean more oversight; they can mean less answerability.
**Mains hook:** Decentralise decisions with named officers.
**Back:** *"More layers, less name on the decision."*

### Flashcard ETH-2167 — Concurrent versus ex-post audit, and the Fifth Pay Commission's "patient" argument
**Front:** How do concurrent and ex-post audit differ, and what did the Fifth Pay Commission argue?
**Definition (learn):** Concurrent audit reviews transactions close to real time and supports in-course correction. CAG's independent ex-post audit remains essential for propriety and systemic learning. The Fifth Pay Commission recommended audit "as concurrent as possible" to reduce the time-lag. Its argument: post-mortems are useful, but can only be conducted while the patient is dead.
**Breakdown:**
- Concurrent: real-time, in-course correction (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §2).
- Ex-post: independent propriety and performance (✅ owner 11 §4; advanced §2).
- Fifth Pay Commission quote (✅ owner 11 §5, advanced §3, as cited in the ARC Colloquium annexure).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifth Pay Commission (✅ owner 11 §5; quote as cited by owner).
**Links:** ETH-2160, ETH-2175, ETH-2182.
**Trap:** Treating concurrent audit as a replacement for CAG (see ETH-2182).
**Mains hook:** The two are complementary; concurrent audit reduces the cost of delay.
**Back:** *"Post-mortems come too late; cure the patient while there is time."*

### Flashcard ETH-2168 — The CVC: 1964 resolution, Vineet Narain (1997) and the CVC Act, 2003
**Front:** Trace the statutory history of the Central Vigilance Commission.
**Definition (learn):** The CVC was set up by an executive resolution in 1964 on the Santhanam Committee's recommendation. The Supreme Court's Vineet Narain judgment was decided on 18 December 1997. The CVC Act, 2003 gave statutory status (assent 11 September 2003).
**Breakdown:**
- Resolution: 11 February 1964 (✅ fetched Wikipedia "Central Vigilance Commission").
- Santhanam Committee on Prevention of Corruption (✅ Wikipedia).
- CVC Act 2003 passed in September 2003 (✅ Wikipedia; assent date not on the fetched page).
- *Vineet Narain v. Union of India*, (1998) 1 SCC 226, decided 18 December 1997 (⚠️ owner 05 and 11; not verified).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1964; 1997; September 2003 (assent 11 September 2003 per owner ⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2159, ETH-2169, ETH-2132.
**Trap:** Saying Vineet Narain created the CVC. It was the basis for statutory status.
**Mains hook:** The CVC's independence problem began in 1964 and was addressed in part in 2003.
**Back:** *"1964 resolution, 1997 judgment, 2003 statute."*

### Flashcard ETH-2169 — Section 6A DSPE Act struck down (2014) and Section 17A PC Act (2018)
**Front:** What happened to Section 6A of the DSPE Act, and which rank-neutral approval rule replaced it?
**Definition (learn):** The CVC Act, 2003 inserted Section 6A into the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946, requiring prior central approval to investigate officers of Joint Secretary rank and above. A Constitution Bench struck it down as violating Article 14 in *Dr. Subramanian Swamy v. Director, CBI* (2014). The approval requirement now operative is Section 17A of the PC Act, inserted in 2018, which is rank-neutral.
**Breakdown:**
- Section 6A: rank-based single directive (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §4).
- Struck down, Article 14 (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §4).
- Judgment decided 6 May 2014 (✅ fetched Wikipedia "Central Vigilance Commission" citing the 6 May 2014 date).
- Section 17A PC Act, 2018 (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 May 2014; (2014) 8 SCC 682 (owner citation, ⚠️ not verified).
**Links:** ETH-2168, ETH-2159, ETH-2180.
**Trap:** Treating Section 6A as still operative.
**Mains hook:** Prior approval is a live design question; rank-neutral safeguards are the current model.
**Back:** *"Section 6A fell in 2014; Section 17A rose in 2018 without rank."*

### Flashcard ETH-2170 — The social audit cycle: from disclosure to remedy
**Front:** What makes a social audit more than publication of data?
**Definition (learn):** A credible social audit separates verification from the implementing agency, proactively discloses muster rolls, estimates, bills and asset records, verifies them with workers and at the worksite, and places findings before a public hearing or Gram Sabha. The cycle is incomplete without a time-bound action-taken report, recovery or disciplinary referral, grievance remedy and protection against intimidation.
**Breakdown:**
- Independent verification (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Public disclosure (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Hearing or Gram Sabha (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Action-taken report and remedy (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2161, ETH-2173, ETH-2174.
**Trap:** Stopping at publication of data.
**Mains hook:** Social audit is valuable only if it closes with remedy.
**Back:** *"Verify, disclose, hear, act, remedy."*

### Flashcard ETH-2171 — Political versus bureaucratic accountability
**Front:** How does political accountability differ from bureaucratic accountability?
**Definition (learn):** Political accountability makes ministers answerable to the legislature and electorate for policy and outcomes. Bureaucratic accountability makes officials answerable for lawful, impartial and competent execution through hierarchy, files, audit, vigilance, legislative committees and courts.
**Breakdown:**
- Ministers: legislature and electorate (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Officials: hierarchy, files, audit, vigilance, courts (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Political direction cannot require illegality; neutrality does not permit obstruction of a lawful mandate (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2099, ETH-2183, ETH-2182.
**Trap:** Using bureaucratic neutrality to justify obstructing lawful policy.
**Mains hook:** Two accountabilities, one lawful line.
**Back:** *"Ministers answer to the House and voters; officials answer to law and record."*

### Flashcard ETH-2172 — E-governance with ethical safeguards
**Front:** What ethical safeguards does e-governance need?
**Definition (learn):** E-governance reduces delay, discretion and record tampering, but can create exclusion, language or disability barriers, authentication errors, privacy risks, opaque automated decisions, vendor dependence and poor data. Safeguards: assisted and offline access, human review, speaking reasons, audit logs and appeal.
**Breakdown:**
- Benefits: less delay and tampering (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Risks: exclusion, privacy, opacity (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Design requirements: human review, reasons, logs, appeal (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2145, ETH-2161, ETH-2186.
**Trap:** Equating digitisation with accountability.
**Mains hook:** Digital systems need speaking reasons and an appeal route.
**Back:** *"Digital reduces discretion, but only human reasons and appeals make it accountable."*

### Flashcard ETH-2173 — MGNREGA restoration: reconciling records and restoring accountability (2025 GS-IV Q11)
**Front:** What steps would an administrator take to restore MGNREGA accountability against ghost beneficiaries or unverifiable work?
**Definition (learn):** Reconcile demand registers, job cards, muster rolls, attendance, wage payments, technical sanctions and physical assets; hold an independent social-audit hearing; protect complainants; initiate recovery, disciplinary or criminal referral where warranted; restore timely wages and grievance closure.
**Breakdown:**
- Reconcile records (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Independent social audit (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Protect complainants (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Recovery and referral (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- Restore wages and grievance closure (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
- 2025 GS-IV Q11, Section B, 20 marks (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 GS-IV Q11 (✅ owner 11). Facts are an exam hypothetical, not proof of programme-wide conditions (✅ owner 11 §4).
**Links:** ETH-2170, ETH-2161, ETH-2111.
**Trap:** Generalising the exam facts to the whole programme.
**Mains hook:** Records, audit, protection, remedy.
**Back:** *"Reconcile, hear, protect, recover, pay, close."*

### Flashcard ETH-2174 — Social capital: bonding versus bridging, and the MKSS audit mechanism (2023 GS-IV Q6(b))
**Front:** How does social capital support social audit, and what is its limit as an accountability substitute?
**Definition (learn):** Social capital operates as an informal input into social control. Villagers who trust the audit process and each other's testimony can hold a powerful local functionary answerable. Bonding (in-group) capital can shield misconduct, so bridging capital matters. It cannot replace internal or external control.
**Breakdown:**
- Social audits function only where bridging capital exists (⚠️ owner 11 §10).
- Low or purely bonding capital: capture or intimidation (⚠️ owner 11 §10).
- Limit: strengthens the social leg only (⚠️ owner 11 §10).
- Full concept in Ethics 06 §11 (✅ owner 11 §10 cross-link).
- 2023 GS-IV Q6(b) (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2023 GS-IV Q6(b) (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ETH-2170, ETH-2161, ETH-2162.
**Trap:** Treating social capital as a replacement for CAG or CVC.
**Mains hook:** Trust is a precondition for social audit, not a substitute for control.
**Back:** *"Bridging trust lets people audit; bonding trust can hide the fault."*

### Flashcard ETH-2175 — Digital dashboards: transparency is not accountability
**Front:** Why does a real-time dashboard not by itself create accountability?
**Definition (learn):** Dashboards improve transparency, meaning visibility of data. Accountability requires a consequence attaching to a responsible individual, with an identifiable officer, exception-review duty, public access, data-quality checks and an escalation or sanction route.
**Breakdown:**
- Visibility is transparency (⚠️ owner 11 §4, advanced §5).
- Responsible officers and escalation make it accountability (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §5).
- Data quality and access are preconditions (⚠️ owner 11 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2088, ETH-2167, ETH-2154.
**Trap:** "The dashboard is live, so the scheme is accountable."
**Mains hook:** Visibility becomes accountability only when a forum can act.
**Back:** *"Visible data is not answerable data until someone must act on it."*

### Flashcard ETH-2176 — CAG Report No. 2 of 2026: MGNREGA implementation in Rajasthan (📰)
**Front:** What is the CAG report on MGNREGA in Rajasthan, and how should it be used?
**Definition (learn):** CAG, Implementation of the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act in Rajasthan, Report No. 2 of 2026, tabled 21 August 2026. It is a current external-accountability anchor for independent audit and follow-up architecture (✅ owner 11 advanced §9 as-of the owner date).
**Breakdown:**
- Use the report metadata only (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §9).
- Do not invent audit findings or financial totals without the report (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Report No. 2 of 2026; tabled 21 August 2026 (✅ owner 11 advanced §9; not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-2160, ETH-2173, ETH-2167.
**Trap:** Quoting findings not read.
**Mains hook:** Cite the audit as an external-control anchor; discuss follow-up, not totals.
**Back:** *"Cite the report's existence; quote its findings only after reading them."*

### Flashcard ETH-2177 — Mechanism-targeted reform: a five-step procedure (mains)
**Front:** Give the five-step procedure for a mechanism-targeted accountability reform.
**Definition (learn):** (1) Classify the failure by control layer. (2) Name its structural mechanism (collusion, time-lag, capacity, diffusion). (3) Cite the reform already attempted and its documented limit. (4) Propose a mechanism-targeted improvement. (5) Name the safeguard against the improvement's own risk.
**Breakdown:**
- Layer, mechanism, prior reform, targeted measure, safeguard (⚠️ owner 11 advanced §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-2163, ETH-2164, ETH-2175.
**Trap:** Generic "strengthen vigilance" answers.
**Mains hook:** Mechanism first, reform second, safeguard third.
**Back:** *"Layer, mechanism, past reform, targeted fix, guard against the fix."*

### Flashcard ETH-2178 — Trap: accountability and responsibility mean the same thing
**Front:** Are accountability and responsibility the same?
**Definition (learn):** No. Responsibility assigns the duty. Accountability requires explanation and justification to a forum capable of review, correction, remedy or consequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Duty versus answerability (⚠️ owner 11 §6).
- Accountability needs a forum (⚠️ owner 11 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 11 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2143, ETH-2144.
**Trap:** Treating them as synonyms.
**Mains hook:** Name the duty, then the forum.
**Back:** *"Duty is assigned; answerability is owed."*

### Flashcard ETH-2179 — Trap: CAG audit alone ensures ethical use; internal vigilance substitutes for external control
**Front:** Does CAG audit alone ensure ethical use of public funds, and does strengthening internal vigilance replace external or social control?
**Definition (learn):** No on both. CAG audit is typically time-lagged, which limits its deterrent value unless made more concurrent. Internal vigilance catches administrative lapses, while external and social control catch collusion. They are complementary.
**Breakdown:**
- CAG time-lag (✅ owner 11 §6).
- Complementarity (✅ owner 11 §6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 11 §6).
**Links:** ETH-2160, ETH-2162, ETH-2167.
**Trap:** Substitution claims in either direction.
**Mains hook:** Each layer addresses different failures.
**Back:** *"Each layer catches a different failure; none is a substitute."*

### Flashcard ETH-2180 — Trap: the CVC Act gave the CVC power to prosecute
**Front:** Did the CVC Act, 2003 give the CVC direct power to prosecute corruption cases?
**Definition (learn):** No. It gave the CVC statutory status and superintendence over the CBI's anti-corruption functions, plus advisory powers. Prosecution remains with the CBI or state agencies, subject to sanction requirements.
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory status and superintendence (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
- Advisory powers (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
- Prosecution with CBI and states (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2159, ETH-2168, ETH-2169.
**Trap:** Confusing superintendence with prosecution.
**Mains hook:** Name the agency that actually prosecutes.
**Back:** *"CVC supervises; CBI investigates; sanction still gates prosecution."*

### Flashcard ETH-2181 — Trap: PIL activism has largely solved India's retail corruption problem
**Front:** Has judicial PIL activism substantially solved retail corruption?
**Definition (learn):** No. PIL and Article 142 activism has been most visible against high-profile scams. Retail corruption remains bottlenecked in delay-prone trial courts.
**Breakdown:**
- Scam activism (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
- Retail bottleneck (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2164, ETH-2165.
**Trap:** Measuring success by scam verdicts alone.
**Mains hook:** Reform trial-court capacity.
**Back:** *"Scams make headlines; retail corruption needs trial courts."*

### Flashcard ETH-2182 — Trap: concurrent audit has replaced post-facto CAG audit
**Front:** Has concurrent audit replaced post-facto CAG audit in India?
**Definition (learn):** No. Post-facto CAG audit remains the predominant model. Digital and real-time expenditure tracking is a supplementary, expanding practice, not a wholesale replacement.
**Breakdown:**
- Post-facto remains predominant (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
- Concurrent is supplementary (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner trap (✅ owner 11 advanced §8).
**Links:** ETH-2167, ETH-2160, ETH-2162.
**Trap:** Confusing a digital dashboard with a constitutional audit.
**Mains hook:** Concurrent audit supplements, not replaces, CAG.
**Back:** *"Real-time tracking helps; it does not replace the auditor."*

### Flashcard ETH-2183 — 2019 GS-IV Q10: politicisation of the bureaucracy
**Front:** What does the 2019 GS-IV Q10 question on bureaucratic politicisation ask?
**Definition (learn):** The question asks about the political executive encroaching on the permanent executive, including transfers and postings, and the consequences of this trend. It is a 20-mark case-style discussion routed to the Ethics method.
**Breakdown:**
- Encroachment: transfers and postings (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- Consequences: neutrality, morale, service delivery (⚠️ owner analysis).
- 2019 GS-IV Q10, Discuss, 20 marks (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2019 GS-IV Q10 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ETH-2099, ETH-2171.
**Trap:** Describing politicisation as always illegal; some ministerial direction is lawful.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish lawful direction from illegal interference.
**Back:** *"Transfers are the lever: lawful direction and illegal interference differ."*

### Flashcard ETH-2184 — 2021 GS-IV Q6: independent social audit and integrity as empowerment
**Front:** What does the 2021 GS-IV Q6 pair ask about social audit and integrity?
**Definition (learn):** (a) Elaborate or justify independent social audit as an absolute must for public-service accountability and ethical conduct. (b) Explain integrity as a value that empowers the human being.
**Breakdown:**
- Social audit's independence (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- Integrity as empowerment (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- 10 + 10 marks, 150 words each (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-IV Q6 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ETH-2170, ETH-2174, ETH-2150.
**Trap:** Calling social audit absolute without naming its independence limits.
**Mains hook:** Independence and follow-up make social audit credible.
**Back:** *"Independent audit, followed up, makes integrity visible."*

### Flashcard ETH-2185 — 2021 GS-IV Q9: elevated corridor crack and pressure to overlook
**Front:** What options and dilemmas does the 2021 GS-IV Q9 case involve?
**Definition (learn):** A project manager notices a crack in a pier of an elevated corridor. The minister and chief engineer pressure him to overlook it. The question asks for options, ethical dilemmas, professional challenges and consequences.
**Breakdown:**
- Options (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- Ethical dilemmas and professional challenges (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- Consequences (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- 20 marks, Section B (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 GS-IV Q9 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ETH-2127, ETH-2128, ETH-2111.
**Trap:** Treating the case as a purely technical engineering question.
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder: record the concern, escalate, protect public safety.
**Back:** *"A crack in the pier is a public-safety fact; the pressure is not a technical opinion."*

### Flashcard ETH-2186 — 2022 GS-IV Q4: e-governance benefits and ethics of online methodology
**Front:** What does the 2022 GS-IV Q4 pair ask about e-governance and online methodology?
**Definition (learn):** (a) Good governance and e-governance initiatives for beneficiaries. (b) Ethical issues in online methodology affecting vulnerable sections of society.
**Breakdown:**
- Beneficiary benefit (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- Ethics of online methods for vulnerable groups (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
- 10 + 10 marks, 150 words each (✅ owner 11 PYQ table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2022 GS-IV Q4 (✅ owner 11).
**Links:** ETH-2172, ETH-2161.
**Trap:** Praising digital access without naming exclusion.
**Mains hook:** Pair benefits with assisted access and remedy.
**Back:** *"Online reaches the many; the vulnerable need assisted access and remedy."*

## ETHICS — Part 3: Corporate and International Ethics, Emerging Ethics, Probity

> Scope: owner files 12 (Corporate Governance and International Ethics), 13 (Emerging Ethics: Technology, AI and Environment) and 14 (Probity: Concept and Philosophical Basis of Governance), basic and advanced versions read in full. Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = repository owner text; `(✅ Wikipedia)` = fetched page text; `⚠️` = unverified against a source or owner-vs-source conflict (flagged inside the card). Cards are numbered ETH-3001 to ETH-3083 with no gaps.

### GROUP — 12 Corporate Governance and International Ethics

### Flashcard ETH-3001 — Corporate governance, international ethics and the "ethics in business" frame

**Front:** What is the core theme of owner 12, and how do corporate ethics, corporate governance, CSR and international anti-corruption law fit together?

**Definition (learn):** Owner 12 asks how ethical conduct is embedded in ordinary commercial decisions and how domestic and international law reinforces it. Four layers: (1) "ethics in business" (how every decision is made); (2) corporate governance (structures that direct and control the company accountably); (3) CSR (a statutory spend obligation under Companies Act 2013 s.135); (4) international anti-corruption architecture (UNCAC, ADB-OECD, World Bank conditionality, FCPA).

**Breakdown:**
- Layer 1 — ethics in business: honesty, labour treatment, procurement, pricing, environmental compliance in ordinary decisions.
- Layer 2 — governance: board composition, audit committee independence, related-party-transaction disclosure.
- Layer 3 — CSR: ring-fenced, Schedule VII spend; it does not prove core-business ethics.
- Layer 4 — international: binding treaty (UNCAC), cooperative peer framework (ADB-OECD), lender conditionality (World Bank).
- Shared diagnosis with public administration: fragmented oversight, retaliation against reporters, compliance theatre (cross-link ETH-3017, ETH-3014).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 12 is grounded in the 2nd ARC 4th Report *Ethics in Governance* (2007), Companies Act 2013 s.135, and GS-IV PYQs (2019, 2022, 2023, 2024). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3002 (ethics in business), ETH-3004 (CSR vs ethics), ETH-3011 (UNCAC), ETH-3017 (Sarbanes-Oxley whistleblowing).

**Trap:** Treating all four layers as one "compliance" idea. Each has its own legal basis, actor and failure mode, and an answer should keep them separate.

**Mains hook:** "Corporate ethics cannot be outsourced to a compliance department or to CSR spend." Qualified conclusion: ethical authority must sit inside core decisions, backed by domestic enforcement and layered international commitments.

**Back:** *"Four floors, one roof: business ethics in the core, governance above it, CSR beside it, treaties around it."*

### Flashcard ETH-3002 — "Ethics in business" versus "business ethics" (2nd ARC, para 1.17)

**Front:** Why does the 2nd ARC prefer "ethics in business" to "business ethics"?

**Definition (learn):** The ARC's preferred phrase, "ethics in business", means ethical conduct is integrated into ordinary commercial decision-making. "Business ethics" in the ARC's pejorative sense is a bolt-on compliance or PR layer added to profit-seeking. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- "Business ethics" (narrow): external PR or compliance layer, disconnected from core operations.
- "Ethics in business" (ARC 1.17): how business is actually conducted, embedded in every decision.
- The shift is a "paradigm shift" the ARC explicitly endorses, applying across the board.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 4th Report *Ethics in Governance* (2007), para 1.17. (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3001 (layers), ETH-3009 (greenwashing), ETH-3003 (governance structures).

**Trap:** Saying the two phrases mean the same thing. The ARC chose "ethics in business" precisely to reject the bolt-on connotation.

**Mains hook:** Use an Indian corporate example to show the difference (for example, a governance ethics function with real veto power over high-risk transactions). Qualified conclusion: ethics is a property of decisions, not of a department.

**Back:** *"Business ethics is a department; ethics in business is a decision."*

### Flashcard ETH-3003 — Corporate governance, agency problem and board committees

**Front:** Define corporate governance and name the three standard board committees mentioned in the owner and source checks.

**Definition (learn):** Corporate governance is the structure of processes, practices and relations through which boards, managers, shareholders and stakeholders direct and control a corporation. Its foundational economic-ethics problem is the agency problem: owners (shareholders) separated from control (managers) who may prioritise personal or short-term gain. (⚠️ owner 12 gives the agency problem; ✅ Wikipedia gives the definition.)

**Breakdown:**
- Agency problem: separation of ownership and control creates incentives for managers to serve themselves.
- Audit committee: independent members; SOX requires independence and disclosure of a financial expert.
- Nominating/governance committee: NYSE listing rule requires independent directors (cited as a comparator).
- Compensation committee: named among standard board committees.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Governance committees are the mechanism by which board oversight becomes concrete. (✅ Wikipedia for committee definitions; ⚠️ NYSE rule is a foreign comparator, not Indian law.)

**Links:** ETH-3001, ETH-3004 (independent board and related-party scrutiny), ETH-3014 (SFO model).

**Trap:** Presenting NYSE or SOX rules as Indian law. They are comparators.

**Mains hook:** Governance ethics needs independent oversight, not only codes. Qualified conclusion: governance structures are necessary but are reliable only when independent directors and audit committees have real information and authority.

**Back:** *"Ownership and control split; agency problems follow; committees close the gap."*

### Flashcard ETH-3004 — CSR is not corporate ethics

**Front:** What is the difference between CSR and corporate ethics, and why is "CSR spend" never a proxy for core-business conduct?

**Definition (learn):** CSR under Companies Act 2013 s.135 is a statutory, ring-fenced expenditure obligation on qualifying companies, spent on Schedule VII activities. Corporate ethics concerns the honesty and lawfulness of the company's core operations (accounting, labour, environment, anti-bribery). (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- CSR: "Has the company met its mandated minimum social spend?"
- Corporate ethics: "Is this transaction and business practice honest and lawful?"
- Governance: "Is the internal decision structure trustworthy?"
- A company can meet CSR to the last rupee while its core business is unethical.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three-layer comparison table in owner 12, section 10. (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3001, ETH-3005 (s.135 thresholds), ETH-3009 (greenwashing), ETH-3008 (impact assessment).

**Trap:** "A company that spends 2% on CSR is ethical." It is compliant with s.135, not necessarily ethical in its core operations.

**Mains hook:** Any "critically examine CSR" question must separate the statutory floor from core conduct. Qualified conclusion: CSR is a valuable floor, but it must be paired with core-business ethics and governance integrity.

**Back:** *"CSR is the spend; ethics is the business."*

### Flashcard ETH-3005 — Companies Act 2013, Section 135: applicability and 2% spend

**Front:** What are the three applicability thresholds for CSR under Section 135 and what is the spending obligation?

**Definition (learn):** A company must comply with s.135 if, in the immediately preceding financial year, it had net worth of at least ₹500 crore, or turnover of at least ₹1,000 crore, or net profit of at least ₹5 crore. Such a company must spend at least 2% of the average net profits of the three immediately preceding financial years on Schedule VII activities, through a board-approved CSR policy. (✅ owner 12; ✅ Wikipedia for the Act's existence and s.135 subject matter.)

**Breakdown:**
- Threshold test: any one of net worth ₹500 crore, turnover ₹1,000 crore, net profit ₹5 crore.
- Base: average net profit of the three preceding financial years.
- Output: at least 2% on Schedule VII activities.
- Board: CSR Committee and board-approved policy (exemption in s.135(9) for spend not exceeding ₹50 lakh, see ETH-3006).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Threshold and 2% figures are statutory, not rule-level. Owner 12 notes rule-level detail changes more often than the statutory thresholds. (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3004, ETH-3006, ETH-3007.

**Trap:** Confusing the 2% base (average net profit of three years) with 2% of current-year turnover or profit.

**Mains hook:** Use s.135 to show that CSR is a statutory obligation, not a voluntary favour. Qualified conclusion: a statutory floor for social spend is a strength, but the obligation can still be met through low-impact activity.

**Back:** *"Five-hundred, a thousand, five; two percent of the three-year average."*

### Flashcard ETH-3006 — Section 135: unspent-fund architecture and the 2020 penalty

**Front:** What happens to unspent CSR money, and what penalty did the 2020 amendment impose?

**Definition (learn):** Under s.135(5)-(6) (inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act 2019, in force from 22 January 2021): an amount not linked to an ongoing project goes to a Schedule VII fund within six months of the financial year-end. An amount for an ongoing project goes to an Unspent CSR Account within 30 days, must be spent within three financial years, and any residue goes to a Schedule VII fund within 30 days. The Companies (Amendment) Act 2020 converted the s.135(7) sanction into a civil monetary penalty and inserted s.135(9). (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- Non-ongoing project: Schedule VII fund within 6 months.
- Ongoing project: Unspent CSR Account within 30 days; spend within 3 financial years; residue to fund within 30 days.
- 2020 penalty (company): lesser of twice the transferable amount or ₹1 crore; officer in default: lesser of one-tenth of that amount or ₹2 lakh.
- s.135(9): company whose required spend does not exceed ₹50 lakh need not form a CSR Committee.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** In force from 22 January 2021 (owner 12). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3005, ETH-3007, ETH-3008.

**Trap:** Saying the 2019 amendment created the penalty. The penalty came in 2020; the unspent architecture came in 2019.

**Mains hook:** Use the unspent-fund rules to show that CSR accountability has moved from disclosure to enforceable deadlines. Qualified conclusion: deadlines discipline spend but do not measure social impact.

**Back:** *"Six months, thirty days, three years, then the fund."*

### Flashcard ETH-3007 — CSR impact assessment: the 2021 mandate and the 2022 cost cap

**Front:** When did impact assessment for CSR projects become mandatory, at what threshold, and what did the 2022 amendment change?

**Definition (learn):** The Companies (CSR Policy) Amendment Rules 2021 introduced a requirement that companies with an average CSR obligation of ₹10 crore or more in the three preceding years commission an independent impact assessment for projects with an outlay of ₹1 crore or more, completed at least one year before the assessment. The 2022 amendment did not create the mandate; it revised the cap on assessment cost to 2% of total CSR expenditure for the year or ₹50 lakh, whichever is higher. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- 2021: mandate created; threshold ₹10 crore average obligation; project outlay ₹1 crore.
- 2022: cost cap revised to the higher of 2% of total CSR expenditure or ₹50 lakh.
- Form CSR-2 was introduced by the Companies (Accounts) Amendment Rules 2022, not the 2021 CSR Rules. ⚠️ owner 12 note.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ₹10 crore threshold; ₹1 crore project; ₹50 lakh cap floor. (✅ owner 12)
