**Links:** ETH-3004 (greenwashing gap), ETH-3006, ETH-3009.

**Trap:** Attributing the impact-assessment mandate to the 2022 amendment, or attributing Form CSR-2 to the 2021 rules.

**Mains hook:** Impact assessment is a real regulatory response to box-ticking, but it applies only above ₹10 crore. Qualified conclusion: smaller obligors remain unaudited for social outcomes.

**Back:** *"Ten crore triggers the audit; the 2022 cap sets the price."*

### Flashcard ETH-3008 — Social Stock Exchange route for CSR (27 May 2026)

**Front:** What dated change to CSR under Schedule VII was made by notifications G.S.R. 415(E) and 416(E)?

**Definition (learn):** On 27 May 2026, MCA notifications G.S.R. 415(E) and 416(E) added subscription to eligible zero-coupon zero-principal instruments on the Social Stock Exchange to Schedule VII, with a capped route under a new Rule 4A at 10% of annual CSR expenditure. It is an additional CSR route; it does not change s.135's thresholds or the 2% base. (📰 ✅ owner 12 and advanced 12.)

**Breakdown:**
- New Schedule VII route: subscription to eligible zero-coupon zero-principal instruments on the Social Stock Exchange.
- Cap: 10% of annual CSR expenditure (Rule 4A).
- Unchanged: thresholds and 2% base.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 27 May 2026. Time-varying: verify before quoting in a live context. (✅ owner 12; ⚠️ time-varying, not re-verified.)

**Links:** ETH-3005, ETH-3006.

**Trap:** Saying the 2026 notifications changed the s.135 threshold. They did not.

**Mains hook:** Shows CSR rules evolve; use only as a dated illustration. Qualified conclusion: new routes widen options but do not replace core-business ethics.

**Back:** *"Twenty-six, May: a new CSR door, same floor."*

### Flashcard ETH-3009 — Greenwashing and the box-ticking limit of CSR

**Front:** What is greenwashing, when was the term first used, and how does it apply to CSR?

**Definition (learn):** Greenwashing is marketing or PR that misleads people into thinking an organisation's products, goals or policies are good for the environment. The term is first attributed to 1986, when environmentalist Jay Westerveld used it in an essay about hotel towel-reuse cards. In CSR, box-ticking means meeting the spend requirement through easily measured, low-impact activity while core operations stay carbon-intensive or exploitative. (✅ Wikipedia for definition and 1986 attribution; ⚠️ box-ticking application is owner 12 analysis.)

**Breakdown:**
- Greenwashing: misleading environmental claim.
- Box-ticking: formal compliance without substantive outcome.
- Response: impact assessment (ETH-3007) plus core-business ethics and governance integrity.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** First attribution 1986 (Westerveld). (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3004, ETH-3007, ETH-3010.

**Trap:** Dating the term to the 2000s or treating it as a modern coinage.

**Mains hook:** Use it to diagnose a CSR-only environmental claim. Qualified conclusion: CSR compliance must be paired with, not substituted for, verifiable core change.

**Back:** *"Painted leaves on a smoking chimney."*

### Flashcard ETH-3010 — Stakeholder versus shareholder models of corporate ethics

**Front:** Contrast the shareholder-primacy and stakeholder models of corporate ethics, with their key proponents.

**Definition (learn):** Shareholder-primacy models judge corporate ethics mainly by fiduciary duty to owners and profit. Stakeholder models extend ethical obligation to employees, customers, communities, environment and future generations. R. Edward Freeman is widely identified as the father of stakeholder theory; his *Strategic Management: A Stakeholder Approach* (1984) argues a firm should not serve only owners. (✅ owner 12; ✅ Wikipedia for Freeman 1984.)

**Breakdown:**
- Shareholder model: profit and fiduciary duty to owners.
- Stakeholder model: employees, consumers, communities, environment count as legitimate interests.
- Milton Friedman (1970 essay on business's social responsibility to increase profits): ⚠️ not verified against a fetched source in this card; use with a source check.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Freeman 1984 (book); stakeholder theory usage in the 2024 GS-IV Q7 framing. (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3001, ETH-3003, ETH-3009.

**Trap:** Presenting stakeholder theory as legally required in India. The Companies Act s.135 creates a CSR floor, not a general stakeholder duty.

**Mains hook:** The 2024 Q7 dilemma is a stakeholder-versus-shareholder tension. Qualified conclusion: a stakeholder-sensitive board still needs verifiable, time-bound commitments.

**Back:** *"Owners first is one view; Freeman says the whole table counts."*

### Flashcard ETH-3011 — UN Convention against Corruption (UNCAC)

**Front:** When was UNCAC adopted, when did it enter into force, and what are India's signature and ratification dates?

**Definition (learn):** UNCAC is the principal global anti-corruption treaty. It requires States Parties to take preventive, criminalisation, cooperation and asset-recovery measures; Articles 6 and 8 concern preventive bodies and public-official codes. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- Adopted by UNGA resolution 58/4, 31 October 2003.
- Opened for signature at Merida, December 2003.
- In force: 14 December 2005.
- India: signed 9 December 2005; ratified 9 May 2011.
- Preventive bodies (Art 6) and public-official codes (Art 8) are the articles to name.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 31 October 2003; 14 December 2005; 9 December 2005 (signed); 9 May 2011 (ratified). (✅ owner 12. Attempted Wikipedia check failed in this build; treat the dates as owner-sourced.)

**Links:** ETH-3012 (ADB-OECD), ETH-3013 (three layers), ETH-3018 (FCPA), ETH-3017.

**Trap:** Saying India is bound by the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention because it cooperates with the OECD. UNCAC ratification is the binding treaty commitment.

**Mains hook:** Any question on global anti-corruption cooperation should name UNCAC by article. Qualified conclusion: UNCAC is binding only through national implementation and has no direct enforcement over sovereigns.

**Back:** *"Two-oh-three adopted, two-oh-five in force, two-oh-eleven India ratified."*

### Flashcard ETH-3012 — ADB-OECD Anti-Corruption Action Plan and the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention

**Front:** What is the ADB-OECD Anti-Corruption Action Plan, when did India endorse it, and why is it different from the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention?

**Definition (learn):** The ADB-OECD Anti-Corruption Action Plan for Asia and the Pacific is a regional, non-binding cooperative framework that India endorsed on 30 November 2001. It commits signatories to inter-regional cooperation on corruption prevention. It is not the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention, 1997, to which India is not a party. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- ADB-OECD Action Plan: regional, peer-review, non-binding.
- OECD Anti-Bribery Convention 1997: binding for its parties; India is not a party.
- Confusing the two is the central trap.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** India endorsed the Action Plan on 30 November 2001 (owner 12, quoting ARC 1.14). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3011, ETH-3013, ETH-3018.

**Trap:** "India is bound by the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention because it works with the OECD." Wrong: India endorsed the Action Plan and ratified UNCAC but is not a party to the Convention.

**Mains hook:** Use the distinction to show India's commitments are layered, not singular. Qualified conclusion: peer-review frameworks depend on political will at home.

**Back:** *"Action Plan endorsed in two-thousand-one; the Convention is not ours."*

### Flashcard ETH-3013 — Three layers of international anti-corruption: treaty, peer review, conditionality

**Front:** Name the three international anti-corruption layers in ARC 1.14 and the enforcement logic of each.

**Definition (learn):** (1) UNCAC: binding multilateral treaty. (2) ADB-OECD Action Plan: cooperative peer framework. (3) World Bank conditionality: a lender refusing to fund projects "tainted by corrupt practices". The ARC also notes a 2006 IMF-World Bank Singapore joint statement on a common anti-fraud framework. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- Treaty obligation: UNCAC.
- Cooperative peer review: ADB-OECD.
- Financial conditionality: World Bank.
- Do not merge them into one undifferentiated "international pressure".

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ARC 1.14; 2006 Singapore joint statement (IMF-WB). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3011, ETH-3012, ETH-3014.

**Trap:** Saying the Action Plan is a treaty, or that World Bank conditions are treaty law.

**Mains hook:** Name the logic of each layer to show analytical precision. Qualified conclusion: each layer enforces differently, so no single instrument is sufficient.

**Back:** *"Treaty binds, peers review, lenders condition."*

### Flashcard ETH-3014 — The Serious Fraud Office model (UK) and why the ARC copied it

**Front:** What is the Serious Fraud Office model, and what design features did the ARC endorse?

**Definition (learn):** A dedicated, multi-disciplinary investigative body (law, accountancy, investigation expertise) for complex corporate and financial fraud, modelled on the UK Serious Fraud Office. The UK SFO was constituted under the Criminal Justice Act 1987 and began operating in April 1988. Its Director acts under the superintendence of the Attorney General and may institute and conduct its own prosecutions. (✅ owner 12 — ARC Box 3.2, 3.7.4; the Wikipedia check returned only a disambiguation page, so the 1987/1988 dates rest on the owner.)

**Breakdown:**
- Multi-disciplinary team: lawyers, accountants, investigators.
- Director under the Attorney General's superintendence.
- Can prosecute its own cases without a separate prosecution service.
- Investigates offences that appear to involve "serious or complex fraud" on reasonable grounds; the term is left undefined by the Act.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** CJA 1987; operational April 1988 (owner 12 advanced). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3015 (Naresh Chandra), ETH-3016 (SFIO), ETH-3013.

**Trap:** Treating the SFO as an Indian body. It is the UK model that the ARC recommended India follow.

**Mains hook:** Multi-disciplinary investigation is the cure for fragmented corporate fraud oversight. Qualified conclusion: the model works only with independent prosecutorial power and adequate expertise.

**Back:** *"Lawyers, accountants and investigators in one room, with their own prosecutions."*

### Flashcard ETH-3015 — Naresh Chandra Committee (2002) and the Corporate Serious Fraud Office proposal

**Front:** What did the Naresh Chandra Committee recommend in 2002, and what is its formal name?

**Definition (learn):** The committee recommended a Corporate Serious Fraud Office in the then Department of Company Affairs: a multi-disciplinary team that uncovers fraud and can direct and supervise prosecutions, with a task force for each case and oversight by a committee headed by the Cabinet Secretary. ARC 3.7.9 cites it. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- Recommendation: Corporate Serious Fraud Office (CSFO).
- Location: Department of Company Affairs.
- Oversight: committee headed by Cabinet Secretary.
- ⚠️ Owner 12 gives the formal name as High Level Committee on Corporate Audit and Governance, appointed 21 August 2002. ARC 3.7.9 calls it the committee on "Corporate Audit and Finance". The two names conflict; flag and use the owner name with the caveat.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2002 recommendation; appointment date 21 August 2002 (⚠️ owner-only; not verified). Naming conflict flagged.

**Links:** ETH-3014 (SFO model), ETH-3016 (SFIO as the institution that followed).

**Trap:** Describing the committee's name without noting the ARC's variant name.

**Mains hook:** A two-decade lag between recommendation and institution is evidence that reform arrives slowly. Qualified conclusion: recommendations need statutory design and funding to become institutions.

**Back:** *"Two thousand two, a corporate SFO on paper, a Cabinet Secretary above it."*

### Flashcard ETH-3016 — Serious Fraud Investigation Office (SFIO) under the Companies Act 2013

**Front:** Where does the SFIO's statutory basis lie, how was it notified, and what is its arrest power?

**Definition (learn):** The SFIO is India's institution that followed the Naresh Chandra recommendation. It was first set up by Government Resolution of 2 July 2003 and later given statutory footing under s.211 of the Companies Act 2013, notified by S.O. 2005(E) dated 21 July 2015. Section 212(8) empowers arrest where there is material-based reason to believe a person is guilty of fraud under s.447. (✅ owner 12 — basic and advanced.)

**Breakdown:**
- 2 July 2003: Government Resolution setting up the SFIO (owner).
- s.211 Companies Act 2013: statutory basis.
- S.O. 2005(E), 21 July 2015: notification.
- s.212(8): arrest power; s.447: fraud penalty provision.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2 July 2003; 21 July 2015; s.211, s.212(8), s.447. (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3014, ETH-3015, ETH-3017.

**Trap:** Saying the SFIO is identical to the 2002/2007 CSFO proposal. It is related but not identical: the proposal envisaged a body under a Cabinet Secretary-headed committee, while the SFIO works under s.211 with its own arrest power.

**Mains hook:** Use the SFIO as the "recommendation to institution" example. Qualified conclusion: statutory arrest power requires procedural safeguards, which courts have examined in specific cases.

**Back:** *"Two-thousand-three set it up; two-thousand-fifteen gave it teeth."*

### Flashcard ETH-3017 — Sarbanes-Oxley Act 2002: whistleblower sections 806 and 1107

**Front:** What did SOX s.806 and s.1107 provide for whistleblowers, and which one carries the ten-year prison term?

**Definition (learn):** The Sarbanes-Oxley Act was signed into law by President George W. Bush on 30 July 2002 after the Enron and WorldCom scandals. Section 806 is the civil anti-retaliation remedy for employees of public companies (18 U.S.C. §1514A); section 1107 is the criminal offence of retaliating against a person who gives law enforcement truthful information (18 U.S.C. §1513(e)), punishable by up to 10 years' imprisonment. (✅ Wikipedia; owner 12 agrees on §1514A and §1513(e).)

**Breakdown:**
- s.806 (civil): reinstatement, back pay with interest, special damages; claims go first to OSHA; no prison term.
- s.1107 (criminal): fine, up to 10 years, or both.
- WorldCom disclosed on 25 June 2002 that it had overstated earnings by more than $3.8 billion over five quarters (✅ Wikipedia).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 30 July 2002 (signed); 18 U.S.C. §1514A; 18 U.S.C. §1513(e); 10 years.

**Links:** ETH-3014, ETH-3016, ETH-3001.

**Trap:** Attaching the 10-year prison term to s.806. It belongs to s.1107 (criminal). s.806 is civil.

**Mains hook:** Corporate and public-sector failures trigger the same whistleblower logic (see ETH-3016 and the public-sector owner 19). Qualified conclusion: protection is credible only with an enforceable remedy, not only a declaration.

**Back:** *"Eight-oh-six sues, eleven-oh-seven jails: ten years for the chill."*

### Flashcard ETH-3018 — US Foreign Corrupt Practices Act (FCPA), 1977

**Front:** What does the FCPA cover, and what does it say about books and records and internal controls?

**Definition (learn):** The Foreign Corrupt Practices Act was enacted in 1977 (signed by President Carter on 19 December 1977). It bans payments to foreign officials to obtain or retain business. It covers domestic concerns (US citizens, nationals, residents and businesses based in the US), issuers with securities registered under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, and, since 1998, foreign persons who act in furtherance of a corrupt payment while in US territory. (✅ Wikipedia)

**Breakdown:**
- Anti-bribery: payments to foreign officials; extraterritorial reach via issuers and US territory.
- Books and records: companies must keep books that "accurately and fairly reflect the transactions".
- Internal accounting controls: "an adequate system of internal accounting controls".
- Advanced note: FCPA enforcement policy changed in 2025 under EO 14209 (10 February 2025) and revised DOJ guidelines of 9 June 2025. ⚠️ Owner 12 date-stamps these; not re-verified here. Describe enforcement as selective and policy-dependent, not abandoned.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1977 (signed 19 December 1977); 1998 amendment; ⚠️ EO 14209 (owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3011, ETH-3012, ETH-3017.

**Trap:** Describing the FCPA as a UN instrument. It is a US statute with extraterritorial reach.

**Mains hook:** The FCPA shows extraterritorial enforcement is possible but depends on policy. Qualified conclusion: cross-border bribery needs coordinated, not merely national, response.

**Back:** *"Seventy-seven bans the bribe, and the books must tell the truth."*

### Flashcard ETH-3019 — Just War: jus ad bellum criteria

**Front:** Name the jus ad bellum criteria used in the owner's Just War framework and state which one the 2026 Q3(c) quotation tests.

**Definition (learn):** Just War tradition criteria for resorting to war: sovereignty (territorial integrity, UN Charter Art. 2(4)), legitimate (competent) authority (Art. 51 self-defence or Security Council authorisation), right intention, last resort, reasonable prospect of success and proportionality of ends. The legitimate-authority criterion is the one most often omitted and the one that decides the 2026 Q3(c) "arms are fair" quotation. (⚠️ owner 12 gives the framework; ✅ Wikipedia lists four named criteria: competent authority, probability of success, last resort, just cause.)

**Breakdown:**
- Sovereignty: paradigm violation is aggression against a sovereign state.
- Legitimate authority: a public authority, not a faction.
- Right intention: necessary but not sufficient.
- Last resort: a checkable record of alternatives offered and refused.
- Reasonable prospect and proportionality of ends.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UN Charter Art. 2(4) (prohibition of force) and Art. 51 (self-defence). ⚠️ The criteria list differs between owner and Wikipedia's shorter list; both are cited, not merged.

**Links:** ETH-3020 (jus in bello), ETH-3022 (Hotspur), ETH-3023 (intention trap).

**Trap:** Saying "right intention" is the same as "just cause". See ETH-3023.

**Mains hook:** Use the criteria as a conjunctive test: failing one is fatal even if others hold. Qualified conclusion: procedure legitimises the act; intention purifies only the agent.

**Back:** *"Authority, cause, intent, last resort, prospects, proportion: all six, not one."*

### Flashcard ETH-3020 — Jus in bello: distinction, proportionality and humanitarian neutrality

**Front:** What are the core jus in bello principles, and what is India's treaty position on Additional Protocol I?

**Definition (learn):** Jus in bello governs conduct in war regardless of the cause. Core principles: distinction (between combatants and civilians, and civilian infrastructure), proportionality (civilian harm must not be excessive relative to concrete military advantage), military necessity and humane treatment of prisoners. Humanitarian neutrality (ICRC: neutrality, impartiality, independence) allows aid access. (✅ Wikipedia lists distinction, proportionality, military necessity, fair treatment of prisoners and "no means malum in se" as named principles; owner 12 supplies the rest.)

**Breakdown:**
- Distinction: target only combatants and military objectives.
- Proportionality: even lawful targets must not cause excessive civilian harm.
- Military necessity: force only to achieve legitimate military aims.
- Neutrality: aid must be impartial to be accessible to both sides.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** India is a party to the four Geneva Conventions of 1949; ⚠️ owner 12 says India is not a party to Additional Protocols I and II of 1977. Not re-verified in this build.

**Links:** ETH-3019, ETH-3021, ETH-3028 (refugee rule is a separate instrument).

**Trap:** Writing "India is bound by Additional Protocol I". Use "Geneva Conventions and customary IHL" instead.

**Mains hook:** Use distinction and proportionality to evaluate a specific strike, not "war is bad". Qualified conclusion: conduct is judged independently of cause, and symmetrically for both belligerents.

**Back:** *"Spare the civilian, weigh the strike, feed the neutral."*

### Flashcard ETH-3021 — Doctrine of double effect

**Front:** State the four conditions of the doctrine of double effect and why it cannot launder harm used as a means.

**Definition (learn):** A scholastic test, associated with Aquinas, for acts with both a good and a foreseen bad effect. Four conditions: (i) the act itself is not intrinsically wrong; (ii) the bad effect is foreseen but not intended, neither as end nor as means; (iii) the good effect is not produced by the bad effect; (iv) there is proportionate reason for permitting the bad effect. (⚠️ owner 12 only; the Wikipedia check for this card was not completed.)

**Breakdown:**
- Distinguishes incidental civilian death in a proportionate strike from terror bombing.
- Excuses side-effects, never means.
- Cannot justify a case where harm is the mechanism of the intended good.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Aquinas association (⚠️ owner-only attribution).

**Links:** ETH-3019, ETH-3020, ETH-3023.

**Trap:** "Double effect permits any harm the agent did not desire." It requires all four conditions, including proportionality.

**Mains hook:** Use it to separate side-effect harm from means-harm. Qualified conclusion: intention and consequence are both needed.

**Back:** *"Foreseen, not intended, not the means, proportionate: all four or none."*

### Flashcard ETH-3022 — "Arms are fair when the intent of bearing them is just" (Hotspur, Henry IV Part 1)

**Front:** Who speaks the line "the arms are fair when the intent of bearing them is just", and what is its dramatic context?

**Definition (learn):** The line is spoken by Hotspur (Henry Percy), a rebel commander rallying his troops before the Battle of Shrewsbury, in *Henry IV, Part 1*, Act 5 scene 2. In the same scene Worcester has suppressed the King's offer of pardon and instructed Vernon to conceal it, so Hotspur's sense of just intent rests on falsified information. (✅ owner 12 with the Folger text. The play was probably written in the mid-1590s and first published in quarto in 1598 (✅ Wikipedia). The Wikipedia page did not quote the line, so the quotation itself rests on the owner's Folger check.)

**Breakdown:**
- Speaker: Hotspur, rebel.
- Setting: Act 5 scene 2, before Shrewsbury (1403).
- Context: the King's offer was concealed by Worcester.
- Lesson: sincerity without authority, correct facts or non-violent alternatives does not justify war.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Play composed mid-1590s; quarto 1598 (✅ Wikipedia); Battle of Shrewsbury 1403 (⚠️ owner-only date).

**Links:** ETH-3019, ETH-3023.

**Trap:** Presenting the line as Shakespeare's endorsement that intention sanctifies war. It is a rebel's self-justification, ironically framed.

**Mains hook:** Use the line as the thesis to test. Qualified conclusion: intention is necessary but not sufficient.

**Back:** *"A rebel's line, a deceived mind, a hidden pardon."*

### Flashcard ETH-3023 — Right intention is not just cause

**Front:** Distinguish right intention from just cause in Just War reasoning, and give the two standard traps.

**Definition (learn):** Just cause concerns the objective wrong being answered. Right intention concerns why the agent actually acts. A just cause pursued for annexation fails right intention; a sincere motive without just cause fails just cause. Both are necessary; neither is sufficient. (✅ owner 12)

**Breakdown:**
- Just cause: objective wrong, such as aggression.
- Right intention: motive, such as restoring a just peace versus plunder or revenge.
- Culpability (agent) tracks intention; rightness (act) tracks cause, authority, last resort and proportion.
- Symmetry objection: if sincere intent sufficed, both sides would be just.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 12 comparison chart. (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3019, ETH-3022, ETH-3024.

**Trap:** "Right intention is the same as just cause." Also, "ends-justify-means is the opposite of this quotation": both are single-criterion positions.

**Mains hook:** Use the symmetry objection and the agent-act distinction. Qualified conclusion: intention purifies the agent, procedure legitimises the act.

**Back:** *"Cause is what is wronged; intent is who is doing it."*

### Flashcard ETH-3024 — Walzer's "dirty hands"

**Front:** Who coined the modern "dirty hands" argument, when, and what is its ethical minimum?

**Definition (learn):** Michael Walzer, "Political Action: The Problem of Dirty Hands", published in *Philosophy & Public Affairs* in 1973 (✅ Wikipedia). A leader may be obliged to do something genuinely wrong to discharge her office, and the moral residue does not disappear. The ethical minimum: acknowledge the wrong, bear its cost, and refuse to redefine it as right. (✅ Wikipedia for author, year, title, journal; ⚠️ owner's "moral residue" formulation is owner 12 analysis.)

**Breakdown:**
- Source: Walzer 1973; term originates in Sartre's play *Dirty Hands* (1948) per Wikipedia.
- Supreme emergency: a community's survival may justify grave wrong, with guilt accepted.
- Ethical minimum: acknowledge, bear, refuse to rename.
- Ethics is subjugated when the trade-off is renamed a virtue.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1973; Philosophy & Public Affairs. (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3025 (realism), ETH-3001.

**Trap:** Presenting dirty hands as permission to do wrong. The residue must be acknowledged and never called right.

**Mains hook:** Use it to distinguish an honest trade-off from a laundered one. Qualified conclusion: admitted, bounded, reviewed and compensated trade-offs remain ethical costs, not virtues.

**Back:** *"Do the dirty work, name it dirty, carry the stain."*

### Flashcard ETH-3025 — Realism, idealism and constructivism on ethics and national interest

**Front:** Contrast classical realism, liberal idealism and constructivism on whether ethics is subjugated to interest.

**Definition (learn):** Classical realism (Hans Morgenthau, *Politics Among Nations*, 1948; ⚠️ 1948 date owner-only, not verified in this build): states act on power-defined interest; moral principles must be applied through circumstance, and prudence is the supreme political virtue. Liberal idealism (Kant, *Perpetual Peace*, 1795; UN Charter; R2P in the 2005 World Summit Outcome, paras 138-139): law and institutions domesticate anarchy. Constructivism (Alexander Wendt, 1992, "Anarchy is what States Make of It", *International Organization* 46(2), pp. 391-425, ✅ Wikipedia): interests are constituted by norms and identity.

**Breakdown:**
- Realism: "Morgenthau is not an amoralist"; prudence is moral mediation.
- Idealism: duties run to persons; risk of moral overreach.
- Constructivism: explains how ethical claims become interests.
- Limitation of constructivism: weak decision guidance for a specific dilemma.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wendt 1992 (✅ Wikipedia). Morgenthau 1948 (⚠️ owner-only). Kant 1795 (⚠️ owner-only; Wikipedia check did not complete).

**Links:** ETH-3024, ETH-3026, ETH-3029.

**Trap:** Saying realism says ethics is irrelevant. Morgenthau's fourth and sixth principles say ethics relocates into prudence and consequences.

**Mains hook:** The directive tests a conceptual relation: one tradition, one counter-tradition, one concept (dirty hands), one Indian anchor (Article 51, strategic autonomy). Qualified conclusion: ethics is not external to interest; treating it as external measures interest over too short a horizon.

**Back:** *"Realists weigh, idealists bind, constructivists make interests from norms."*

### Flashcard ETH-3026 — Limitation versus derogation (ICCPR Art. 4)

**Front:** Distinguish limitation from derogation and state the non-derogable core under ICCPR Art. 4(2).

**Definition (learn):** Limitation is the ordinary, permanent qualification of a right in normal times (in India, Art. 19(2)-(6)). Derogation is the temporary suspension of a treaty obligation during a proclaimed public emergency that threatens the life of the nation (ICCPR Art. 4; Indian Part XVIII, Art. 352-360). Art. 4(2) bars derogation from articles 6, 7, 8(1)-(2), 11, 15, 16 and 18 (life, torture, slavery, imprisonment for contract, retrospective criminal law, legal personality, thought conscience religion). (✅ owner 12, verbatim ICCPR text per owner.)

**Breakdown:**
- Limitation: prescribed by law, legitimate aim, necessity, proportionality, procedural safeguards.
- Derogation: proclaimed, strictly required, non-discriminatory, notified to the UN Secretary-General under Art. 4(3).
- Non-derogable core: Art. 4(2) list.
- Siracusa Principles (1984): persuasive, non-binding interpretive statement; ⚠️ not used for paragraph numbers.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ICCPR Art. 4(1)-(3). India acceded to the ICCPR in 1979 (⚠️ owner note: verify precise date).

**Links:** ETH-3027, ETH-3028.

**Trap:** "Derogation means rights are suspended." The core is non-derogable and every derogation still needs proclamation and necessity.

**Mains hook:** Use the two-layer grammar to show security is a limitation question under normal law and a derogation question only in a proclaimed emergency. Qualified conclusion: security and rights are a single question about least restrictive means.

**Back:** *"Limit in calm, derogate in storm, never touch the core six-to-eighteen."*

### Flashcard ETH-3027 — Puttaswamy proportionality ladder for State restrictions

**Front:** State the operative Indian proportionality test for a State restriction on rights after Justice K.S. Puttaswamy.

**Definition (learn):** *Justice K.S. Puttaswamy (Retd.) v. Union of India* was decided on 24 August 2017 by a nine-judge bench, unanimously holding privacy a fundamental right intrinsic to life and personal liberty under Art. 21 (and linked to Arts. 14 and 19 and Part III freedoms); it overruled *M.P. Sharma* and *Kharak Singh*. The operative test: legality, legitimate aim, rational connection, necessity or least restrictive means, proportionality stricto sensu, procedural safeguards. (✅ Wikipedia for citation, date, bench and holding; the six-step test is owner 12 and 13.)

**Breakdown:**
- Legality: a law must authorise the action.
- Legitimate aim: a stated purpose.
- Rational connection: the measure must further the aim.
- Necessity: least restrictive means.
- Stricto sensu: benefit outweighs intrusion.
- Procedural safeguards: review, notice and audit.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 August 2017; nine judges; (2017) 10 SCC 1; AIR 2017 SC 4161. (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3026, ETH-3028, ETH-3054 (DPDP), ETH-3035.

**Trap:** Saying national security is a complete answer. It is only a legitimate aim, the second of six steps.

**Mains hook:** Apply the six steps to a specific measure. Qualified conclusion: a rights restriction must be bounded, temporary and reviewable.

**Back:** *"Law, aim, link, least, balance, record."*

### Flashcard ETH-3028 — Non-refoulement: the refugee rule and India's status

**Front:** What is non-refoulement, which Convention article states it, and what is India's treaty position?

**Definition (learn):** Non-refoulement is the prohibition on returning a refugee to a place of threatened persecution. Article 33 of the 1951 Refugee Convention expresses it. The 1951 Convention was approved on 28 July 1951 and took effect in 1954; the 1967 Protocol came into force on 4 October 1967 (✅ Wikipedia). India is not a party to the 1951 Convention or the 1967 Protocol (⚠️ owner 12; the Wikipedia check did not confirm India's status).

**Breakdown:**
- Art. 33 of the 1951 Convention: no expulsion or return.
- India's answer: use NHRC v. State of Arunachal Pradesh for Art. 21 protection of non-citizens (⚠️ owner-only); treat Mohammad Salimullah as interim, not final.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28 July 1951; 1954 entry into force; 4 October 1967 (Protocol). (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3026, ETH-3019.

**Trap:** Stating that India is bound by the 1951 Convention because it signed the Universal Declaration. It is not a party.

**Mains hook:** Use non-refoulement as the "persecution" limit on expulsion. Qualified conclusion: India's protection of non-citizens runs through Art. 21 and domestic judicial practice, not the Convention.

**Back:** *"Thirty-three forbids the return; India is outside the treaty but not outside the law."*

### Flashcard ETH-3029 — Arms-export due diligence: SCOMET, EUC and the IMWG

**Front:** What are SCOMET, the IMWG and the End-Use-cum-End-User Certificate (EUC), and what does the EUC require?

**Definition (learn):** SCOMET (Special Chemicals, Organisms, Materials, Equipment and Technologies) is India's dual-use and munitions export-control list, notified as Appendix 3 to Schedule 2 of the ITC (HS) Classification of Export and Import Items, administered by DGFT under the Foreign Trade (Development and Regulation) Act, 1992. The Inter-Ministerial Working Group (IMWG) in DGFT decides SCOMET authorisations. The EUC is the operative due-diligence instrument: the end-user must use the item only for the stated purpose, not change or replicate it without Government of India consent, not re-transfer it without consent, and facilitate verification; the EUC names all supply-chain entities. (✅ owner 12.)

**Breakdown:**
- SCOMET: Appendix 3 to Schedule 2 of ITC (HS).
- Authority: DGFT; Foreign Trade (D&R) Act 1992.
- IMWG criteria: end-user credentials; chain integrity; end-use; risk of terrorists or non-State actors; the recipient's export controls; NSG, MTCR, Australia Group and Wassenaar lists.
- EUC conditions: stated purpose only; no modification or replication; no re-transfer without consent; verification facilitated.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Foreign Trade (D&R) Act 1992; Appendix 3 to Schedule 2 of ITC (HS). (✅ owner 12)

**Links:** ETH-3030 (MTCR), ETH-3031 (regime membership), ETH-3032 (staged due diligence), ETH-3033 (replaceability).

**Trap:** "An end-user certificate settles the matter." It is a conditioning instrument, not a guarantee; value depends on verification and follow-through.

**Mains hook:** Use the EUC plus post-shipment verification to show that due diligence is continuing control. Qualified conclusion: an EUC without verification is paperwork.

**Back:** *"Appendix three lists the goods; the certificate binds the buyer; the IMWG signs."*

### Flashcard ETH-3030 — MTCR Category I: the presumption of denial

**Front:** What are the MTCR Category I thresholds and what presumption applies to Category I transfers?

**Definition (learn):** Category I covers complete rocket and unmanned-aerial-vehicle systems (ballistic missiles, space launch vehicles, sounding rockets, cruise missiles, target and reconnaissance drones) capable of delivering a payload of at least 500 kg to a range of at least 300 km, plus major subsystems and production facilities. MTCR Guidelines: "particular restraint... regardless of their purpose", and a "strong presumption to deny" Category I transfers; production facilities are not authorised. (✅ owner 12; ✅ Wikipedia for the 500 kg / 300 km threshold; the MTCR was established in April 1987 by G7 countries.)

**Breakdown:**
- Thresholds: 500 kg payload; 300 km range.
- Presumption: deny regardless of purpose; rare exceptions with government undertakings.
- Production facilities for Category I: not authorised.
- Category II: licensing and restraint regime.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** MTCR founded April 1987 (✅ Wikipedia); 500 kg / 300 km (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** ETH-3029, ETH-3031.

**Trap:** "MTCR bans all missile exports." It creates a strong presumption of denial for Category I, with licensing for Category II.

**Mains hook:** Use the regardless-of-purpose rule to show a benign stated purpose does not relieve the presumption. Qualified conclusion: a hard threshold eliminates options.

**Back:** *"Five hundred kilos, three hundred kilometres: deny by presumption, whatever the purpose."*

### Flashcard ETH-3031 — India's regime-membership map: MTCR, Wassenaar, Australia Group, NSG, ATT and NPT

**Front:** State India's status in the MTCR, Wassenaar Arrangement, Australia Group, Nuclear Suppliers Group, Arms Trade Treaty and NPT.

**Definition (learn):** MTCR: member (27 June 2016) (✅ Wikipedia). Wassenaar Arrangement: participating State (admitted 7 December 2017) (✅ Wikipedia; founded 12 July 1996 in Wassenaar; 42 participating states). Australia Group: participant (joined 19 January 2018) (✅ Wikipedia; founded 1985). Nuclear Suppliers Group: not a member; India received a clean waiver on 6 September 2008 (✅ Wikipedia; NSG set up in 1974). Arms Trade Treaty: not a party or signatory (✅ Wikipedia; adopted 2 April 2013, in force 24 December 2014). NPT: not a party (⚠️ owner 12).

**Breakdown:**
- Adherence commitments (MTCR, Wassenaar, Australia Group) are not treaties and are implemented domestically through SCOMET.
- NSG waiver is not membership.
- ATT: normative reference only; India abstained in the 2013 vote (✅ Wikipedia).
- NPT: argue through MTCR restraint, UNSCR 1540 and regional stability.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed above. (✅ Wikipedia for each year except NPT, which is ⚠️ owner-only.)

**Links:** ETH-3029, ETH-3030.

**Trap:** "India is an NSG member" or "India is bound by the ATT." Both are wrong.

**Mains hook:** Use the map to avoid factual errors on international commitments. Qualified conclusion: India controls exports through national law, not through membership of every regime.

**Back:** *"Twenty-sixteen MTCR, two-seventeen Wassenaar, eighteen Australia Group; NSG waiver, not membership; ATT never."*

### Flashcard ETH-3032 — Staged due diligence in arms transfers (seven stages)

**Front:** Name the seven stages of arms-transfer due diligence and state the ethical point the diagram makes.

**Definition (learn):** (1) Pre-enquiry screening: classify the item; screen buyer, intermediaries and freight. (2) Risk assessment: embargo check; non-State-actor and proxy check; human-rights and IHL record; regional stability; diversion and re-export capability; economic compatibility. (3) Authorisation: DGFT/IMWG; end-use conditions. (4) Contractual conditioning: EUC naming every entity; no-modification clause; no-re-transfer undertaking; State assurance; verification clause. (5) Shipment control: consignee and transhipment checks. (6) Post-shipment verification: reports and on-site checks as the EUC permits. (7) Continuing oversight: consent over re-transfer; suspension on adverse information; sanction for non-reporting. (✅ owner 12.)

**Breakdown:**
- Seven stages, each with a distinct control.
- Continuing control, not a one-time signature.
- UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights (2011): identify, prevent, mitigate and account for impacts (⚠️ owner-only date).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** UNGPs 2011 (⚠️ owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3029, ETH-3033, ETH-3034.

**Trap:** "We only sold it" is not a UNGP-compliant position.

**Mains hook:** Use the diagram to answer "due diligence at each stage". Qualified conclusion: a supplier that cannot verify, condition or stop onward transfer has done paperwork, not due diligence.

**Back:** *"Screen, assess, authorise, condition, ship, verify, keep watching."*

### Flashcard ETH-3033 — The replaceability defence and its four replies

**Front:** What is the replaceability defence and what are its four replies?

**Definition (learn):** The argument that "if we refuse, the buyer will buy elsewhere; our refusal changes nothing". Four replies: (1) agency: responsibility attaches to one's own act; (2) proves too much: it would excuse every wrong in a competitive market; (3) empirically weak: substitution is rarely perfect and collective restraint is the mechanism of export-control regimes; (4) self-defeating: a reputation for diversion-risk sales raises the cost of future partnerships. (✅ owner 12; ⚠️ the analysis is owner-only.)

**Breakdown:**
- Replies run from moral (agency) to practical (self-defeating).
- Concession: where refusal is certainly futile, shift the action to regime information-exchange.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis only.

**Links:** ETH-3029, ETH-3032, ETH-3025.

**Trap:** Treating replaceability as a permissibility argument.

**Mains hook:** Concede the kernel, then shift the action to collective restraint. Qualified conclusion: refusal may be symbolic alone but becomes effective as collective action.

**Back:** *"Mine is mine, the bribe-taker would also argue, the rivals are weaker, and reputation is a cost."*

### Flashcard ETH-3034 — EU Common Position 2008/944/CFSP: the eight criteria (foreign comparator)

**Front:** Name the topics of the eight EU licensing criteria and state the Indian caution.

**Definition (learn):** The EU Common Position 2008/944/CFSP (amended 2019) is a foreign comparator, not binding on India. Its eight criteria: (1) international obligations; (2) human rights and IHL in the destination; (3) internal situation; (4) regional peace and stability; (5) supplier's national security and allies; (6) buyer's conduct towards the international community; (7) risk of diversion or undesirable re-export; (8) compatibility with the recipient's technical and economic capacity. (⚠️ owner 12; the Wikipedia check was not completed.)

**Breakdown:**
- Criterion 2: deny where clear risk of internal repression or serious IHL violations.
- Criterion 5: supplier self-interest may not override criteria 2 and 4.
- Criterion 8: "least diversion of human and economic resources for armaments".

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2008/944/CFSP; eight criteria (owner 12). (⚠️ owner-only)

**Links:** ETH-3029, ETH-3032.

**Trap:** Presenting the EU grid as Indian law. Route the Indian authority through SCOMET/IMWG and the UNSC/MTCR.

**Mains hook:** Use the eight criteria as an assessment template. Qualified conclusion: supplier interest is capped by human-rights and regional-stability criteria.

**Back:** *"Obligations, rights, internal, region, self, conduct, diversion, capacity: eight doors, one exit."*

### GROUP — 13 Emerging Ethics: Technology, AI and Environment

### Flashcard ETH-3035 — Emerging ethics: extending classical frameworks to fast-moving technology and environment

**Front:** What is "emerging ethics" in owner 13, and why does it need anticipatory reasoning?

**Definition (learn):** Emerging ethics extends duty, consequence and virtue frameworks (owner 08) to domains where technological or environmental change outruns settled rules: AI in administration, data and privacy, environmental clearance, and climate change. The response is anticipatory and precautionary, not only retrospective rule application. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis; the GS-IV syllabus frames ethics as dynamic.)

**Breakdown:**
- AI in administration: bias, reliability, opacity, accountability, human oversight.
- Data/privacy: consent, purpose limitation, minimisation.
- Environmental clearance in border areas: development vs ecology vs security.
- Climate: greed in the name of development (2024 GS-IV Q2(b)).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner 13 grounded in *Vellore Citizens' Welfare Forum* (1996), Brundtland (1987), Rio (1992), and 2024-2025 GS-IV PYQs. (✅ owner 13)

**Links:** ETH-3036 (precaution), ETH-3046 (AI bias), ETH-3054 (data/privacy), ETH-3044 (Vellore).

**Trap:** Treating AI and environment as unrelated topics. Owner 13 shows a common structure: deferred precaution against diffuse, delayed or voiceless harm.

**Mains hook:** Name the specific emerging risk, then apply precaution or CBDR by name. Qualified conclusion: build accountability and proportionality before scale-up, not after harm.

**Back:** *"Old frameworks, new speed: anticipate before you scale."*

### Flashcard ETH-3036 — Precautionary principle (Rio Declaration Principle 15)

**Front:** What does the precautionary principle require under Rio Principle 15 (1992), and what is the "approach" versus "principle" caution?

**Definition (learn):** Where there are threats of serious or irreversible damage, lack of full scientific certainty shall not be used as a reason for postponing cost-effective measures to prevent environmental degradation. (✅ Wikipedia for the Rio Declaration, 1992, and Principle 15.) The classic Rio wording speaks of a "precautionary approach"; many texts call it a principle.

**Breakdown:**
- Trigger: serious or irreversible threat.
- Standard: uncertainty is not a reason to delay cost-effective protection.
- Indian law: applied in *Vellore* (ETH-3044).
- AI analogy: applies by analogy, not as a binding Indian AI rule (owner 13).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rio Declaration 1992; Principle 15 (✅ Wikipedia). Owner 13 caution: the principle is an "approach" in the Rio text.

**Links:** ETH-3035, ETH-3037, ETH-3044.

**Trap:** "Precaution requires certainty of harm before acting." It applies precisely because certainty is absent. Also: precaution does not mean stop all development.

**Mains hook:** Name precaution by name and show it is proportionate. Qualified conclusion: precaution justifies proportionate protection, not a blanket development veto.

**Back:** *"Serious harm, no certainty: act at a cost you can bear."*

### Flashcard ETH-3037 — CBDR and CBDR-RC (common but differentiated responsibilities)

**Front:** Where does CBDR appear, and how does the Paris Agreement phrase it?

**Definition (learn):** All states share responsibility for climate change, but responsibilities are differentiated by historical contribution and respective capabilities. Rio Principle 7; UNFCCC Art. 3.1 ("on the basis of equity and in accordance with their common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities"; ✅ Wikipedia) and Art. 4.1. Paris Agreement Art. 2(2) adds "in the light of different national circumstances" (CBDR-RC). Paris was adopted on 12 December 2015 at Le Bourget and entered into force on 4 November 2016 (✅ Wikipedia). The Art. 2(2) wording itself was not verified in this build (⚠️).

**Breakdown:**
- Rio Principle 7 (1992).
- UNFCCC Arts 3.1 and 4.1.
- Paris Art. 2(2): CBDR-RC.
- Operationalised through differentiated NDCs.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Paris 12 December 2015 (adopted); 4 November 2016 (in force). (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3036, ETH-3038, ETH-3035.

**Trap:** "CBDR means developing countries have no obligations." It means differentiated, capability-linked obligations.

**Mains hook:** Use CBDR-RC to state India's position without denying its responsibility. Qualified conclusion: the ethical answer is proportionate, capability-linked responsibility, not zero or identical duties.

**Back:** *"Common, differentiated, capable, national: the climate equity grammar."*

### Flashcard ETH-3038 — Sustainable development and the Brundtland definition

**Front:** Give the Brundtland definition of sustainable development and its source.

**Definition (learn):** "Development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs." From *Our Common Future*, the report of the World Commission on Environment and Development (also called the Brundtland Commission), published in 1987. (✅ Wikipedia)

**Breakdown:**
- Two halves: present needs; future capacity.
- Not a one-sided override of development or of environment.
- Bridging concept between development and ecological limits.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1987; World Commission on Environment and Development; *Our Common Future*. (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3037, ETH-3039, ETH-3044.

**Trap:** "Sustainable development means environment always yields to development (or the reverse)." Brundtland holds both needs together.

**Mains hook:** Use Brundtland to frame a development-versus-ecology dilemma. Qualified conclusion: sustainable development is a balance with a future-capacity constraint.

**Back:** *"Meet today's needs without spending tomorrow's capacity."*

### Flashcard ETH-3039 — Intergenerational equity and the public-trust idea

**Front:** What is intergenerational equity, and how does the public-trust idea express it?

**Definition (learn):** The ethical obligation to leave future generations no worse off in their capacity to meet their needs. Owner 13 notes the public-trust doctrine expresses it: the present generation holds natural resources in trust for future generations, not as exhaustible private property. The public-trust doctrine descends from Roman *res communes omnium* ("things held in common by all", such as air, flowing water, the sea and the seashore). (⚠️ owner 13 for intergenerational equity; ✅ Wikipedia for *res communes omnium* and the public-trust origin.)

**Breakdown:**
- Temporal extension of public interest.
- Public trust: State as trustee of natural resources.
- Indian application: *M.C. Mehta v. Kamal Nath* (see ETH-3073).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** *res communes omnium* (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** ETH-3038, ETH-3073, ETH-3014.

**Trap:** Treating intergenerational equity as a settled Indian statutory duty. It is an ethical principle with judicial and constitutional echoes.

**Mains hook:** Use it for long-horizon consequentialism. Qualified conclusion: future harms must not be discounted merely because they are less visible.

**Back:** *"We hold it in trust; the next generation is a beneficiary, not a footnote."*

### Flashcard ETH-3040 — Polluter-pays principle

**Front:** State the polluter-pays principle and its place in *Vellore*.

**Definition (learn):** The party causing pollution bears the cost of remedying or compensating the resulting environmental harm, not the public exchequer or affected citizens. The Supreme Court applied it in *Vellore Citizens' Welfare Forum* (1996) as part of Indian environmental law (owner 13). The principle is treated as a regional custom in most OECD and EU countries (✅ Wikipedia). Wikipedia does not give an OECD origin date (⚠️ year not verified).

**Breakdown:**
- Ex post: liability for harm caused.
- Contrast: precaution is ex ante (ETH-3036).
- Applied in *Vellore* alongside sustainable development and precaution.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** OECD-backed custom (✅ Wikipedia). Year not verified (⚠️).

**Links:** ETH-3036, ETH-3044, ETH-3045.

**Trap:** "Precaution and polluter-pays are the same." Precaution governs whether to proceed; polluter-pays governs liability for harm already caused.

**Mains hook:** Separate ex ante and ex post. Qualified conclusion: polluter-pays is necessary but not sufficient to prevent harm.

**Back:** *"Whoever pollutes, pays after; precaution decides before."*

### Flashcard ETH-3041 — Environmental justice and free, prior and informed consent (FPIC)

**Front:** Define environmental justice and FPIC, and state the UNDRIP articles that express FPIC (with the owner conflict flagged).

**Definition (learn):** Environmental justice: the fair distribution of environmental burdens and benefits, with attention to the poor, tribal and marginalised communities. FPIC: a community affected by a project must consent before the decision is finalised, without coercion, on the basis of full disclosure. (⚠️ owner 13 for environmental justice; ✅ Wikipedia for FPIC definition.)

**Breakdown:**
- Indian statutory echoes: Gram Sabha under FRA 2006 and PESA 1996; RFCTLARR 2013 consent thresholds (ETH-3065).
- UNDRIP (2007): a General Assembly declaration, not a treaty; India does not recognise a distinct "indigenous peoples" category domestically (⚠️ owner 13).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Conflict: owner 13 cites UNDRIP Arts 10, 19 and 32(2) for FPIC. Wikipedia's FPIC article says Arts 11, 19, 28 and 29 explicitly use the term and Art. 32 is a consultation requirement without the term. Flag the conflict; check the text before quoting an article number.

**Links:** ETH-3063, ETH-3064, ETH-3066.

**Trap:** Describing UNDRIP as binding on India. It is a normative standard only.

**Mains hook:** Use FPIC to show consent is about decision competence, not just a hearing. Qualified conclusion: consultation and consent are different rungs.

**Back:** *"Before, freely, informed: consent is a decision, not a meeting."*

### Flashcard ETH-3042 — Three normative orientations toward nature

**Front:** Distinguish anthropocentric, biocentric and ecocentric orientations, with the caution for each.

**Definition (learn):** Anthropocentric: nature has value chiefly for human interests; protection is instrumental. Biocentric: all living organisms have inherent worth; human interests do not automatically override other species. Ecocentric: value attaches to the ecosystem as an interdependent whole. (⚠️ owner 13. Wikipedia: Paul W. Taylor is most associated with biocentrism, in *Respect for Nature* (1986); ✅ Wikipedia.)

**Breakdown:**
- Anthropocentric: wetland protected because it recharges groundwater.
- Biocentric: species protected for its own sake.
- Ecocentric: river flow regime protected as a whole, not only the fish.
- Stewardship (Gandhian trusteeship) is a middle path but still centres human agency.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Taylor 1986 (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** ETH-3043, ETH-3044, ETH-3039.

**Trap:** "Ecocentrism is the only defensible position." GS-IV rewards applying the orientation the scenario calls for.

**Mains hook:** Name the orientation and its limit. Qualified conclusion: India's framework is a sustainable-development and stewardship synthesis.

**Back:** *"Human, species, system: three lenses on the same forest."*

### Flashcard ETH-3043 — Deep ecology and the stewardship caution

**Front:** What is deep ecology, who named it, and why should a GS-IV answer not treat it as the correct view?

**Definition (learn):** Deep ecology is a biocentric or ecocentric position that rejects anthropocentrism as the root cause of the environmental crisis; Arne Naess's term. Owner 13 notes it is one school of thought, not the uncontested correct one. India's constitutional and statutory framework (Art. 48A, Art. 51A(g), Environment (Protection) Act 1986) is anthropocentric-cum-stewardship in structure. (⚠️ owner 13; the Naess date was not verified in this build.)

**Breakdown:**
- Deep ecology: strong ecocentric claim.
- Stewardship: humans as trustees, not owners.
- Indian law: "for the sake of" present and future generations.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Art. 48A and 51A(g) (⚠️ owner-only wording); EPA 1986 (⚠️ owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3042, ETH-3044.

**Trap:** "India's law is deep-ecological." It is not, in the strict Naess sense.

**Mains hook:** Present deep ecology as one school. Qualified conclusion: over-claiming any single orientation as correct is itself a trap.

**Back:** *"Deep is one school; India is a steward, not a deep ecologist."*

### Flashcard ETH-3044 — *Vellore Citizens' Welfare Forum v. Union of India* (1996)

**Front:** What did *Vellore* hold, and what are its limits?

**Definition (learn):** Leather tanneries in Tamil Nadu's Vellore district discharged untreated effluent into the Palar river. The Supreme Court held that sustainable development, the precautionary principle and the polluter-pays principle are part of the environmental law of India. It directed closure of non-complying tanneries pending treatment and required an authority under the Environment (Protection) Act to assess compensation. (⚠️ owner 13 citation (1996) 5 SCC 647, decided 28 August 1996; not verified in this build.)

**Breakdown:**
- Holding: three principles are part of Indian law.
- Remedy: closure, effluent treatment, compensation authority.
- Limits: specific industry and river; the NGT now applies the principles; does not resolve every precautionary dispute (e.g., AI).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (1996) 5 SCC 647; 28 August 1996 (⚠️ owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3036, ETH-3040, ETH-3045.

**Trap:** Calling *Vellore* a general environmental code. It is a Supreme Court judgment on tannery pollution that established principles.

**Mains hook:** State its actual holding, then its limit. Qualified conclusion: *Vellore* gives the principles legal force, enforced now largely through the NGT.

**Back:** *"Tanneries, Palar, three principles, and a compensation authority."*

### Flashcard ETH-3045 — National Green Tribunal and the principles

**Front:** When was the NGT constituted, and under which section does it apply the principles?

**Definition (learn):** The National Green Tribunal, a specialised tribunal, was constituted in 2010 (owner 13). It applies sustainable development, precaution and polluter-pays under NGT Act s.20. It does not displace the Supreme Court's constitutional jurisdiction or erase *Vellore*. (⚠️ owner 13 — s.20 and 2010 not verified in this build; the Wikipedia check did not complete.)

**Breakdown:**
- Specialised: environmental disputes.
- Principles: sustainable development, precaution, polluter-pays (s.20).
- Relation to Supreme Court: parallel, not replacing.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2010; NGT Act s.20 (⚠️ owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3044, ETH-3040.

**Trap:** "The NGT replaced the Supreme Court in environmental law."

**Mains hook:** Use the NGT as the institutional operationalisation of precaution. Qualified conclusion: enforcement capacity, not the principle, is the binding constraint.

**Back:** *"Ten-ten tribunal, section twenty, same three principles."*

### Flashcard ETH-3046 — Algorithmic bias, proxy discrimination and the "delete the field" fallacy

**Front:** Define algorithmic bias and proxy discrimination, and state why deleting a protected field does not solve the problem.

**Definition (learn):** Algorithmic bias is systematic, unfair skew in an AI system's outputs from biased training data, feature selection or labelling. Proxy discrimination is discrimination through a permitted variable (postal code, surname, occupation, electricity consumption) that correlates with a protected attribute such as caste, religion, class or gender. Deleting the protected field does not remove the effect. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis.)

**Breakdown:**
- Bias originates in socially skewed data.
- Proxy variables reconstruct protected attributes.
- Fairness must be tested on outcomes across groups.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis; no source-verified figure.

**Links:** ETH-3047, ETH-3048, ETH-3049.

**Trap:** "AI is more objective than humans." Objectivity claims require independent scrutiny.

**Mains hook:** Name the bias and the proxy. Qualified conclusion: test error distribution across groups, not aggregate accuracy.

**Back:** *"Remove the caste column; the postcode still knows."*

### Flashcard ETH-3047 — Feedback loop and the Oakland predictive-policing example

**Front:** What is a feedback loop in predictive policing, and what does the Oakland example show?

**Definition (learn):** A predictive system trained on past enforcement records directs patrols to areas already over-policed; new records confirm the prediction. It learns where police went, not where crime occurred. Lum and Isaac (2016) reported that applying PredPol to drug-crime data in Oakland sent police disproportionately to Black and low-income neighbourhoods, although survey data showed drug use was spread more evenly. (✅ Wikipedia.)

**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: dirty data, then patrols, then more data.
- Oakland (Lum and Isaac, 2016): disproportion despite even drug-use survey evidence.
- Critics: loops are hard to fix with technical changes alone (Barocas, Hardt, Narayanan).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2016 (Lum and Isaac). (✅ Wikipedia)

**Links:** ETH-3046, ETH-3049, ETH-3062.

**Trap:** Treating rising detections in a targeted area as proof the prediction was correct.

**Mains hook:** Name the feedback loop as the mechanism. Qualified conclusion: rotation and outcome audits can break the loop.

**Back:** *"The system learns where the police went."*

### Flashcard ETH-3048 — Place-based versus person-based prediction

**Front:** Distinguish place-based from person-based prediction and state why person-based carries a higher ethical threshold.

**Definition (learn):** Place-based forecasting predicts where and when an incident is likely and directs patrols; the consequence attaches to an area. Person-based scoring predicts whether a named individual will offend and places them on lists; the consequence attaches to a person for an act not committed. (⚠️ owner 13 table; Wikipedia's predictive-policing article uses a RAND typology of place, offender, perpetrator-identity and victim forecasts, ✅ Wikipedia.)

**Breakdown:**
- Place-based: resource allocation; presumption of innocence not directly engaged.
- Person-based: stops, visits, listing, opposition to bail; presumption engaged.
- EU AI Act Art. 5(1)(d): a foreign comparator prohibiting profiling-based criminal risk assessment, not applicable in India (⚠️ owner 13; not verified in this build).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only for the EU comparator.

**Links:** ETH-3049, ETH-3047.

**Trap:** Treating a person-based score as just another hot-spot map.

**Mains hook:** Use the distinction to show the threshold rises. Qualified conclusion: place-based is manageable with transparency; person-based needs independent grounds.

**Back:** *"Places get patrols; people get lists, and lists need grounds."*

### Flashcard ETH-3049 — A score may direct attention but may not constitute suspicion

**Front:** State the operating rule that owner 13 extracts for algorithmic scores in policing.

**Definition (learn):** An algorithmic score may direct attention and allocate resources. It may never constitute suspicion, supply grounds for a coercive action, or be recorded as a reason in an order. The officer must state facts that would have justified the action without the score. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis, drawing on the EU comparator.)

**Breakdown:**
- Permitted: allocation of patrols.
- Prohibited: suspicion, coercion, recorded reason.
- Test: independent facts sufficient without the score.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis.

**Links:** ETH-3048, ETH-3050, ETH-3062.

**Trap:** "The model is 95% accurate, so it is fair." Accuracy says nothing about the distribution of errors.

**Mains hook:** Use the rule as the operating limit. Qualified conclusion: the anticipatory-guilt risk is removed by independent grounds.

**Back:** *"The score points the way; it cannot make the arrest."*

### Flashcard ETH-3050 — Automation bias

**Front:** Define automation bias and give the documented pilot example.

**Definition (learn):** The tendency to over-trust a machine-generated recommendation and under-weigh contrary evidence, which can hollow out "human in the loop" safeguards into rubber-stamping. (✅ Wikipedia: people prefer automated recommendations even when contrary human information is accurate; errors are commission (following a directive) and omission (missing an unmonitored problem).)

**Breakdown:**
- Commission error: follow the automated directive.
- Omission error: miss a problem the system did not flag.
- Pilot study (Wikipedia): every pilot given a false automated engine-shutdown alert shut the engine off, yet insisted in interviews they would have reduced power instead.
- Safeguard: log override and acceptance rates both.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pilot study described in the article (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** ETH-3049, ETH-3051, ETH-3055.

**Trap:** "A human in the loop means accountability is preserved." Only if the human has information, time, authority and incentive to disagree.

**Mains hook:** Name automation bias and design against it. Qualified conclusion: oversight must be engineered, not asserted.

**Back:** *"Every pilot shut the engine off, and every pilot swore they would not."*

### Flashcard ETH-3051 — Explainability, contestability and the human-in-the-loop safeguard

**Front:** Distinguish explainability from contestability and state what the human-in-the-loop safeguard must include.

**Definition (learn):** Explainability is a property of a model (its decision process is understandable and auditable). Contestability is a right of the affected person: learn an automated system produced an adverse outcome, obtain reasons in intelligible form, submit counter-evidence and obtain human re-decision. Human-in-the-loop review is a safeguard only if the reviewer can disagree. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis; "explainable AI" as a term is standard (✅ general usage, not fetched).)

**Breakdown:**
- Explainability: precondition for accountability.
- Contestability: a person's right, distinct from model property.
- Human-in-the-loop: must record reasons for overrides and acceptances, and monitor rates.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis.

**Links:** ETH-3050, ETH-3046, ETH-3062.

**Trap:** "Explainability is enough; the citizen does not need a right of challenge."

**Mains hook:** Argue for contestability with explainability as the precondition. Qualified conclusion: a reasoned, appealable decision is the test.

**Back:** *"Explain the model; contest the outcome; the human must be able to say no."*

### Flashcard ETH-3052 — India AI Governance Guidelines (MeitY, 5 November 2025)

**Front:** What are the India AI Governance Guidelines, when were they released, and what is their legal status?

**Definition (learn):** The MeitY guidelines *Enabling Safe and Trusted AI Innovation* (released 5 November 2025) set out a policy framework covering fairness, accountability, safety, inclusivity, transparency, risk classification, human oversight, testing and grievance redress. Owner 13 gives seven "sutras" (trust as foundation; people first; innovation over restraint; fairness and equity; accountability; understandable by design; safety, resilience and sustainability) and six pillars. They are recommendations, action plans and voluntary commitments, not a comprehensive AI statute. (📰 owner 13.)

**Breakdown:**
- Non-statutory framework.
- Seven sutras; six pillars (owner).
- Accountability and grievance redress are the institutional anchors.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5 November 2025. (📰 owner 13; ⚠️ time-varying, not re-verified.)

**Links:** ETH-3053, ETH-3054, ETH-3051.

**Trap:** "India has enacted a comprehensive AI law." It has not, on the owner's account.

**Mains hook:** Cite the guidelines for institutional safeguards, not as statute. Qualified conclusion: policy guidance must be backed by enforceable rules to bind.

**Back:** *"Seven sutras, six pillars, zero statute."*

### Flashcard ETH-3053 — UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI and the OECD AI Principles

**Front:** When was the UNESCO AI ethics Recommendation adopted, and what are the OECD AI Principles' year and updates?

**Definition (learn):** UNESCO's *Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence* was adopted on 23 November 2021 (owner 13). The OECD AI Principles were issued in 2019 and updated in May 2024; they name inclusive growth and well-being, human rights and democratic values, transparency and explainability, robustness and safety, and accountability (owner 13). Both predate and inform India's framework. (📰 owner 13; ⚠️ not re-verified in this build.)

**Breakdown:**
- UNESCO: normative vocabulary.
- OECD: five value-based principles.
- Use as citable normative reference, not Indian law.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 23 November 2021; 2019 (updated May 2024). (⚠️ owner-only)

**Links:** ETH-3052, ETH-3051.

**Trap:** Citing the UNESCO Recommendation as binding on India. It is a normative instrument.

**Mains hook:** Name the instrument and its status. Qualified conclusion: international principles inform domestic design.

**Back:** *"Twenty-one UNESCO, nineteen OECD, updated twenty-four."*

### Flashcard ETH-3054 — DPDP Act 2023 and *Puttaswamy*: constitutional privacy versus statutory data protection

**Front:** What is the relationship between *Puttaswamy* and the DPDP Act, 2023, and what is the commencement status flagged by the owner?

**Definition (learn):** *Puttaswamy* (24 August 2017, nine judges) is the constitutional holding that privacy is a fundamental right under Art. 21 and subject to a proportionality test for restrictions. The Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 is the ordinary statute for personal-data processing, built on consent, purpose limitation and data minimisation, with a Data Protection Board of India (s.18). The Act received assent on 11 August 2023 as Act No. 22 of 2023 (✅ Wikipedia). Owner 13 says G.S.R. 843(E) (13 November 2025) commenced only specified provisions; core duties begin on 13 May 2027 (📰 owner 13; ⚠️ time-varying, verify before use).

**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional: Puttaswamy.
- Statutory: DPDP Act.
- Ethical duty exists before and independent of the statutory duty.
- Phased commencement: do not call future duties enforceable now.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 August 2017; 11 August 2023; Act No. 22 of 2023; Data Protection Board of India (s.18). (✅ Wikipedia for 2017 and 2023 items; ⚠️ commencement dates owner-only.)

**Links:** ETH-3027, ETH-3052, ETH-3055.

**Trap:** "The DPDP Act has replaced Puttaswamy." It has not; Puttaswamy is the constitutional foundation.

**Mains hook:** Separate constitutional principle from statutory status. Qualified conclusion: the duty of dignity and purpose limitation applies now, whatever the commencement schedule.

**Back:** *"Puttaswamy is the constitution's privacy; DPDP is the statute's plumbing."*

### Flashcard ETH-3055 — The four distinct wrongs of AI-assisted work

**Front:** Name the four distinct wrongs in AI-assisted academic or professional work and why they must be kept apart.

**Definition (learn):** (1) Misattribution of authorship: a false claim about who is answerable. (2) Abdication of verification: passing on unverified fluent output. (3) Undisclosed substitution of effort where effort is what is assessed. (4) Plagiarism and derivative appropriation: others' IP without attribution. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis.) The integrity question is answerability: "whose judgement stands behind this?"

**Breakdown:**
- Wrong 1: truthfulness about provenance.
- Wrong 2: duty of care and competence.
- Wrong 3: integrity of evaluation.
- Wrong 4: credit and IP.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner analysis.

**Links:** ETH-3056 (ICMJE), ETH-3050 (automation bias), ETH-3054.

**Trap:** Collapsing all AI use into "plagiarism". Plagiarism is only one of four, often not the operative one.

**Mains hook:** Separate the four, then apply the answerability test. Qualified conclusion: the tool may draft; only a named human can be answerable.

**Back:** *"Who signed, who checked, who was assessed, whose words."*

### Flashcard ETH-3056 — ICMJE authorship criteria and the chatbot rule

**Front:** State the four ICMJE authorship criteria and the chatbot rule.

**Definition (learn):** ICMJE (International Committee of Medical Journal Editors) authorship criteria, all four required: (i) substantial contributions to conception or design, or to data acquisition, analysis or interpretation; (ii) drafting or critical review; (iii) final approval of the version; (iv) agreement to be accountable for all aspects of the work. ICMJE states chatbots should not be listed as authors because they cannot be responsible for accuracy, integrity and originality; authors must disclose AI use. (✅ owner 13 quoting ICMJE recommendations.)

**Breakdown:**
- Four criteria: contribute, draft, approve, be accountable.
- Chatbots fail the accountability limb.
- Disclose AI use in the cover letter and manuscript.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ICMJE recommendations (owner text quoted). (✅ owner 13)

**Links:** ETH-3055, ETH-3051.

**Trap:** "A chatbot can be an author if it contributed text." Authorship requires accountability.

**Mains hook:** Apply the ICMJE logic by analogy to administrative notes (the officer's own application of mind). Qualified conclusion: the principle extends beyond journals only by analogy; say so.

**Back:** *"Four boxes to tick, and a chatbot cannot be accountable."*

### Flashcard ETH-3057 — Digital gender divide as an emerging-ethics dimension

**Front:** How does the digital divide re-create an older gender-equity concern?

**Definition (learn):** Unequal digital access and digital literacy between men and women can widen the substantive-equality gap that formal legal equality does not fix. Owner 13 cross-links this to the 2020 GS-IV Q5(a) gender-inequality demand, owned in full by owner 02 (Section 8a). Owner 13 adds the technology-specific channel: digital access and algorithmic bias in AI-assisted services. (⚠️ owner 13 analysis.)

**Breakdown:**
- Formal equality vs substantive equality.
- Digital access and literacy channel.
- Algorithmic bias channel.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner cross-link; no figure given.

**Links:** ETH-3046, ETH-3035.

**Trap:** Treating digital expansion as automatically equalising.

**Mains hook:** Name the channel (access or algorithm). Qualified conclusion: technology can reproduce exclusion unless access is designed in.

**Back:** *"Connection is not equality until everyone can log on."*

### Flashcard ETH-3058 — Declaration of Helsinki (WMA): adoption and the 2024 revision

**Front:** When was the Declaration of Helsinki adopted, and when was the most recent revision?

**Definition (learn):** The World Medical Association's Declaration of Helsinki, adopted in 1964 at the WMA meeting in Helsinki. The most recent (eighth) revision was approved at the General Assembly in October 2024, at the 75th WMA meeting in Helsinki (✅ Wikipedia). It is a professional standard, not a statute. Owner 13 quotes ¶¶19-20, 23, 25, 27 and 37 (⚠️ owner-only quotations; the ¶37 wording was not verified in this build).

**Breakdown:**
- Independent ethics review (¶23).
- Individual consent cannot be replaced by community assent (¶25).
- Dependent relationships (¶27).
- Unproven interventions (¶37).

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1964; October 2024 (✅ Wikipedia).

**Links:** ETH-3059, ETH-3060, ETH-3061.

**Trap:** Citing Helsinki as Indian law. Indian force runs through the NDCT Rules 2019 and ICMR guidance (owner 13).

**Mains hook:** Use Helsinki as the professional threshold; cite Indian law for enforceability. Qualified conclusion: urgency changes the speed of review, not its existence.

**Back:** *"Sixty-four in Helsinki, twenty-four revised; consent is individual."*

### Flashcard ETH-3059 — Four routes for medical interventions: which one do the facts describe?

**Front:** Name the four routes (standard care, clinical research, compassionate or unproven use, emergency public-health measure) and their key limits.

**Definition (learn):** Standard care: approved intervention with established evidence; ordinary consent. Clinical research: knowledge-generating intent; full informed consent; independent ethics committee with suspension powers; registration. Compassionate or unproven use (Helsinki ¶37): an individual patient, approved options inadequate, trial enrolment impossible, expert advice and informed consent; never a programme. Emergency public-health measure: statutory authority; still proportionate; time-bound and reviewed; never research by another name. (✅ owner 13.)

**Breakdown:**
- Key test: intent to generate knowledge and use of an unvalidated intervention.
- Pilot on a captive or dependent population is "research wearing the clothes of service delivery".

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only table.

**Links:** ETH-3058, ETH-3060.

**Trap:** "It is a service pilot, not research." The label does not matter; intent and unvalidated use do.

**Mains hook:** Identify the route first. Qualified conclusion: compassionate use is individual, never population-wide.

**Back:** *"Standard, research, individual, emergency: never a pilot on the captive."*

### Flashcard ETH-3060 — Vulnerability taxonomy and the Belmont principles

**Front:** Name the three Belmont principles and the vulnerability sources an answer should use.

**Definition (learn):** The Belmont Report lists three principles: respect for persons, beneficence, and justice (fair distribution of research burdens and benefits). Owner 13 uses five vulnerability sources: capacity-based (children; cognitively impaired); dependency-based (patients of the investigator, employees, students, prisoners); deprivation-based; structural (Scheduled Tribes, manual scavengers, trafficked persons, undocumented migrants); situational (disaster or conflict). (✅ Wikipedia for the Belmont principles.)

**Breakdown:**
- Respect for persons: autonomy and informed consent.
- Beneficence: maximise benefit, minimise harm.
- Justice: fair distribution.
- Vulnerability: a property of situations and relationships.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Belmont Report principles (✅ Wikipedia). Report date: Wikipedia gives 1978 (released 30 September 1978) and Federal Register publication in 1979; owner 13 gives 1979 (⚠️ conflict, flagged).

**Links:** ETH-3058, ETH-3059, ETH-3061.

**Trap:** "Vulnerability is a property of persons." It is a property of situations and relationships.

**Mains hook:** Use the three principles to show justice is about burdens and benefits. Qualified conclusion: protection comes from structure, not good intention.

**Back:** *"Respect, do good, share fairly: and see the dependency."*

### Flashcard ETH-3061 — Nuremberg Code (1947): voluntary consent

**Front:** What did the Nuremberg Code establish about research subjects?

**Definition (learn):** The Nuremberg Code (1947) made voluntary consent of the human subject absolutely essential, required the subject to be free to withdraw, and required experiments to yield fruitful results unobtainable by other means. (⚠️ owner 13 date; the Wikipedia check did not complete in this build.)

**Breakdown:**
- Consent: voluntary, absolutely essential.
- Withdrawal: freedom.
- Results: fruitful and unobtainable otherwise.

**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1947 (⚠️ owner-only).

**Links:** ETH-3058, ETH-3060.

**Trap:** Treating Nuremberg as a binding treaty. It is a foundational ethical code.

**Mains hook:** Use consent as the first floor of research ethics. Qualified conclusion: consent alone is not sufficient; review and justice are also required.

**Back:** *"Forty-seven: consent is absolute, and the subject may leave."*

### Flashcard ETH-3062 — Surveillance proportionality and the contestability architecture for predictive systems

**Front:** Apply the Puttaswamy steps to a predictive-surveillance deployment and state the operational architecture the owner recommends.

**Definition (learn):** The six steps (legality; legitimate aim; rational connection; necessity and least restrictive means; proportionality stricto sensu assessed group-wise; procedural safeguards) must each have a specific showing, for example a statutory basis for this use, evidence independent of the system's own output, and demonstrated failure of less intrusive alternatives. The architecture: before deployment (published purpose and legal basis; independent impact assessment; disparate-impact testing on group-wise error rates); in operation (score may allocate patrols but not constitute suspicion; officer records independent grounds; override and acceptance rates logged; rotation of hot-spot designations); after adverse outcome (notice; intelligible reasons; counter-evidence; human re-decision by a different officer; independent grievance); periodic (sunset clause; external audit). (⚠️ owner 13 analysis.)

**Breakdown:**
- Six-step surveillance ladder.
- Four-phase architecture.
