- Employer action: within sixty days of the IC's recommendation.
- Drafting note (⚠️ owner): Section 4(1) uses both "Internal Complaints Committee" and "Internal Committee" for one body; "IC" is used as the consistent short form.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.1). Statutory text not fetched in this build.
**Links:** ETH-6029, ETH-6031.
**Trap:** Assuming the statute alone guarantees an impartial IC. The owner's Sec. 14.6 case shows a compromised presiding officer.
**Mains hook:** Process versus effectiveness. Qualified: procedure is mandatory, but impartiality depends on who sits on the committee.
**Back:** *"Ten staff, half women, three months to complain, ninety days to inquire, sixty to act."*

### Flashcard ETH-6031 — From Vishaka Guidelines to POSH Act: judicial interim to statutory process
**Front:** How does the POSH Act 2013 relate to the Vishaka Guidelines, and what limit survives codification?
**Definition (learn):** The 2013 Act codified the 1997 guidelines, replacing interim directions with a statutory committee-based process. The Act supplies the procedure; it does not by itself ensure impartial members.
**Breakdown:**
- 1997: judicial guidelines, interim, sixteen years.
- 2013: statute with IC, timelines and external member.
- Surviving limit: effective redress depends on the actual composition and impartiality of the IC.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.1, Must-Know Facts).
**Links:** ETH-6029, ETH-6030.
**Trap:** "The POSH Act made Vishaka irrelevant." The statute codified the guidelines; their judicial origin is still the historical point.
**Mains hook:** Assess how far the Act addressed the guidelines' limitations (owner probable question). Qualified: procedure is necessary, not sufficient.
**Back:** *"Court drafted interim rules; Parliament made them statute with a committee."*

### Flashcard ETH-6032 — ICMR National Ethical Guidelines (2017): research consent and vulnerable participants
**Front:** What do the ICMR National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical and Health Research (2017) require?
**Definition (learn):** Ethical guidelines for research involving human participants, administered through each institution's Institutional Ethics Committee (IEC).
**Breakdown:**
- Informed consent: documented, comprehensible process (Section 2.2).
- Vulnerable participants (Section 2.9): children, pregnant women, prisoners, persons with cognitive impairment, and the economically or socially disadvantaged.
- Oversight: continuing IEC oversight, with full and undistorted reporting of trial data to the IEC.
- Limit (⚠️): not a single central regulator with uniform enforcement across sites.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2017 (✅ owner 23 Sec. 4.1). Section numbers as cited by owner (not fetched).
**Links:** ETH-6046, ETH-6029.
**Trap:** Citing an IEC in a surgical-consent case where no research is involved (ETH-6046).
**Mains hook:** Research ethics depends on the rigour of each IEC. Qualified: guidelines are only as strong as their institutional enforcement.
**Back:** *"Consent in plain words, extra care for the vulnerable, and the IEC watches every trial."*

### Flashcard ETH-6033 — NHRC v. State of Arunachal Pradesh (1996): Article 21 for every person
**Front:** What did NHRC v. State of Arunachal Pradesh (1996) hold, and what does it not hold?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court held Article 21 protects every person in India, not only citizens, and directed the State to protect Chakma and Hajong refugees from threatened eviction and violence and to forward their citizenship applications without obstruction.
**Breakdown:**
- Citation: (1996) 1 SCC 742; AIR 1996 SC 1234; decided 9 January 1996.
- Precedent cited: Louis De Raedt v. Union of India (1991), protecting foreign nationals under Article 21.
- Limit: protects life, liberty and due process for non-citizens; does not create a general right to remain in India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.2). Date and citation as owner states; not fetch-verified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-6034, ETH-6035.
**Trap:** Reading Article 21 as a right against removal or to remain.
**Mains hook:** Due process for non-citizens. Qualified by the Salimullah interim reasoning (ETH-6034).
**Back:** *"Life and liberty for every person; the right to stay is another question."*

### Flashcard ETH-6034 — Mohammad Salimullah v. Union of India (2021): interim refusal of stay
**Front:** What did the Supreme Court's 8 April 2021 order in Mohammad Salimullah decide, and what is its status?
**Definition (learn):** An interim order declining a stay on the deportation of a group of detained Rohingya individuals, while the underlying petition remained pending.
**Breakdown:**
- Status: interim, not a final judgment on the writ.
- Reasoning: Articles 14 and 21 protect everyone; the right not to be deported is ancillary to Article 19(1)(e) (right to reside or settle), which belongs to citizens.
- Treaty point: India is not a party to the 1951 Refugee Convention or its 1967 Protocol, so the Court reasoned non-refoulement is not binding domestically.
- Procedure: deportation could proceed if the ordinary procedure (owner: Foreigners Act, 1946) was followed.
- Open question: whether non-refoulement binds India as customary international law was raised and not adjudicated; scholars criticise the order.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 8 April 2021 (✅ Wikipedia: order dated 8 April 2021, listed as pending; ✅ owner 23). Reasoning (✅ Wikipedia). Foreigners Act reference (⚠️ owner only).
**Links:** ETH-6033, ETH-6035, ETH-6051.
**Trap:** Quoting Salimullah as a final holding that settles non-refoulement or customary law.
**Mains hook:** Present as the Court's interim reasoning on an open question. Qualified: neither "automatic protection" nor "automatic deportation".
**Back:** *"Stay refused, merits pending: Article 19(1)(e) argued, customary law left open."*

### Flashcard ETH-6035 — Holding Arunachal and Salimullah together
**Front:** How should a case-study or Mains answer use NHRC v. Arunachal Pradesh and Mohammad Salimullah together?
**Definition (learn):** Arunachal (final judgment) confirms Article 21 due process for every person; Salimullah (interim) reasons that the right against deportation is tied to Article 19(1)(e), available only to citizens, with the customary-law question left open.
**Breakdown:**
- Proposition 1 (final): non-citizens have Article 21 protection.
- Proposition 2 (interim): no right to remain or to bar deportation derived from Article 19(1)(e).
- Proposition 3 (open): customary non-refoulement not adjudicated.
- Answer: state both; flag Salimullah's status.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.2, and the 2026 Section B routing in Sec. 7).
**Links:** ETH-6033, ETH-6034, ETH-6056.
**Trap:** Citing only one case to reach an absolute position on refugee rights.
**Mains hook:** On "constitutional protections for non-citizens do not amount to a right against deportation", a qualified "partly, yes" answer uses both cases. Qualified: interim status.
**Back:** *"Life protected for all; residence reasoned for citizens; the interim door left ajar."*

### Flashcard ETH-6036 — Bhagalpur violence (1989) and its Commission of Inquiry
**Front:** What was the 1989 Bhagalpur violence, and what does the record show about its Commission of Inquiry and causes?
**Definition (learn):** Communal violence in Bhagalpur, Bihar, beginning 24 October 1989 and lasting about two months, in which the owner records over a thousand people killed. A Commission of Inquiry examined causes and the administrative and police response.
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: Bhagalpur city and roughly 250 nearby villages; a second wave in March 1990 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Trigger: a Ramshila procession reaching Tatarpur on 24 October, where crude bombs were thrown (✅ Wikipedia).
- Rumour: claims that about 200 Hindu students had been killed and 31 Hindu boys' bodies dumped in a well were later shown to be unfounded; investigators found 12 bodies, all from one family (✅ Wikipedia).
- Commission: three-member, judges C.P. Sinha and S. Samshul Hassan; final report 1995; blamed several police officers, including SP K.S. Dwivedi (✅ Wikipedia).
- Later inquiry: the Justice N.N. Singh commission, set up in 2005, reported in February 2015 (✅ Wikipedia; not in the owner).
- Owner-vs-source conflict (flag): the owner attributes rumour, procession and prior-tension findings to the Commission. Wikipedia reports the Commission's finding as police and administrative failure, and describes rumour and procession as causes separately. Check the report before attributing those findings to the Commission.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 October 1989 (✅ Wikipedia; owner gives October-November 1989). Over 1,000 killed (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia, which notes other estimates are higher).
**Links:** ETH-6037, ETH-6038.
**Trap:** Treating a commission of inquiry as a crisis-management tool. Its report came in 1995.
**Mains hook:** Post-facto accountability versus real-time prevention. Qualified: the record blames administrative and police lapse, which is what the owner's point rests on.
**Back:** *"Procession, rumour, police lapse: the inquiry reported in 1995, prevention had to be real-time."*

### Flashcard ETH-6037 — Nanavati Commission: 1984 riots, constituted 2000
**Front:** What did the Nanavati Commission examine, when was it constituted, and what did it find about the police?
**Definition (learn):** A Commission of Inquiry under Justice G.T. Nanavati, constituted in 2000 to re-examine the 1984 anti-Sikh riots: the sequence of events and lapses in administrative and police response.
**Breakdown:**
- Constitution: 8 May 2000, New Delhi (✅ Wikipedia).
- Report: completed February 2005 in two volumes, against a six-month mandate (✅ Wikipedia).
- Police: held Delhi police commissioner S.C. Tandon directly responsible; faulted Lieutenant Governor P.G. Gavai for failing in his duty and for issuing orders late (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner-vs-source conflict (flag): the owner says the findings implicated "some political actors" and criticised police inaction, and that they were "cautious" on individual culpability. Wikipedia records direct responsibility findings against named officials. Verify against the report before quoting.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Constituted 2000 (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia on 8 May 2000).
**Links:** ETH-6036, ETH-6038.
**Trap:** Conflating retrospective fact-finding with real-time crisis management.
**Mains hook:** A commission delivers accountability after the event. Qualified: its findings on culpability are contested in the owner's own account.
**Back:** *"Appointed 2000, reported 2005: fixed 1984 for the record, not the crisis."*

### Flashcard ETH-6038 — Choosing the right tool: commission, peace committee or proportionate action
**Front:** Which instrument fits which phase of communal or disturbed-area management?
**Definition (learn):** Commissions of inquiry are post-facto fact-finding and recommendation tools. Standing peace committees and proportionate administrative action operate before and during tension.
**Breakdown:**
- Post-facto: Bhagalpur (report 1995), Nanavati (report 2005).
- Preventive: district peace and harmony committees (ETH-6039).
- Real-time: proportionate, reasoned, published and time-bound restrictions (ETH-6040, ETH-6041).
- Rule: do not cite a commission as a substitute for timely administrative action.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.3). ⚠️ Analytical synthesis.
**Links:** ETH-6036, ETH-6037, ETH-6039, ETH-6041.
**Trap:** "A commission of inquiry was set up to prevent the violence." It is retrospective.
**Mains hook:** Recommend standing early-warning and preventive mechanisms. Qualified: those need capacity and political will.
**Back:** *"Commissions count the dead; committees and proportionate orders try to stop the next."*

### Flashcard ETH-6039 — District peace and harmony committees
**Front:** What are district peace and harmony committees, and what is the limit on citing them?
**Definition (learn):** Standing local committees, bringing together police, district administration and representative community leaders, used as a dialogue-based preventive mechanism to defuse tension before escalation.
**Breakdown:**
- Composition: police, district administration, representative community leaders.
- Purpose: prevention through dialogue, distinct from post-facto inquiry.
- Legal status (⚠️): a general administrative practice, not a single all-India statute.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner-only general practice, unverified for any specific state.
**Links:** ETH-6038.
**Trap:** Presenting these committees as a uniform nationwide legal requirement.
**Mains hook:** Verify the state-specific order before citing it. Qualified: effectiveness depends on the committee's real functioning.
**Back:** *"Talk before tension: standing committees, not a national statute."*

### Flashcard ETH-6040 — Anuradha Bhasin v. Union of India (2020): proportionality and procedural discipline
**Front:** What did Anuradha Bhasin v. Union of India (2020) lay down on restrictions on communication and movement?
**Definition (learn):** Arising from restrictions in Jammu and Kashmir, the Supreme Court held that restriction orders must satisfy proportionality, be published, not be indefinite, and be subject to periodic review; it also recognised constitutional protection for free speech and trade through the internet.
**Breakdown:**
- Citation: (2020) 3 SCC 637; decided 10 January 2020.
- Proportionality: legitimate aim, rational connection, least restrictive alternative, balance of interests.
- Publication: orders must be made available so affected persons can challenge them; an unpublished order is in practice unreviewable.
- Duration: not indefinite; subject to periodic review.
- Rights: freedom of speech and trade through the internet enjoy constitutional protection.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.3, Must-Know Facts). Wikipedia case page returned 404; the date and citation are not fetch-verified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-6041, ETH-6038.
**Trap:** "Bhasin held internet shutdowns unconstitutional." It laid down a standard and a procedure, not a prohibition.
**Mains hook:** Standard of review for restrictions in a disturbed district. Qualified: the judgment does not resolve any particular order's validity.
**Back:** *"Least harm, published, time-bound, reviewed: a discipline, not a ban."*

### Flashcard ETH-6041 — Framework: designing a restriction order that survives Bhasin
**Front:** What checklist should a restriction order satisfy to meet the Bhasin standard?
**Definition (learn):** Written, narrow, reasoned, published, time-bound and periodically reviewed, using the least restrictive alternative and balancing the rights affected.
**Breakdown:**
- Written and reasoned: the grounds are stated.
- Narrow: area, duration and service limited to the legitimate aim.
- Least restrictive: no broader measure where a narrower one suffices.
- Published: available so the order can be challenged.
- Time-bound and reviewed: not indefinite; periodic review.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.3; Probable questions). ⚠️ Checklist is an analytical distillation.
**Links:** ETH-6040, ETH-6038.
**Trap:** "A disturbed district justifies any order." Proportionality still applies.
**Mains hook:** Applies to a conflict district (2026 Section B, Q11). Qualified: the test is a standard of review.
**Back:** *"Write it narrow, reason it, publish it, time-limit it, review it."*

### Flashcard ETH-6042 — D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997): eleven custodial safeguards
**Front:** What did D.K. Basu v. State of West Bengal (1997) lay down on arrest and detention?
**Definition (learn):** The Supreme Court laid down eleven procedural safeguards on arrest and detention, flowing from Articles 21 and 22, in a case treated as a writ petition on custodial deaths.
**Breakdown:**
- Citation: (1997) 1 SCC 416; decided 18 December 1996 (owner), bench Kuldip Singh and Dr A.S. Anand JJ.
- Safeguards included (the list is not exhaustive here):
  - a witnessed, countersigned arrest memo;
  - information to a friend, relative or well-wisher about the arrest and place of custody;
  - medical examination at arrest and at roughly 48-hour intervals, with injuries recorded;
  - entries in a diary or custody register;
  - information to the jurisdictional magistrate.
- Statutory follow-through: Cr.P.C. amendments; the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 replaced the Cr.P.C. from 1 July 2024, renumbering the provisions. Verify section numbers before citing.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.4, Must-Know Facts). Wikipedia case page returned 404; citation, bench and safeguard list are owner-grounded and not fetch-verified (⚠️).
**Links:** ETH-6043, ETH-6040.
**Trap:** Citing the Cr.P.C. section number as current after 1 July 2024.
**Mains hook:** Enumerate the safeguards and explain their role in preventing custodial abuse. Qualified: safeguards bind regardless of the eventual excess finding (ETH-6043).
**Back:** *"Memo, friend, doctor, register, magistrate: safeguards against custodial abuse."*

### Flashcard ETH-6043 — Framework: a skipped custodial safeguard is a breach in itself
**Front:** In a custodial-excess case, how should a missed Basu safeguard be analysed?
**Definition (learn):** Procedural safeguards are owed regardless of the separate factual question of whether force was excessive. Skipping one is an independent breach.
**Breakdown:**
- Question 1 (procedural): were the safeguards followed?
- Question 2 (substantive): was force excessive?
- Answer structure: a missed safeguard is a breach even before excess is proved.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.4 use note; applies Sec. 14.12 of the Basic owner 22).
**Links:** ETH-6042.
**Trap:** "No excess proven, so no breach." Wrong on the procedural question.
**Mains hook:** Separate procedural and substantive questions. Qualified: the outcome of the substantive question remains open until evidence is weighed.
**Back:** *"A skipped safeguard is a breach, even before excess is proved."*

### Flashcard ETH-6044 — Samira Kohli v. Dr. Prabha Manchanda (2008): consent and therapeutic surgery
**Front:** What happened in Samira Kohli v. Dr. Prabha Manchanda (2008), and what consent principles did the Supreme Court state?
**Definition (learn):** An unmarried adult woman consented to a diagnostic and operative laparoscopy under general anaesthesia. During the procedure, while she was unconscious, the surgeon obtained her mother's written consent and performed an abdominal hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy.
**Breakdown:**
- Citation: (2008) 2 SCC 1; decided 16 January 2008.
- Consent must be real and valid: capacity, voluntariness, adequate information on nature, purpose, benefits, material risks and alternatives.
- Consent for a diagnostic procedure does not extend to therapeutic surgery.
- Exception: additional surgery is permitted only if necessary to save life or preserve health and unreasonable to delay for consent.
- Competent adult: no one else may consent on her behalf.
- Holding: an unauthorised invasion of the body and deficiency in service; the doctor's bona fides were taken into account in relief.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.5, Must-Know Facts). Date and citation are owner-stated; Wikipedia case page returned 404 (⚠️ not fetch-verified).
**Links:** ETH-6045, ETH-6046, ETH-6056.
**Trap:** "A benevolent motive makes the surgery lawful." The Court took good faith into account only in culpability and relief.
**Mains hook:** "Consent for a diagnostic procedure is not consent for a therapeutic one." Qualified: the necessity exception is narrow.
**Back:** *"Diagnostic yes is not therapeutic yes; no relative consents for a competent adult."*

### Flashcard ETH-6045 — Framework: the two-limb necessity exception in therapeutic consent
**Front:** What must both be true before additional surgery can proceed without fresh consent under Samira Kohli?
**Definition (learn):** The exception requires both limbs: the surgery must be necessary to save life or preserve health, and it must be unreasonable to delay the operation to obtain consent.
**Breakdown:**
- Limb 1: life or health is at stake.
- Limb 2: waiting to obtain consent would be unreasonable.
- Documentation: both limbs should be genuinely made out and contemporaneously recorded (⚠️ owner analysis).
- Scope: not a general licence for intra-operative decisions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Must-Know Facts; Sec. 4.5 limits).
**Links:** ETH-6044, ETH-6046.
**Trap:** Treating the "emergency" exception as one limb only.
**Mains hook:** A therapeutic-consent answer should state both limbs. Qualified: the exception is narrow.
**Back:** *"Both limbs or none: life or health at stake, and waiting unreasonable."*

### Flashcard ETH-6046 — Research consent vs therapeutic consent: different forums
**Front:** Which framework and forum govern research participation, and which govern therapeutic or surgical consent?
**Definition (learn):** Research consent is governed by the ICMR 2017 guidelines and the Institutional Ethics Committee. Therapeutic consent is governed by Samira Kohli, and its forum is the treating team, clinical ethics committee, State Medical Council, consumer forum or court.
**Breakdown:**
- Research: ICMR 2017 guidelines; IEC oversight (ETH-6032).
- Therapeutic: Samira Kohli (ETH-6044); the treating team, hospital clinical ethics committee, State Medical Council, consumer forum or civil or criminal court.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.5 Distinguish table).
**Links:** ETH-6032, ETH-6044.
**Trap:** Answering a surgical-consent dispute with an IEC. It imports the wrong authority.
**Mains hook:** Keeping the tracks separate signals a problem-level answer. Qualified: a dual-track case may need both.
**Back:** *"Research goes to the IEC; the operating table goes to the council and the court."*

### Flashcard ETH-6047 — Algorithmic fairness: no single Indian statute
**Front:** What is the Indian legal position on algorithmic fairness, predictive policing and public-sector facial recognition?
**Definition (learn):** At present there is no single Indian statute governing these comprehensively. The operative anchors are constitutional and general-statutory: Articles 14 and 15 (non-arbitrariness and non-discrimination), Article 21 with privacy and proportionality doctrine, and the data-protection statute's principles, subject to exemptions for state instrumentalities.
**Breakdown:**
- Constitutional anchors first.
- Data-protection statute: principles, with state exemptions.
- Foreign regimes and literature: comparators only.
- Substantive treatment: 13_Emerging-Ethics-Technology-AI-and-Environment (owner cross-reference).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Owner position (Sec. 4.6, Must-Know Facts), analytical.
**Links:** ETH-6048, ETH-6049, ETH-6050, ETH-6051.
**Trap:** Citing a foreign statute as the governing law for an Indian predictive-policing case.
**Mains hook:** Lead with Articles 14, 15 and 21. Qualified: the anchors are general, not algorithm-specific.
**Back:** *"No Indian algorithm statute: Articles 14, 15 and 21 carry the argument."*

### Flashcard ETH-6048 — EU AI Act-style risk tiers: a comparator, not Indian law
**Front:** What distinction do EU-style risk-tiered AI rules supply, and why is it only a comparator in India?
**Definition (learn):** Such frameworks treat individual criminal-risk prediction based solely on profiling or personality traits as impermissible, while evidence-linked, place- and time-based deployment remains permissible but high-risk, subject to human oversight, logging and transparency duties.
**Breakdown:**
- Impermissible: prediction of individual criminality from profiling or personality traits.
- High-risk but permissible: evidence-linked, place- and time-based deployment.
- Duties for high-risk: human oversight, logging, transparency.
- Use: to draw the line (owner's example: what the district SP may retain and what he must suspend, Sec. 14.17 of Basic owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Foreign law with no application in India; phased commencement; article numbers and dates not verified here.
**Links:** ETH-6047, ETH-6050, ETH-6051.
**Trap:** Quoting article numbers or obligations as settled law without checking the primary text.
**Mains hook:** Cite as an illustrative design after the Indian anchor. Qualified: the Indian argument does not depend on it.
**Back:** *"Profile the person: impermissible. Map the evidence: high-risk, not banned."*

### Flashcard ETH-6049 — The "dirty data" feedback loop in predictive policing
**Front:** What is the dirty-data or feedback-loop critique of predictive policing, and what remedy does the owner point to?
**Definition (learn):** Historic enforcement records (arrests, stops, complaints) are mistaken for a record of crime. A historically over-policed area therefore generates more recorded contacts, the model sends more patrols there, and the loop confirms itself. Proxy variables (postcode, immigration status, income) can stand in for protected characteristics without any discriminatory rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: enforcement record treated as crime record.
- Loop: more contacts → more patrols → more contacts.
- Proxy: a neutral variable correlates with a protected characteristic.
- Remedy: independent audit against victimisation-survey baselines, not arrest counts.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Research-derived analytical concept, not a legal finding (owner Sec. 4.6).
**Links:** ETH-6047, ETH-6048, ETH-6050.
**Trap:** Presenting the critique as proof that a particular Indian system is biased.
**Mains hook:** The critique explains the mechanism an allegation asserts; the audit is the response. Qualified: the critique does not dispense with the audit.
**Back:** *"Arrests are not crime: the loop confirms the patrols it created."*

### Flashcard ETH-6050 — Algorithmic fairness impossibility results
**Front:** Why can't several intuitive fairness criteria be satisfied together, and what does the owner conclude?
**Definition (learn):** Equal error rates across groups, equal predictive value and calibration cannot in general all be satisfied at once when base rates differ. The choice of criterion is therefore an ethical and political decision.
**Breakdown:**
- Criteria in tension: equal error rates, equal predictive value, calibration.
- Consequence: "make the algorithm fair" is not a single implementable instruction.
- Required practice: name the criterion, choose it, justify it and publish it, by an accountable human.
- Trap of over-reading: fairness is not impossible in general.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ Technical literature point, owner-stated; no named authors or results verified.
**Links:** ETH-6047, ETH-6049.
**Trap:** "Fairness is impossible, so bias is unavoidable." The owner's inference is about transparency.
**Mains hook:** Demand openly chosen, published fairness criteria. Qualified: the trade-off is a governance obligation.
**Back:** *"You cannot maximise every fairness metric when base rates differ: choose openly."*

### Flashcard ETH-6051 — Case-study hierarchy: Indian constitutional anchor first, comparator last
**Front:** In a case-study answer that touches foreign AI law or refugee rights, what order of argument must the answer follow?
**Definition (learn):** State the Indian constitutional anchor first (Articles 14, 15, 19(1)(e) where it applies, and 21, with NHRC v. Arunachal and Salimullah where refugees are involved), then the statutory layer, and use foreign comparators last, labelled as foreign.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: constitutional anchor.
- Step 2: statutory or judicial layer.
- Step 3: foreign or research comparator, one or two sentences, labelled.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 4.6 use note).
**Links:** ETH-6035, ETH-6047, ETH-6048.
**Trap:** Leading with a foreign statute and never reaching Articles 14 or 21. The owner calls this an inverted hierarchy.
**Mains hook:** Shows the examiner the right hierarchy of argument. Qualified: comparators can sharpen but not replace the Indian anchor.
**Back:** *"Constitution first, comparator last, labelled as foreign."*

### Flashcard ETH-6052 — Advanced skill: extract the mechanism behind each named case
**Front:** What is the advanced skill the owner asks for when using named cases?
**Definition (learn):** Extract the precise causal mechanism behind a case's success or limit, and do not treat cases as generic illustrations of "civil society is good" or "technology helps". The owner's example question: why did CARD fail when Bhoomi succeeded?
**Breakdown:**
- Question to ask: what did the reform change, and did it remove discretion?
- Two named failure modes: hero-story simplification and repeating an uncorroborated claim (Korea) as verified fact.
- Link to reform: the mechanism points to a specific reform.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 advanced Sec. 1-2 (Analytical claim; Hero-story simplification).
**Links:** ETH-6001, ETH-6016, ETH-6054.
**Trap:** Telling the case only as a success story, which the owner calls hero-story simplification.
**Mains hook:** Shows the difference between a case description and an analysis. Qualified: mechanism claims are the owner's analytical reading.
**Back:** *"Mechanism, not mascot: each case earns its lesson and admits its limit."*

### Flashcard ETH-6053 — CVC PIDPI Resolution (2004): the interim whistleblower mechanism
**Front:** What is the CVC PIDPI Resolution, and why does the owner rank it weaker than a statute?
**Definition (learn):** The Central Vigilance Commission's Public Interest Disclosure and Protection of Informants (PIDPI) mechanism, operating under Resolution No. 371/12/2002-AVD-III of 21 April 2004, which is administrative rather than statutory.
**Breakdown:**
- Nature: administrative resolution, operative in the interim.
- Protection: materially weaker than a statute would provide.
- Comparison: the 2014 Act would provide stronger legal protection, but is not in force (ETH-6005).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 21 April 2004; resolution number (✅ owner 23 advanced Sec. 3). ⚠️ Not fetched; government pages are often inaccessible.
**Links:** ETH-6005, ETH-6054.
**Trap:** Treating PIDPI as statutory protection.
**Mains hook:** Name the interim status. Qualified: operative does not mean statutory.
**Back:** *"2004 resolution holds the field; the 2014 Act holds the law, not yet in force."*

### Flashcard ETH-6054 — Framework: five steps for a named-case or comparative reform answer
**Front:** What five-step structure does the owner recommend for a named-case or comparative-model reform answer?
**Definition (learn):** Name the case precisely with source and date; state its causal mechanism; state its verified limitation; apply selective adaptation where a model is borrowed; close with a mechanism-targeted reform.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: name, with source and date.
- Step 2: precise causal mechanism of success or limit.
- Step 3: honestly acknowledged, verified limitation or gap.
- Step 4: adaptation for borrowed models (Vivekananda principle, ETH-6027).
- Step 5: specific, mechanism-targeted recommendation, not a generic one.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 advanced Sec. 11 (Mains-ready framework).
**Links:** ETH-6001, ETH-6027, ETH-6052.
**Trap:** Ending with a generic reform such as "strengthen institutions".
**Mains hook:** The 15-mark design question (advanced Sec. 12) uses all five steps. Qualified: each step must be source-grounded.
**Back:** *"Name, mechanism, limit, adapt, target."*

### Flashcard ETH-6055 — Snowden (2018 GS-IV Q12): the public-interest case study
**Front:** What was the 2018 GS-IV Question 12 demand on Edward Snowden, and how should it be answered?
**Definition (learn):** Snowden released classified government surveillance documents, citing a moral obligation to inform the public. The question asks whether that was ethically justified even though legally prohibited, weighing competing values.
**Breakdown:**
- Format: case study, 20 marks, 250 words (owner PYQ integration).
- Routing: the Ethics case-study method; prepare context, core dimensions, evidence or examples, counterpoint and concise conclusion.
- Competing values: public right to know versus state secrecy and legal duty.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2018, GS-IV, Q12 (✅ owner 23 PYQ integration). ⚠️ The 2013 disclosure date is from general knowledge, not the owner.
**Links:** ETH-6051, ETH-6054.
**Trap:** Treating legality as the answer. The question weighs competing values.
**Mains hook:** Qualified conclusion: disclosure can be ethically defensible while legally wrong, but the case does not settle how far such disclosure should go.
**Back:** *"Legal or right? Weigh the public duty against secrecy, then qualify."*

### Flashcard ETH-6056 — 2026 GS-IV Section B: named anchors and their routing
**Front:** Which named anchors serve 2026 GS-IV Section B Questions 7, 8 and 11?
**Definition (learn):** Routing from the owner's 2026 Section B note: Q7 (therapeutic consent) is anchored by Samira Kohli; Q11 (restrictions in a conflict district) by Anuradha Bhasin, read with the caution that commissions are post-facto; Q8 (predictive policing) by Indian constitutional anchors with comparators used sparingly.
**Breakdown:**
- Q7: Samira Kohli (ETH-6044, ETH-6045, ETH-6046).
- Q11: Bhasin (ETH-6040, ETH-6041) plus commission caution (ETH-6038).
- Q8: Articles 14, 15, 21 (ETH-6047) plus comparators (ETH-6048, ETH-6049, ETH-6050).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 (Sec. 7); routing file `_PYQ-GS4-SectionB-2026.md` (owner cross-reference).
**Links:** ETH-6044, ETH-6040, ETH-6047.
**Trap:** Citing Basu (custody) for the predictive-policing question.
**Mains hook:** Match the named anchor to the subpart's demand. Qualified: routing is the owner's mapping.
**Back:** *"Q7 consent, Q11 restrictions, Q8 constitution with comparators last."*

### Flashcard ETH-6057 — MKSS and Parivartan: why they are not universal templates
**Front:** Why should MKSS and Parivartan not be presented as a replicable template everywhere?
**Definition (learn):** Their success depended on unusually well-organised civil-society capacity, leadership and local context. Replicating them requires capacity-building investment that the cases do not show as cheap.
**Breakdown:**
- Dependency: organisational capacity.
- Dependency: leadership quality.
- Dependency: local context.
- Implication: budget for capacity, not only for the method.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 advanced Sec. 2 and 6 (capacity dependency); ⚠️ analytical.
**Links:** ETH-6009, ETH-6010, ETH-6011.
**Trap:** Treating MKSS as a universal template.
**Mains hook:** Qualified civil-society claim: method is replicable, capacity is not automatic.
**Back:** *"Jan Sunwai needs organisers; a template without people is a slogan."*

### Flashcard ETH-6058 — E-governance limit: procurement and collusion remain
**Front:** What does discretion-removal e-governance not address?
**Definition (learn):** E-governance removal of discretion is effective against certain corruption types, such as mutation delay and record-access denial, but does not address collusive corruption in procurement or large contracts, which needs separate, targeted systemic reform.
**Breakdown:**
- Works against: mutation delay, record-access denial.
- Does not reach: collusion in procurement and large contracts (see owner 18).
- Required: separate systemic reform for that category.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ✅ owner 23 advanced Sec. 6 (Limitations and trade-offs); cross-reference to owner 18 (procurement).
**Links:** ETH-6016, ETH-6017.
**Trap:** "E-governance solves corruption." It solves only some types.
**Mains hook:** Map each corruption type to the reform that addresses it. Qualified: no single lever covers all types.
**Back:** *"Automation cures the counter; collusion needs its own cure."*
